Galois actions on surfaces and a higher genus Grothendieck-Teichmüller group
Luciana Basualdo Bonatto, Marcy Robertson

Abstract
. We construct an operadic model for the higher-genus Teichmüller tower. More precisely, we define a modular operad 𝐒 \mathbf{S} in groupoids built from mapping class groups, with compositions and contractions encoding gluing operations on surfaces. We prove a presentation theorem for maps out of 𝐒 \mathbf{S} , showing that they are determined by a small number of genus-zero and genus-one generators and relations. Using this presentation and the work of Nakamura–Schneps, we construct a faithful action of the Nakamura–Schneps subgroup Γ ^ ⊆ 𝖦𝖳 ^ \widehat{\mathsf{\Gamma}}\subseteq\widehat{\mathsf{GT}} on the profinite completion 𝐒 ^ \widehat{\mathbf{S}} , and hence an action of Gal ⁡ ( ℚ ¯ / ℚ ) \operatorname{Gal}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q}) . The genus-zero truncation of 𝐒 \mathbf{S} recovers the cyclic operad of parenthesized ribbon braids, and its group of object-fixing profinite automorphisms recovers 𝖦𝖳 ^ \widehat{\mathsf{GT}} . Finally, the profinite completion of the classifying spaces of 𝐒 \mathbf{S} assemble into a modular ∞ \infty -operad in profinite spaces whose values identify with the étale homotopy types of moduli stacks of curves with marked tangent vectors, and the Γ ^ \widehat{\mathsf{\Gamma}} -action extends to this homotopy-coherent Teichmüller tower.



1.
Introduction

In the essay
Esquisse d’un Programme
, Grothendieck proposed that the absolute Galois group of
\mathbb{Q}
,
\mathbf{\mathrm{Gal}}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q})
, could be studied through its action on a “tower” of geometric objects, namely étale fundamental groups of the moduli spaces
\mathcal{M}_{g}^{n}
, together with the geometric morphisms relating these spaces
[
30
]
. These morphisms record that the standard action of
\mathbf{\mathrm{Gal}}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q})
is compatible with maps such as forgetting marked points, gluing curves along marked points or tangent directions, and passing to boundary strata. Already in genus zero this action is highly nontrivial: Belyi’s theorem implies that the action of
\mathbf{\mathrm{Gal}}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q})
on
\pi_{1}^{\acute{e}t}(\mathcal{M}_{0}^{4})
is faithful
[
7
]
. Grothendieck’s guiding idea was that there should be an “ideal Teichmüller tower” containing enough of these compatibility maps such that the automorphisms of the tower would recover precisely
\mathbf{\mathrm{Gal}}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q})
. The main purpose of this paper is to construct a topological model for a Teichmüller tower as a modular operad.

For
2g-2+n>0
, the étale fundamental group of
\mathcal{M}_{g}^{n}
identifies with the profinite completion of the mapping class group
\Gamma_{g}^{n}
of a genus
g
surface with
n
marked points fixed pointwise
[
51
,
14
]
. The action of
\mathbf{\mathrm{Gal}}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q})
then gives an outer action on the profinite completion of
\Gamma_{g}^{n}
. Passing from marked points to boundary components corresponds topologically to remembering tangent directions at the marked points, and this gives corresponding actions on the profinite completions of the mapping class groups
\Gamma_{g,n}
of genus
g
surfaces with
n
boundary components fixed pointwise (Definition
2.28
). Thus the tower of étale fundamental groups has a topological counterpart built from mapping class groups and compatibility maps coming from topological operations such as cutting along simple closed curves, gluing boundary components, and forgetting marked points.

This approach via mapping class groups provides the topological framework for the modular operad (Definition
2.22
) constructed in this paper. Namely, we define a family of groupoids
\mathbf{S}(g,n+1)
, for
2g+n\geq 1
, satisfying

\mathrm{B}\mathbf{S}(g,n+1)\simeq\mathrm{B}\Gamma_{g,n+1}.

The groupoids
\mathbf{S}(g,n+1)
assemble into a modular operad in groupoids, denoted
\mathbf{S}
(Definition
3.2
). Its compositions and contractions encode the maps induced by gluing boundary components. After profinite completion, we obtain a modular operad
\widehat{\mathbf{S}}
in profinite groupoids (see Section
4.1
). This construction supports the Galois action envisioned by Grothendieck:

Theorem

(Theorem
4.8
)
.

The absolute Galois group
\mathbf{\mathrm{Gal}}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q})
acts faithfully on the modular operad

\widehat{\mathbf{S}}
, via an explicit homomorphism

\mathbf{\mathrm{Gal}}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q})\longrightarrow\operatorname{Aut}(\widehat{\mathbf{S}}).

A substantial part of the paper is devoted to constructing this action and relating it to the classical genus-zero Grothendieck–Teichmüller theory.

The genus-zero part of the Teichmüller tower was first studied in depth by Drinfeld
[
19
, Section 4]
. He considered a tower of profinite braid groups whose structure maps include the standard operations of forgetting strands and replacing one strand by several parallel strands. Drinfeld introduced the profinite Grothendieck–Teichmüller group
\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}
via the symmetries of this tower (Definition
4.4
). Ihara showed that there is an embedding

\mathbf{\mathrm{Gal}}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q})\hookrightarrow\widehat{\mathsf{GT}},

and that the induced action on Drinfeld’s tower of profinite braid groups agrees with the Galois actions arising from the geometric étale fundamental groups
of the genus-zero moduli spaces
[
44
]
.

Operadic models for the genus-zero Teichmüller tower have been studied in, for instance,
[
25
,
5
,
66
,
42
,
13
,
59
]
. The version most directly related to the surfaces used in this paper is the cyclic operad of parenthesized ribbon braids
\mathsf{PaRB}^{\mathrm{cyc}}
, introduced in
[
16
]
. Its cyclic structure reflects the fact that, for genus-zero surfaces, all boundary components can be treated symmetrically rather than singling out one as an output.

Our modular operad
\mathbf{S}
extends this genus-zero surface model to higher genus. More precisely, its genus-zero truncation
\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{0}}\mathbf{S}
, defined in Appendix
A.1
, is naturally a cyclic operad and satisfies the following comparison.

Proposition

(Proposition
3.15
)
.

There is an isomorphism of cyclic operads

\operatorname{tr}_{\leq 0}\mathbf{S}\cong\mathsf{PaRB}^{\mathrm{cyc}}.

The key technical input for constructing actions on
\widehat{\mathbf{S}}
is a presentation theorem for maps out of
\mathbf{S}
. This is the higher-genus analogue of the presentation of maps out of genus-zero operadic models such as parenthesized ribbon braids. In the genus-zero case, an operadic map out of
\mathsf{PaRB}
is determined by an object together with morphisms representing a twist, a braiding, and an associator satisfying certain relations
[
25
,
13
]
. In the higher-genus setting we obtain the following analogue.

Theorem

(Theorem
3.17
)
.

Let
\mathcal{Q}
be a modular operad in groupoids. A map of modular operads
f:\mathbf{S}\longrightarrow\mathcal{Q}
is uniquely determined by a tuple

(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{t},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{a},\mathbf{s})

consisting of an object
\mathbf{m}\in\operatorname{ob}(\mathcal{Q}(0,3))

and morphisms
\mathbf{t}
(twist),
\mathbf{b}
(braiding),
\mathbf{a}
(associator), and
\mathbf{s}
(genus-one switch), satisfying the standard genus-zero relations and three additional genus-one relations.

The proof of this presentation theorem uses complexes of marked pants decompositions on surfaces. More precisely, we compare the groupoids
\mathbf{S}(g,n+1)
with the fundamental groupoids of CW complexes pants decompositions equipped with additional marking data (Definition
2.38
). These complexes refine the Hatcher–Thurston pants-decomposition complex
[
39
]
by adding marking data similar to that of
[
3
]
. Their connectedness and simple connectedness, proved in Appendix
C
, allow us to read off generators and relations for maps out of
\mathbf{S}
.

Using the presentation theorem, we construct the action on
\widehat{\mathbf{S}}
from the compatible actions of Nakamura–Schneps on profinite mapping class groups. In
[
54
]
, Nakamura and Schneps introduced a subgroup
\widehat{\mathsf{\Gamma}}
of
\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}
by imposing additional genus-one relations on elements of
\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}
. They proved that
\widehat{\mathsf{\Gamma}}
contains the image of
\mathbf{\mathrm{Gal}}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q})
, giving inclusions

\mathbf{\mathrm{Gal}}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q})\hookrightarrow\widehat{\mathsf{\Gamma}}\hookrightarrow\widehat{\mathsf{GT}},

and it remains open whether either inclusion is strict
[
54
, Theorem 1.2]
. They also constructed compatible actions of
\widehat{\mathsf{\Gamma}}
on profinite mapping class groups of surfaces equipped with additional structure, extending the genus-zero action of
\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}

[
54
, Theorem 1.4]
. Our construction packages these actions operadically: the presentation theorem reduces the verification to the defining genus-zero and genus-one relations, and hence produces a faithful action of
\widehat{\mathsf{\Gamma}}
on
\widehat{\mathbf{S}}
.

Restricting to genus zero recovers the usual Grothendieck–Teichmüller symmetry. More precisely, we obtain the following compatibility with the cyclic genus-zero model.

Proposition

(Proposition
4.6
)
.

There is an isomorphism of profinite groups

\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}\cong\operatorname{Aut}_{0}(\operatorname{tr}_{\leq 0}\widehat{\mathbf{S}})

where
\operatorname{Aut}_{0}(-)
denotes automorphisms fixing the objects of the underlying groupoids.

Thus the Grothendieck–Teichmüller action respects the operation of treating all boundary components of a genus-zero surface symmetrically, rather than distinguishing one boundary component as an output. This is compatible with Kontsevich’s discussion of the framed genus-zero Teichmüller tower and its higher-genus modular-operadic analogue
[
46
]
, and is closely related to the work of the second author and Singh on cyclic parenthesized ribbon braids and framed tangles
[
59
]
.

The presentation theorem also gives an operadic form of Grothendieck’s
two-level principle
: the full tower should be generated from its genus-zero and genus-one parts. In the present setting, the generators and defining relations occur already in the levels corresponding to

\Gamma_{0,4},\qquad\Gamma_{0,5},\qquad\Gamma_{1,1},\qquad\Gamma_{1,2}.

Equivalently, if
\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{1}}\mathbf{S}
denotes the genus-one truncation of
\mathbf{S}
, defined in Appendix
A.1
, then
\mathbf{S}
is freely recovered from this truncation.

Corollary

(Corollary
4.2
)
.

There is an isomorphism of modular operads

\mathbf{S}\cong(\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{1}})_{!}(\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{1}}\mathbf{S}),

where
(\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{1}})_{!}
denotes the left adjoint to the genus-one truncation functor.

In the final part of the paper, we pass from profinite groupoids to profinite spaces in order to compare our construction with the étale homotopy types of moduli spaces. This gives a homotopical refinement of the Teichmüller tower: it retains the étale fundamental groups while also remembering higher homotopy information. We use the modern
\infty
-categorical formulation of étale homotopy types as shapes of étale
\infty
-topoi, together with their profinite refinements. This perspective is compatible with the classical constructions of Artin–Mazur
[
2
]
and Friedlander
[
26
]
; see also
[
50
, Definition 7.1.6.1 and Example 7.1.6.9]
,
[
17
]
, and
[
37
]
.

Oda
[
55
, Theorem 1]
gives a concrete description of the profinite étale homotopy types of the moduli spaces
\mathcal{M}_{g}^{n}
. As recalled in Example
2.7
, for
2g-2+n>0
there is an equivalence in profinite spaces

\Pi^{\acute{e}t}_{\infty}(\mathcal{M}_{g}^{n}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\overline{\mathbb{Q}})\simeq\widehat{\mathrm{B}\Gamma_{g}^{n}}.

Thus, in this case, the étale homotopy type is identified with the profinite completion of the classifying space of a mapping class group.

For our topological model of the Teichmüller tower, we work with boundary mapping class groups
\Gamma_{g,n}
. On the algebro-geometric side, this corresponds to replacing marked points by marked points equipped with non-zero tangent vectors. We denote by
\mathcal{M}_{g,n}^{\tan}
the moduli stack of smooth curves with
n
ordered marked points equipped with non-zero tangent vectors. The corresponding form of Oda’s comparison identifies its profinite étale homotopy type with the classifying space of the completed boundary mapping class group:

\Pi^{\acute{e}t}_{\infty}(\mathcal{M}_{g,n}^{\tan}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\overline{\mathbb{Q}})\simeq\widehat{\mathrm{B}\Gamma_{g,n}}.

We recall this comparison in Lemma
6.16
. Using the equivalence
\mathrm{B}\mathbf{S}(g,n+1)\simeq\mathrm{B}\Gamma_{g,n+1}
, this identifies
\widehat{\mathrm{B}\mathbf{S}}(g,n+1)
with the corresponding étale homotopy type of
\mathcal{M}_{g,n+1}^{\tan}
.

It is not formal, however, that these profinite spaces assemble into a modular operad. The classifying space functor preserves products, so the collection of spaces
\mathrm{B}\mathbf{S}(g,n+1)
inherit a strict modular operad structure from
\mathbf{S}
. After profinite completion, this argument breaks down because profinite completion of spaces rarely preserves products
[
13
, Proposition 3.9]
. This is a problem because the composition laws in a modular operad are encoded by products over the vertices of graphs (Section
2.5
).

To address this, we use modular
\infty
-operads, a homotopy-coherent version of modular operads. More precisely, we use a genus-graded version of the Segal model developed by Hackney, the second author, and Yau in
[
34
]
. In this model, a modular
\infty
-operad is a contravariant functor on the modular dendroidal category of genus-graded graphs satisfying Segal equivalences over the vertices of each graph. The required compatibility with products is thus imposed up to homotopy, rather than as a strict product-preservation statement.

Applying this formalism to the profinite classifying spaces of
\mathbf{S}
, we obtain a modular
\infty
-operad in profinite spaces whose values on corollas identify with the étale homotopy types of the moduli stacks
\mathcal{M}_{g,n+1}^{\tan}
. Its values on more general graphs encode the corresponding gluing data through the modular Segal conditions. After applying classifying spaces and passing to derived automorphisms, the strict
\widehat{\mathsf{\Gamma}}
-action on
\widehat{\mathbf{S}}
induces an action in the homotopy category of modular
\infty
-operads in profinite spaces.

Theorem

(Theorem
6.20
)
.

The profinite classifying spaces
\widehat{\mathrm{B}(\mathbf{S}(g,n+1))}
, for
2g+n\geq 1
, assemble into a modular
\infty
-operad
\widehat{\mathrm{B}\mathrm{N}\mathbf{S}}
in profinite spaces. The action of
\widehat{\mathsf{\Gamma}}
on
\widehat{\mathbf{S}}
induces a faithful action of
\widehat{\mathsf{\Gamma}}

on
\widehat{\mathrm{B}\mathbf{S}}
in the homotopy category, via an explicit injective homomorphism

\widehat{\mathsf{\Gamma}}\longrightarrow\pi_{0}\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Aut}(\widehat{\mathrm{B}\mathbf{S}}).

Restricting to genus zero recovers the isomorphism:

\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}\cong\pi_{0}\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Aut}(\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{0}}\widehat{\mathrm{B}\mathrm{N}\mathbf{S}}).

1.1.

2.
Preliminaries

Throughout, we will write
\mathscr{E}=(\mathscr{E},\otimes,1_{\mathscr{E}})
to denote a cocomplete, cartesian, symmetric monoidal category such that the monoidal product
\otimes
commutes with small colimits in each variable. In this paper,
\mathscr{E}
will be (completed versions of) the category of sets, groupoids, or simplicial sets. All of our categories are
simplicial
categories, meaning that they are tensored and co-tensored over the category of simplicial sets. In particular, this means that one can replace the set of maps
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathscr{E}}(X,Y)
by a space
1
1

1

We follow a common abuse of terminology and often use the term
space
when referring to simplicial sets.
of maps
\operatorname{Map}_{\mathscr{E}}(X,Y)
, for any
X,Y
in
\mathscr{E}
. To reduce clutter, we will often suppress the category
\mathscr{E}
from our notation and simply write
\operatorname{Hom}(X,Y)
or
\operatorname{Map}(X,Y)
when no confusion can arise.

In order to discuss homotopy classes of maps between (modular and cyclic) operads, we will make use of the language of Quillen model categories and
\infty
-categories. For Quillen model categories, we take
[
41
]
or
[
4
]
as comprehensive references. We will use the formalism of relative categories from
[
6
]
or
[
4
, Chapter 13]
as our model for
\infty
-categories.

Definition 2.1
.

A
relative category
is a pair
(\mathscr{E},\mathscr{W})
where
\mathscr{E}
is a small category and
\mathscr{W}
is a wide subcategory of
\mathscr{E}
whose morphisms are a class of designated
weak equivalences
in
\mathscr{E}
. A functor of relative categories
F:(\mathscr{E},\mathscr{W})\rightarrow(\mathscr{E}^{\prime},\mathscr{W}^{\prime})
consists of a functor
F:\mathscr{E}\rightarrow\mathscr{E}^{\prime}
such that
F(\mathscr{W})\subset\mathscr{W}^{\prime}
.

Example 2.2
.

Any model category
\mathscr{E}
is a relative category in which
\mathscr{W}
is the class of weak equivalences in the model structure on
\mathscr{E}
.

The
homotopy category
of a relative category
\mathscr{E}
, denoted
\operatorname{Ho}(\mathscr{E})
, is obtained by formally inverting the morphisms in
\mathscr{W}
. To describe maps in the homotopy category
\operatorname{Ho}(\mathscr{E})
we use the Dwyer-Kan enhancement of the homotopy category. This is a simplicially enriched category,
\mathcal{L}(\mathscr{E},\mathscr{W})
, which has the same objects as
\mathscr{E}
and, for any two objects
X,Y\in\operatorname{ob}(\mathscr{E})
, a space of maps
\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}(X,Y)
.
2
2

2

Here, the
\mathbb{R}
is to indicate that we are taking the “right derived” version of the
\operatorname{Map}(-,-)
functor. This is also commonly denoted
\operatorname{Map}^{h}(X,Y)
in the literature, e.g.
[
41
]
.
By considering
\mathscr{E}
to be a discrete simplicial category, one can define a natural inclusion
\mathscr{E}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathscr{E},\mathscr{W})
, with the property that, for every
X,Y\in\operatorname{ob}(\mathscr{E})
,

\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{Ho}(\mathscr{E})}(X,Y)=\pi_{0}\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}_{\mathscr{E}}(X,Y).

The fact that the connected components of
\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}_{\mathscr{E}}(X,Y)
correspond to morphisms in
\mathscr{E}
reflects that the simplicial category
\mathcal{L}(\mathscr{E},\mathscr{W})
captures the higher homotopical data of morphisms in
\mathscr{E}
. In particular, for any weak equivalence
X\rightarrow X^{\prime}
and any
Y\in\operatorname{ob}(\mathscr{E})
in
\mathscr{E}
the induced maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}_{\mathscr{E}}(X^{\prime},Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}_{\mathscr{E}}(X,Y)}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}_{\mathscr{E}}(Y,X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}_{\mathscr{E}}(Y,X^{\prime})}

are weak equivalences of spaces.

In general, it is difficult to compute the derived mapping spaces
\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}_{\mathscr{E}}(X,Y)
. However, if
\mathscr{E}
admits the structure of a simplicial model category (
[
41
, Chapter 9]
), we have the following useful fact (
[
22
, Corollary 4.7]
):

Theorem 2.3
.

Let
\mathscr{E}
be a simplicial model category. If
X
is cofibrant and
Y
is fibrant in
\mathscr{E}
, then there exists a natural zigzag of weak equivalences

\operatorname{Map}_{\mathscr{E}}(X,Y)\simeq\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}_{\mathscr{E}}(X,Y).

Let
\mathscr{C}
and
\mathscr{D}
be two simplicial model categories. A simplicial Quillen adjunction between two simplicial model categories
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}}
F
G
gives rise to a homotopy adjunction

(2.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{W}_{\mathscr{C}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}(\mathscr{D},\mathscr{W}_{\mathscr{D}}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{L}\text{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}\text{G}}

This means that there exists a weak equivalence of spaces

\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}_{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbb{L}\text{F}(X),Y)\simeq\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}_{\mathscr{C}}(X,\mathbb{R}\text{G}(Y)),

for all
X\in\mathscr{C}
and
Y\in\mathscr{D}
. Here, the notion
\mathbb{L}
and
\mathbb{R}
denotes that we are using the left and right derived functors of
F
and
G
, respectively.

2.1.
Adjunctions between spaces and groupoids

Let
\mathscr{S}
denote the category of simplicial sets with its usual Kan-Quillen simplicial model structure (e.g.
[
4
, Chapter 6.1]
) and let
\mathscr{G}
denote the category of groupoids equipped with the model structure in which weak equivalences are equivalences of categories, cofibrations are morphisms that are injective on objects, and fibrations are isofibrations (e.g.
[
1
]
,
[
15
]
). Note that in this model structure, every object in
\mathscr{G}
is both fibrant and cofibrant.

The functor which sends a space
X
to its fundamental groupoid
\Pi_{1}(X)
, is the left adjoint in an adjunction

(2.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi_{1}:\mathscr{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{G}:\mathrm{B}.}

The right adjoint is called the
classifying space
functor. The category of groupoids becomes a simplicial category via this adjunction. In particular, one defines the space of maps between two groupoids as

\operatorname{Map}_{\mathscr{G}}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D}):=\operatorname{Map}_{\mathscr{S}}(\mathrm{B}\mathscr{C},\mathrm{B}\mathscr{D}).

The classifying space functor preserves and reflects weak equivalences and fibrations which makes (
2.2
) into a simplicial Quillen adjunction in which the right adjoint is
homotopically fully faithful
in the sense that the induced map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}_{\mathscr{G}}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}_{\mathscr{S}}(\mathrm{B}\mathscr{C},\mathrm{B}\mathscr{D})}

is a weak equivalence of spaces for all
\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D}\in\mathscr{G}
.

2.2.
Profinite completion of spaces and groupoids

For any small category
\mathscr{E}
, the category of pro-objects in
\mathscr{E}
, denoted
\operatorname{Pro}(\mathscr{E})
, can be thought of as the category obtained by freely adding all co-filtered limits to
\mathscr{E}
. More precisely, one can describe the
category of pro-objects
in
\mathscr{E}
,
\operatorname{Pro}(\mathscr{E})
, as the category whose objects are pairs
(I,X)
where
I
is a cofiltered category and
X=\{X_{i}\}_{i\in I}
is a diagram
X:I\rightarrow\mathscr{E}
. If
X:I\to\mathscr{E}
and
Y:J\to\mathscr{E}
are cofiltered diagrams, then morphisms in
\operatorname{Pro}(\mathscr{E})
are defined by

\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{Pro}(\mathscr{E})}(\{X\}_{i},\{Y\}_{j})=\lim_{j\in J}\,\colim_{i\in I}\,\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathscr{E}}(X_{i},Y_{j}).

Similarly, given a full subcategory
\mathscr{C}\subseteq\mathscr{E}
, we write
\operatorname{Pro}_{\mathscr{E}}(\mathscr{C})\subseteq\operatorname{Pro}(\mathscr{E})
for the full subcategory spanned by those pro-objects
(I,X)
such that
X_{i}\in\mathscr{C}
for all
i\in I
; equivalently,
\operatorname{Pro}_{\mathscr{E}}(\mathscr{C})\cong\operatorname{Pro}(\mathscr{C})
.

If the category
\mathscr{E}
is complete and
\mathscr{C}\subseteq\mathscr{E}
is a full subcategory closed under finite limits, we can define an adjunction

(2.3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{(-)}:\mathscr{E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Pro}_{\mathscr{E}}(\mathscr{C}):|-|.}

The left adjoint,
\widehat{(-)}
, is called the
pro-
\mathscr{C}
-completion
functor. The right adjoint,
|-|
, sends a pro-object
\{X_{i}\}_{i\in I}
to its limit in
\mathscr{E}
. If, moreover,
\mathscr{E}
is a cofibrantly generated simplicial model category and
\mathscr{C}\subseteq\mathscr{E}
satisfies a specific list of conditions (e.g., the conditions of a
fibration test category
in
[
12
, Definition 5.1]
) then
\operatorname{Pro}_{\mathscr{E}}(\mathscr{C})
admits a fibrantly generated model category structure and the adjunction (
2.3
) becomes a simplicial Quillen pair.

2.2.1.
Profinite groupoids

Let
\mathsf{Grp}
denote the category of discrete groups and let
\mathsf{Grp}_{\mathrm{fin}}\subseteq\mathsf{Grp}
be the full subcategory of finite groups. The pro-category

\widehat{\mathsf{Grp}}:=\operatorname{Pro}_{\mathsf{Grp}}(\mathsf{Grp}_{\mathrm{fin}})

is the category of
profinite groups
. There is an adjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{(-)}:\mathsf{Grp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathsf{Grp}}:|-|}

in which the right adjoint sends a pro-object
X=\{X_{j}\}_{j\in J}
to its limit in
\mathsf{Grp}
.
The left adjoint is the classical profinite completion functor which sends a group
\mathsf{G}
to the pro-object
\{\mathsf{G}/\mathsf{N}\}_{\mathsf{N}\triangleleft_{f}\mathsf{G}}
indexed by the cofiltered poset of finite-index normal subgroups
\mathsf{N}\triangleleft_{f}\mathsf{G}
. Equivalently, one may identify
\widehat{\mathsf{G}}
with the inverse limit

\widehat{\mathsf{G}}:=\lim_{\mathsf{N}\triangleleft_{f}\mathsf{G}}\,\mathsf{G}/\mathsf{N}

in
\mathsf{Grp}
.

Example 2.4
.

Let
X
be a connected, locally Noetherian scheme and let

\bar{x}\to X
be a geometric point. The
\acute{e}tale

fundamental group

\pi_{1}^{\acute{e}t}(X,\bar{x})
may be defined as the
automorphism group of the fibre functor from finite
\acute{e}tale
covers of

X
to finite sets. Equivalently, it is the inverse limit

\pi_{1}^{\acute{e}t}(X,\bar{x})\cong\lim_{(Y,y)\to(X,\bar{x})}\operatorname{Aut}_{X}(Y),

where the limit ranges over connected finite
\acute{e}tale
Galois covers

Y\to X
equipped with a lift
y:\bar{x}\to Y
of
\bar{x}
. In
particular,
\pi_{1}^{\acute{e}t}(X,\bar{x})
is a profinite group. See
[
60
,
0BQ8
]
or
[
31
, Exposé V]
for more details.

Example 2.5
.

Let
\mathcal{M}_{g}^{n}
denote the moduli stack of smooth genus
g
curves with

n
marked points, defined over
\mathbb{Q}
. For
2g-2+n>0
, the
geometric
\acute{e}tale
fundamental group of
\mathcal{M}_{g}^{n}
identifies,
after choosing a geometric base point, with the profinite completion of the
mapping class group of a genus
g
surface with
n
marked points fixed
pointwise:

\pi_{1}^{\acute{e}t}\bigl(\mathcal{M}_{g}^{n}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\overline{\mathbb{Q}}\bigr)\cong\widehat{\Gamma_{g}^{n}}.

See
[
51
,
14
]
for more details.

The profinite completion functor for groups admits a direct analogue for groupoids. We say that a
groupoid
\mathscr{E}
is
finite
if it has finitely many morphisms (hence finitely many objects),
and write
\mathscr{G}_{\mathrm{fin}}\subseteq\mathscr{G}
for the full subcategory of finite groupoids. Since

\mathscr{G}_{\mathrm{fin}}
is closed under finite limits, we define the category of
profinite groupoids
by

\widehat{\mathscr{G}}:=\operatorname{Pro}_{\mathscr{G}}(\mathscr{G}_{\mathrm{fin}}).

The category of profinite groupoids
\widehat{\mathscr{G}}
admits a cocombinatorial model structure, and
the profinite completion functor fits into a Quillen adjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{(-)}:\mathscr{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathscr{G}}:|-|}

(see
[
42
, Theorem 4.12 and Proposition 4.22]
), where
|-|
denotes the
limit functor
\widehat{\mathscr{G}}\to\mathscr{G}
.
The following proposition shows that, for groupoids with a finite set of objects, profinite completion
preserves binary products.

Proposition 2.6
.

[
42
, Proposition 4.23]

Let
\mathscr{E}
and
\mathscr{D}
be groupoids with a finite set of objects. Then the canonical map

\widehat{\mathscr{E}\times\mathscr{D}}\longrightarrow\widehat{\mathscr{E}}\times\widehat{\mathscr{D}}

is an isomorphism in
\widehat{\mathscr{G}}
.

2.2.2.
Profinite spaces

A simplicial set
X
is called
\pi
-finite
if
\pi_{0}(X)
is finite and, for every basepoint

x_{0}
, the groups
\pi_{n}(X,x_{0})
are finite for
n\geq 1
and trivial for
n\gg 0
.
Let
\mathscr{S}_{\pi}\subseteq\mathscr{S}
denote the full subcategory of
\pi
-finite spaces; it is closed under
finite limits. Using
[
12
, Theorem 5.2]
, we define the category of
profinite spaces
to be

\widehat{\mathscr{S}}:=\operatorname{Pro}_{\mathscr{S}}(\mathscr{S}_{\pi}).

Quick
[
56
, Theorem 2.12]
equips
\widehat{\mathscr{S}}
with a fibrantly generated model structure in
which cofibrations are monomorphisms and weak equivalences are detected by
\pi_{0}
,
\pi_{1}
, and
(twisted) cohomology with finite coefficients. The induced adjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{(-)}:\mathscr{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathscr{S}}:|-|}

is a Quillen adjunction
[
56
, Proposition 2.28]
, where
|-|
denotes the limit functor.

The classifying space functor sends finite groupoids to
\pi
-finite simplicial sets. It follows that we have a simplicial Quillen adjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\Pi}_{1}:\widehat{\mathscr{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathscr{G}}:\widehat{\mathrm{B}}.}

Moreover, these functors fit into a commutative square of simplicial Quillen adjunctions
(e.g.
[
12
, Example 7.14]
):

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathscr{G}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathscr{S}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{(-)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{(-)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces|-|}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathrm{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\Pi}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces|-|}

Example 2.7
.

Artin–Mazur
[
2
]
and Friedlander
[
26
]

construct the
\acute{e}tale
homotopy type of a scheme as a pro-simplicial
set. We write
\Pi^{\acute{e}t}(X)

for this pro-simplicial set, and write
\widehat{\Pi}^{\acute{e}t}(X)
for its profinite completion, viewed as a profinite space. The same notation
will be used for stacks, using
\acute{e}tale
hypercovers. In Section
6.4
we use the corresponding
\infty
-categorical formulation, where the
\acute{e}tale
homotopy type is viewed as a pro-object in the
\infty
-category of spaces.

Let
\mathcal{M}_{g}^{n}
denote the moduli stack of smooth genus
g
curves with
n
distinct ordered marked points, defined over
\mathbb{Q}
. Oda considers the
\acute{e}tale
homotopy type of the geometric stack

\mathcal{M}_{g}^{n}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
. If
\Gamma_{g}^{n}
denotes the mapping class group of a genus
g
surface with
n
marked points fixed pointwise, then for
2g-2+n>0
,
[
55
, Theorem 1]
shows that there is a weak equivalence of profinite spaces

\Pi^{\acute{e}t}\bigl(\mathcal{M}_{g}^{n}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\overline{\mathbb{Q}}\bigr)\simeq\widehat{\mathrm{B}\Gamma_{g}^{n}}.

Equivalently, the profinite
\acute{e}tale
homotopy type is the profinite completion of the classifying space of the mapping class group
\Gamma_{g}^{n}
.

2.3.
Graphs

We model the operations of operads, cyclic operads, modular operads and their
\infty
-versions with decorated graphs.

Notation 2.8
.

Let
\Sigma_{n}=\operatorname{Aut}(\{1,\ldots,n\})
and
\Sigma_{n}^{+}=\operatorname{Aut}(\{0,1,\ldots,n\})
; then
\Sigma_{n}
identifies naturally with the subgroup of
\Sigma_{n}^{+}
consisting of permutations that fix
0
.

Definition 2.9
.

A
graph

G
consists of a (possibly empty) finite set of
vertices

V_{G}
, a finite set of
half-edges

A_{G}
,
3
3

3

Half-edges are also called “arcs” in the literature, hence the use of the letter
A
for the set of half-edges.
together with gluing data encoded by a diagram of finite sets

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{G}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dagger}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}

Here
\dagger:A_{G}\to A_{G}
is a fixed-point-free involution, and
D_{G}\subseteq A_{G}
is the subset of half-edges adjacent to vertices, with
t:D_{G}\to V_{G}
recording the incident vertex.

An
edge
of
G
is an orbit of
\dagger
, written
e=[a,a^{\dagger}]
, and we write
E_{G}=\{[a,a^{\dagger}]\mid a\in A_{G}\}
for the set of edges. For
v\in V_{G}
, the
neighborhood
of
v
is the set of incident half-edges
nb(v):=t^{-1}(v)\subseteq D_{G}
; its elements are said to be
adjacent
to
v
. An edge
e=[a,a^{\dagger}]
is
internal
if
a,a^{\dagger}\in D_{G}
; we write
iE_{G}\subseteq E_{G}
for the set of internal edges. The remaining half-edges

\partial(G):=A_{G}\setminus D_{G}

are called
legs
, and
\partial(G)
is called the
boundary
of
G
.

Example 2.10
.

(1)

The graph consisting of a single
edge
and no vertices is denoted
\updownarrow
. Concretely, it has
V_{\updownarrow}=\varnothing
,
D_{\updownarrow}=\varnothing
, and
A_{\updownarrow}=\{a,a^{\dagger}\}
with
\dagger(a)=a^{\dagger}
.

(2)

The
(n+1)
st
corolla
, denoted
C_{n+1}
, is the graph with a single vertex
v
and no internal edges. It has
n+1
half-edges adjacent to
v
, and each of these is paired under
\dagger
with a boundary half-edge. These boundary half-edges are the
n+1
legs of the corolla.

Many additional examples of graphs can be found in
[
34
]
,
[
35
]
, and in the survey paper
[
36
]
.

A graph
H
is a
subgraph
of a graph
G
if there is a morphism of the defining diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{H}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{H}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{H}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dagger}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dagger}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}

such that the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{H}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{H}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}

is a pullback and the induced map
V_{H}\to V_{G}
is injective (cf.
[
34
, Definition 1.13]
).

Example 2.11
.

If
G
has at least one vertex and
v\in V_{G}
, there is a canonical subgraph
C_{v}\hookrightarrow G
, called the
corolla at
v
(cf.
[
34
, Definition 1.5]
). It has a single vertex
\{v\}
, and its adjacent half-edges are exactly the elements of
nb(v)
. Each such half-edge is paired with a boundary half-edge in
C_{v}
, so the legs of
C_{v}
are naturally in bijection with
nb(v)
.

For graphs with at least one vertex, the gluing data of
G
can be assembled into a coequalizer diagram (cf.
[
34
, Construction 1.18]
).

Lemma 2.12
.

Let
\mathrm{Fin}
denote the category of finite sets, and let
\mathscr{I}
be the category with three objects and three generating morphisms of the shape

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet.}

A graph
G
with at least one vertex, viewed as an object of
\mathrm{Fin}^{\mathscr{I}}
, is the coequalizer

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\coprod_{e\in iE_{G}}\updownarrow}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\coprod_{v\in V_{G}}C_{v}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G.}

The two arrows send each internal edge to the two corresponding boundary legs of the corollas at its incident vertices.

2.3.1.
Additional structure on graphs

Our combinatorial model admits a geometric realization
|G|
obtained by gluing a point for each vertex and a closed interval for each edge, identifying the endpoints of each interval with the incident vertices via the incidence map
t:D_{G}\to V_{G}
. We say that
G
is
connected
(respectively,
simply connected
) if
|G|
is connected (respectively, simply connected) (see also
[
34
, Definition 1.7]
).

Definition 2.13
.

A
tree
is a simply connected graph. A tree
T
is
rooted
if it is equipped with a distinguished leg
r\in\partial(T)
, called the
root
.

Remark 2.14
.

Any rooted tree admits a canonical orientation by directing every edge toward the root. Since
T
is simply connected, each vertex then has a unique
outgoing
incident edge (the one on the path to the root), and all remaining incident edges are
incoming
(cf.
[
36
, Definition 3.4]
).

In order to use graphs to model operations in modular and cyclic operads we will equip them with additional labeling data.

Definition 2.15
.

A graph
G
is said to be
labelled
if:

(1)

it comes with a total ordering of its vertices, i.e. a bijection

\lambda:\{1,\ldots,k\}\xrightarrow{\cong}V_{G}
;

(2)

for each vertex
v\in V_{G}
, it comes with a bijection

\ell_{v}:\{0,1,\ldots,|nb(v)|-1\}\xrightarrow{\cong}nb(v);

(3)

it comes with a bijection of the boundary (legs)

\ell_{G}:\{0,1,\ldots,n\}\xrightarrow{\cong}\partial(G),\qquad\text{where }n=|\partial(G)|-1.

We write
(G,\lambda,\ell)
for this data.

A
genus grading
on a graph
G
is a function
\epsilon:V_{G}\to\mathbb{N}
. The
genus

of a genus-graded graph
(G,\epsilon)
is

\beta_{1}(G)+\sum_{v\in V_{G}}\epsilon(v),

where
\beta_{1}(G)
is the first Betti number of the geometric realization of
G
.
If
G
is connected and
G\neq\updownarrow
, then

\beta_{1}(G)=|iE_{G}|-|V_{G}|+1.

We adopt the convention that the edge
\updownarrow
has genus zero.

Notation 2.16
.

Graphs in this paper are often equipped with additional structure, such as a vertex-ordering
\lambda
,
labelings
\ell
(Definition
2.15
), and/or a genus grading
\epsilon
.
When we wish to display this data explicitly we write

G=(G,\lambda,\ell,\epsilon),

omitting any entries that are not present. When no confusion can arise, we suppress some or all of
these decorations and simply write
G
for the underlying graph.

Definition 2.17
.

An
isomorphism of graphs

\varphi:G\to G^{\prime}
is a triple of bijections

\varphi_{A}:A_{G}\xrightarrow{\cong}A_{G^{\prime}},\qquad\varphi_{D}:D_{G}\xrightarrow{\cong}D_{G^{\prime}},\qquad\varphi_{V}:V_{G}\xrightarrow{\cong}V_{G^{\prime}}

such that the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{G^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{G^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{G^{\prime}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dagger_{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dagger_{G^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{G^{\prime}}}

If
G
and
G^{\prime}
are labelled graphs, a
strict isomorphism

\varphi:G\to G^{\prime}
is an isomorphism that preserves the vertex ordering and the labelings.

2.4.
Operads and cyclic operads

A symmetric
sequence
in
\mathscr{E}
is a collection of objects
\mathcal{P}=\{\mathcal{P}(n)\}_{n\geq 1}
in
\mathscr{E}
, together with a right action of the symmetric group
\Sigma_{n}
on each
\mathcal{P}(n)
. In particular, for each
n\geq 1
and each
\sigma\in\Sigma_{n}
, there is a morphism

\sigma^{*}:\mathcal{P}(n)\to\mathcal{P}(n).

Definition 2.18
.

An
operad
in
\mathscr{E}
consists of a symmetric sequence
\mathcal{P}=\{\mathcal{P}(n)\}_{n\geq 1}
together with:

•

a distinguished element
\operatorname{id}\in\mathcal{P}(1)
;

•

partial compositions

\hbox to172.32pt{\vbox to17.42pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 86.15738pt\lower-8.65973pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-5.22966pt}{3.35277pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{62}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{

satisfying the following axioms, for all
x\in\mathcal{P}(n)
,
y\in\mathcal{P}(m)
,
z\in\mathcal{P}(\ell)
:

(1)

For
1\leq i\leq n
and
1\leq j\leq m
,

(x\circ_{i}y)\circ_{i+j-1}z=x\circ_{i}(y\circ_{j}z).

(2)

For
1\leq i<k\leq n
,

(x\circ_{i}y)\circ_{k+m-1}z=(x\circ_{k}z)\circ_{i}y.

(3)

For
x\in\mathcal{P}(n)
and
1\leq i\leq n
,
\operatorname{id}\circ_{1}x=x,
and
x\circ_{i}\operatorname{id}=x.

(4)

For
\sigma\in\Sigma_{n}
and
\tau\in\Sigma_{m}
,

(\sigma^{*}x)\circ_{i}(\tau^{*}y)=(\sigma\circ_{i}\tau)^{*}(x\circ_{\sigma^{-1}(i)}y),

where
\sigma\circ_{i}\tau\in\Sigma_{n+m-1}
denotes the standard block permutation determined by
inserting
\tau
into the
i
th input of
\sigma
.

A map
f:\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{Q}
of operads is a
\Sigma_{n}
-equivariant map of symmetric sequences that preserves the unit and partial compositions. We write
\mathrm{Op}(\mathscr{E})
for the category of operads in
\mathscr{E}
.

Remark 2.19
.

In the literature, operads often have operations of arity
0
. In this paper, however, we work exclusively with
reduced
operads, and therefore restrict to arities
n\geq 1
.

A cyclic operad is an operad in which the symmetric group actions on inputs extend compatibly to actions on the output together with the inputs. We write

\Sigma_{n}^{+}:=\operatorname{Aut}(\{0,1,\ldots,n\})

and let
z_{n}^{+}\in\Sigma_{n}^{+}
denote the cyclic permutation
z_{n}^{+}(i)=i+1\pmod{n+1}
.

Definition 2.20
.

Let
\mathcal{P}=\{\mathcal{P}(n)\}_{n\geq 1}
be an operad in
\mathscr{E}
. A
cyclic structure
on
\mathcal{P}
consists, for each
n\geq 1
, of a right action of

\Sigma_{n}^{+}
on
\mathcal{P}(n)
, written

\sigma^{*}:\mathcal{P}(n)\to\mathcal{P}(n),

such that the following conditions hold:

(1)

the restriction along
\Sigma_{n}\subset\Sigma_{n}^{+}
agrees with the given
\Sigma_{n}
-action on
\mathcal{P}(n)
;

(2)

for
x\in\mathcal{P}(n)
and
y\in\mathcal{P}(m)
,

(z_{n+m-1}^{+})^{*}(x\circ_{i}y)=\begin{cases}(z_{n}^{+})^{*}(x)\circ_{i-1}y,&2\leq i\leq n,\\[2.0pt]
(z_{m}^{+})^{*}y\circ_{m}(z_{n}^{+})^{*}x,&i=1.\end{cases}

A
cyclic operad
is an operad equipped with a cyclic structure.

A map
f:\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{Q}
of cyclic operads is a map of operads commuting with the
\Sigma_{n}^{+}
-actions. We write
\mathrm{Cyc}(\mathscr{E})
for the category of cyclic operads in
\mathscr{E}
. As discussed in Appendix
A
, the categories
\mathrm{Op}(\mathscr{E})
and
\mathrm{Cyc}(\mathscr{E})
admit model category structures in which a map
f:\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{Q}
is a weak equivalence if each
f(n):\mathcal{P}(n)\to\mathcal{Q}(n)
is a weak equivalence in
\mathscr{E}
for all
n\geq 1
.

Remark 2.21
.

Cyclic operads are often presented using two-slot partial compositions

\circ_{i}^{j}:\mathcal{P}(n)\times\mathcal{P}(m)\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}(n+m-1),

where
i\in\{0,\ldots,n\}
and
j\in\{0,\ldots,m\}
, rather than using the one-slot compositions
\circ_{i}
of an ordinary operad together with an extended
symmetric group action. The two formalisms are equivalent.

Indeed, using the
\Sigma_{m}^{+}
-action on
\mathcal{P}(m)
, one may first relabel the second operation so that its
j
th slot becomes the distinguished slot
0
.
One then composes using the operation
\circ_{i}^{0}
, and finally reindexes the output using the induced
\Sigma_{n+m-1}^{+}
-action. For example, if

z_{m}^{+}\in\Sigma_{m}^{+}
denotes the cycle
z_{m}^{+}(r)=r+1\pmod{m+1}
, then, up to the chosen convention for the final labelling, one may write

x\circ_{i}^{j}y:=\rho^{*}\Bigl(x\circ_{i}^{0}\bigl(((z_{m}^{+})^{-j})^{*}y\bigr)\Bigr),

where
\rho\in\Sigma_{n+m-1}^{+}
is the permutation determined by the chosen labelling convention for the boundary of the output. For an explicit computation
of
\rho
, see
[
20
]
.

2.5.
Modular operads

For a fixed monoidal category
\mathscr{E}
, a
genus-graded sequence
in
\mathscr{E}
is a collection of objects

\mathcal{P}=\{\mathcal{P}(g,n+1)\}_{g\geq 0,\;n\geq 0}

in
\mathscr{E}
, such that each
\mathcal{P}(g,n+1)
carries a right action of the extended symmetric group
\Sigma_{n}^{+}:=\operatorname{Aut}(\{0,1,\ldots,n\})
.

Definition 2.22
.

A
modular operad
in
\mathscr{E}
consists of a genus-graded sequence
\mathcal{P}=\{\mathcal{P}(g,n+1)\}
together with:

(1)

a distinguished unit object
\operatorname{id}\in\mathcal{P}(0,2)
;

(2)

a family of partial compositions

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}(g,n+1)\times\mathcal{P}(h,m+1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}(g+h,n+m),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ_{i}^{j}}

for
i\in\{0,1,\ldots,n\}
and
j\in\{0,1,\ldots,m\}
;

(3)

a family of contractions

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}(g,n+1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}(g+1,n-1),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{i}^{j}}

for
0\leq i<j\leq n
and
n\geq 2
.

Moreover, we require the composition maps to satisfy associativity, equivariance, and unitality axioms as in
[
40
, Definition 3.3.1]
or
[
29
, Theorem 3.7]
. In addition, we require that the coherence relations governing contractions satisfy the following axioms:

(4)

For
\sigma\in\Sigma_{n}^{+}
and
a\in\mathcal{P}(g,n+1)
,

\bar{\sigma}^{*}(\xi_{i}^{j}a)=\xi_{\sigma(i)}^{\sigma(j)}(\sigma^{*}a),

where
\xi_{\sigma(i)}^{\sigma(j)}
means contraction along the unordered pair
\{\sigma(i),\sigma(j)\}
, and
\bar{\sigma}
is the induced permutation of the remaining labels after the two contracted labels have been removed.

(5)

If
\{i,j\}\cap\{k,\ell\}=\varnothing
, then the two contractions commute:

\xi_{i}^{j}\circ\xi_{k}^{\ell}=\xi_{k}^{\ell}\circ\xi_{i}^{j},

with the evident relabelling of indices after the first contraction.

(6)

For
a\in\mathcal{P}(g,n+1)
and
b\in\mathcal{P}(h,m+1)
, with
n\geq 2
,

\xi_{1}^{2}\bigl(a\circ_{n}^{0}b\bigr)=(\xi_{1}^{2}a)\circ_{n-2}^{0}b.

(7)

For
a\in\mathcal{P}(g,n+1)
and
b\in\mathcal{P}(h,m+1)
, with
m\geq 2
,

\xi_{n}^{n+1}\bigl(a\circ_{n}^{0}b\bigr)=a\circ_{n}^{0}(\xi_{1}^{2}b).

(8)

For
a\in\mathcal{P}(g,n+1)
and
b\in\mathcal{P}(h,m+1)
with
n\geq 1
,

\xi_{n-1}^{n}\bigl(a\circ_{n}^{0}b\bigr)=\xi_{n+m-2}^{\,n+m-1}\Bigl(a\circ_{n-1}^{0}(z_{m}^{+})^{*}b\Bigr),

where
z_{m}^{+}\in\Sigma_{m}^{+}
is the cycle
(0\,1\,\dots\,m)
.

A map
f:\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{Q}
of modular operads is a map of the underlying genus-graded sequences that is equivariant for the
\Sigma_{n}^{+}
-actions and commutes with the unit, partial compositions, and contractions. We write
\mathrm{Mod}(\mathscr{E})
for the category of modular operads in
\mathscr{E}
. As we discuss in Theorem
A.5
,
\mathrm{Mod}(\mathscr{E})
admits a model category structure in which a map
f:\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{Q}
is a weak equivalence if each component

f(g,n+1):\mathcal{P}(g,n+1)\longrightarrow\mathcal{Q}(g,n+1)

is a weak equivalence in
\mathscr{E}
for all
g\geq 0
and
n\geq 0
.

Remark 2.23
.

Our indexing conventions for modular operads are chosen to match the mapping class group conventions of Section
2.6
. Thus we write a genus-graded sequence as
\{\mathcal{P}(g,n+1)\}_{g\geq 0,\;n\geq 0}
, so that the boundary labels are
\{0,1,\ldots,n\}
and the symmetric group action is by
\Sigma_{n}^{+}=\operatorname{Aut}(\{0,1,\ldots,n\})
. This is a slight shift from the convention used in parts of the literature, where one often writes
\{\mathcal{P}(g,n)\}_{g,n\geq 0}
.

We also exclude operations with no boundary components. Equivalently, in the above structure maps we only include compositions and contractions whose targets still have nonempty boundary; see Appendix
A
.

Definition 2.24
.

Let
G=(G,\lambda,\ell,\epsilon)
be a connected labelled, genus-graded graph with nonempty boundary, and let
\mathcal{P}=\{\mathcal{P}(g,n+1)\}
be a genus-graded sequence in
\mathscr{E}
. A
\mathcal{P}
-decoration
of
G
is an ordered tuple

\underline{p}=(p_{\lambda(1)},\ldots,p_{\lambda(k)})
, where
V_{G}=\{v_{\lambda(1)},\ldots,v_{\lambda(k)}\}
and

p_{\lambda(i)}\in\mathcal{P}\bigl(\epsilon(v_{\lambda(i)}),\,|nb(v_{\lambda(i)})|\bigr)
for each
i
.
Equivalently,

\underline{p}\in\prod_{v_{\lambda(i)}\in V_{G}}\mathcal{P}\bigl(\epsilon(v_{\lambda(i)}),\,|nb(v_{\lambda(i)})|\bigr).

Let
G=(G,\lambda,\ell,\epsilon)
and
G^{\prime}=(G^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime},\ell^{\prime},\epsilon^{\prime})
be labelled graphs with nonempty boundary. For

i\in\{0,\ldots,|\partial(G)|-1\}\qquad\text{and}\qquad j\in\{0,\ldots,|\partial(G^{\prime})|-1\},

the
grafting

G\circ_{i}^{j}G^{\prime}
is the labelled graph obtained by gluing the boundary leg
\ell_{G}(i)\in\partial(G)
to the boundary leg
\ell_{G^{\prime}}(j)\in\partial(G^{\prime})
, with the remaining structure inherited in the evident way. Similarly, for
0\leq i<j\leq|\partial(G)|-1
, the
contraction

\xi_{i}^{j}G
is the labelled graph obtained by gluing together the boundary legs
\ell_{G}(i)
and
\ell_{G}(j)
.

Definition 2.25
.

Let
\mathcal{P}=\{\mathcal{P}(g,n+1)\}
be a genus-graded sequence in
\mathrm{Set}
. The
free modular operad

\mathcal{F}\mathcal{P}
is defined by letting
\mathcal{F}\mathcal{P}(g,n+1)
be the set of isomorphism classes of
\mathcal{P}
-decorated, connected, labelled, genus-graded graphs of total genus
g
with
n+1
boundary legs.

The group
\Sigma_{n}^{+}
acts on
\mathcal{F}\mathcal{P}(g,n+1)
by permuting the boundary labels. The partial compositions and contractions in
\mathcal{F}\mathcal{P}
are given by grafting and contracting decorated graphs.

To make this more explicit, for each
g\geq 0
and
n\geq 0
one may describe
\mathcal{F}\mathcal{P}(g,n+1)
as the quotient

\mathcal{F}\mathcal{P}(g,n+1)=\Biggl(\coprod_{[G]}\prod_{v\in V_{G}}\mathcal{P}\bigl(\epsilon(v),|nb(v)|\bigr)\Biggr)\Big/\sim,

where the coproduct ranges over isomorphism classes
[G]
of connected labelled genus-graded graphs
G=(G,\lambda,\ell,\epsilon)

with
|\partial(G)|=n+1
and total genus

g=\beta_{1}(G)+\sum_{v\in V_{G}}\epsilon(v).

The equivalence relation
\sim
identifies two decorated labelled genus-graded graphs
(G,\lambda,\ell,\epsilon,\underline{p})
and
(G^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime},\ell^{\prime},\epsilon^{\prime},\underline{p}^{\prime})
whenever there exists an isomorphism of genus-graded graphs
\iota:G\xrightarrow{\cong}G^{\prime}
such that, for each vertex
v_{\lambda(i)}\in V_{G}
, the induced bijection
\iota:nb(v_{\lambda(i)})\xrightarrow{\cong}nb(\iota(v_{\lambda(i)}))
determines a permutation

\sigma_{v_{\lambda(i)}}:=(\ell^{\prime}_{\iota(v_{\lambda(i)})})^{-1}\circ\iota\circ\ell_{v_{\lambda(i)}}\in\Sigma_{|nb(v_{\lambda(i)})|-1}^{+},

and the decorations satisfy

p^{\prime}_{\lambda^{\prime}(j)}=\bigl(\sigma_{v_{\lambda(i)}}\bigr)^{*}(p_{\lambda(i)})

whenever
\iota(v_{\lambda(i)})=v^{\prime}_{\lambda^{\prime}(j)}
. For further details and variants of this construction, see
[
29
, 2.20]
,
[
35
, 2.1]
, and
[
45
, 13.2.2]
.

2.5.1.
Truncations of modular operads and adjunctions

In Appendix
A.1
we define the
genus-
g
truncation
of a modular operad

\mathcal{P}=\{\mathcal{P}(h,n+1)\}_{h\geq 0,\;n\geq 0},

denoted
\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{g}}\mathcal{P}
, as the truncated modular operad obtained by retaining only the operations whose output has genus at most
g
. Thus compositions and contractions whose output has genus greater than
g
are no longer defined. More concretely, we say

x\circ_{i}^{j}y\qquad\text{and}\qquad\xi_{i}^{j}w

is undefined whenever

•

x\in\mathcal{P}(h_{1},m_{1}+1)
and
y\in\mathcal{P}(h_{2},m_{2}+1)
with

h_{1}+h_{2}>g
, or

•

w\in\mathcal{P}(h,m+1)
with
h\geq g
, so that

\xi_{i}^{j}w\in\mathcal{P}(h+1,m-1)
has genus greater than
g
.

We write
\mathrm{Mod}_{\leq g}(\mathscr{E})
for the category of genus-
g
truncated modular
operads in
\mathscr{E}
. The truncation construction defines a functor

\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{g}}^{*}:\mathrm{Mod}(\mathscr{E})\longrightarrow\mathrm{Mod}_{\leq g}(\mathscr{E}),

which admits both a left adjoint and a right adjoint:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Mod}(\mathscr{E})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Mod}_{\leq g}(\mathscr{E}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{g}}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{g}})_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{g}})_{*}}

Proposition 2.26
.

The category of cyclic operads is naturally equivalent to the category of genus-
0
truncated modular operads. Moreover, the truncation functor

\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{0}}^{*}:\mathrm{Mod}(\mathscr{E})\longrightarrow\mathrm{Cyc}(\mathscr{E})

preserves weak equivalences and is the right adjoint in a Quillen adjunction.

Similarly, there is a forgetful functor
u^{*}:\mathrm{Cyc}(\mathscr{E})\to\mathrm{Op}(\mathscr{E})
which forgets the cyclic structure. This functor
admits a left adjoint, called the
cyclic envelope
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Op}(\mathscr{E})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Cyc}(\mathscr{E}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{*}}

An explicit description of
u_{!}
is somewhat involved; see
[
20
, Section 3.1]
or
[
65
, Section 9]
. The forgetful functor
u^{*}
preserves weak equivalences, and the adjunction above is a Quillen adjunction.

2.6.
Mapping Class Groups

A surface of type
(g,n+1)
is a compact, oriented surface
S
of genus
g
with
n+1
boundary components.

Definition 2.27
.

A
labelled surface
of type
(g,n+1)
is a pair
(S,\delta)
, where

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta:\{0,\ldots,n\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}(\partial S)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

is a labelling of the boundary components, i.e. we write
\partial_{i}S:=\delta(i)
for the
i
th boundary component. A
parameterized labelled surface

(S,\delta,\{x_{i}\})
consists of a labelled surface together with a choice of point
x_{i}\in\partial_{i}S
for each
0\leq i\leq n
.

Definition 2.28
.

Let
S
be a surface of type
(g,n+1)
. The associated
pure mapping class group
is

\Gamma(S)\;:=\;\pi_{0}\,\operatorname{Diff}^{+}(S;\partial S),

where
\operatorname{Diff}^{+}(S;\partial S)
denotes the group of orientation-preserving diffeomorphisms of
S
that fix
\partial S
pointwise. Elements of the mapping class group
\Gamma(S)
are called
mapping classes
. The group structure on
\Gamma(S)
is induced by composition, and we use the usual order of composition of functions when writing the group multiplication.

Any two surfaces of type
(g,n+1)
have isomorphic pure mapping class groups and we denote this group by
\Gamma_{g,n+1}
.

Remark 2.29
.

More generally, a
mapping class
may refer to the isotopy class of a diffeomorphism between different manifolds that is not required to fix the boundary pointwise. Mapping classes that do fix the boundary pointwise are often called
pure mapping classes
. Since all but one of the mapping classes considered in this paper (see Figure
4(b)
) fix the boundary pointwise, we will use the term
mapping class
to mean
pure mapping class
.

2.6.1.
Presentations of mapping class groups

Mapping class groups of orientable surfaces are well understood, and several presentations are available
[
28
,
43
,
39
,
64
]
. We highlight two cases that will play an important role throughout the paper. For a comprehensive reference, see
[
24
]
.

Example 2.30
.

The mapping class group of the disc is trivial, i.e.
\Gamma(D^{2})=*
. This is known as Alexander’s lemma
[
24
, Lemma 2.1]
.

We now recall the mapping classes that are used as standard generators for higher genus surfaces.
Throughout this paper, we use the terms
curve
and
loop
to mean the isotopy class of an embedded closed curve in
S
. The geometric
intersection number

|a\cap b|
of two curves
a
and
b
is the minimum number of intersection points among all representatives in their isotopy classes. Similarly, the
self-intersection number
of a curve is the minimum self-intersection number among all representatives. A
simple loop
is a loop of self-intersection number
0
.

A fundamental family of mapping classes is given by
Dehn twists
about simple loops. We denote the Dehn twist about a simple loop
a
by
D_{a}
.
See
[
23
, Section 4.4]
for details.

Proposition 2.31
.

[
27
,
49
]

For a surface
S
of type
(g,n+1)
the mapping class group
\Gamma(S)
is generated by all Dehn twists along simple closed curves together with the following relations:

(C)

If
|a\cap b|=0
, then
D_{a}D_{b}=D_{b}D_{a}
.

(B)

If
|a\cap b|=1
and
c=D_{b}(a)
, then
D_{c}=D_{b}D_{a}D_{b}^{-1}
.

Relation (B) implies the
braid relation

D_{a}D_{b}D_{a}=D_{b}D_{a}D_{b}
, if
|a\cap b|=1
.

(D)

The
doughnut relation
,
(D_{a}D_{b}D_{a})^{4}=D_{\partial}
which takes place on a subsurface of
S
of type
(1,1)
,
a
and
b
are the curves represented in Figure
1(a)
, and
D_{\partial}
denotes the twist around the boundary.

(L)

The
lantern relation
,
D_{\partial_{0}}D_{\partial_{1}}D_{\partial_{2}}D_{\partial_{3}}=D_{b_{1}}D_{b_{2}}D_{b_{3}}
, where the
\partial_{i}
are loops in
S
bounding a subsurface of type
(0,4)
and the
b_{j}
are as in Figure
1(b)
.

(a)

(b)

Figure 1
.

We will also make repeated use of the following:

Lemma 2.32
.

Let
S,S^{\prime}
be parametrized labelled surfaces and let
f:S\to S^{\prime}
be a diffeomorphism taking the marked point on the
i
th boundary component of
S
to the corresponding marked point of
S^{\prime}
. Let
a
be a simple loop in
S
. Then

D_{f(a)}=f\cdot D_{a}\cdot f^{-1}

as elements of
\Gamma(S^{\prime})
. Moreover, the mapping class
f\cdot D_{a}\cdot f^{-1}
depends only on the isotopy class of
f
relative to the boundary.

Proof.

The proof is standard; see
[
24
, Fact 3.7]
.
∎

2.6.2.
A diagrammatic representation of mapping classes

The goal of this subsection is to give a concrete combinatorial model for mapping classes in genus
0
. More precisely, we describe how isotopy classes of boundary-fixing diffeomorphisms can be encoded by embedded trees. This provides the diagrammatic framework needed later to give a presentation of our modular operad.

Let
(S,\delta,\{x_{i}\}_{i=0}^{n})
and
(S^{\prime},\delta^{\prime},\{x^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i=0}^{n})
be parametrised labelled surfaces of type
(0,n+1)
, each equipped with a chosen point
x_{i}\in\partial_{i}S
and
x^{\prime}_{i}\in\partial_{i}S^{\prime}
on the
i
th boundary component. We write
\operatorname{Diff}^{+}\bigl(S,S^{\prime};\partial\bigr)
for the space of
orientation-preserving diffeomorphisms
\phi:S\to S^{\prime}
that preserve the boundary labels and satisfy
\phi(x_{i})=x^{\prime}_{i}
for all
0\leq i\leq n
. Its set of path components

\pi_{0}\ \operatorname{Diff}^{+}\bigl(S,S^{\prime};\partial\bigr)

is the set of isotopy classes of such diffeomorphisms. In genus
0
we will encode these isotopy classes by combinatorial data coming from embedded trees.

Definition 2.33
.

Let
T
be a tree with
|\partial(T)|=n+1
equipped with a boundary labelling

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell_{T}:\{0,1,\ldots,n\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial(T)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

Let
(S,\delta,\{x_{i}\})
be a labelled surface of type
(0,n+1)
with chosen points
x_{i}\in\partial_{i}S
for
0\leq i\leq n
. A
marking
of
(S,\delta,\{x_{i}\})
by
T
is an equivalence class of embeddings
m:T\hookrightarrow S
such that

m\bigl(\ell_{T}(i)\bigr)=x_{i}\in\partial_{i}S,\qquad\text{ for all }i\in\{0,\ldots,n\},

where
m\sim m^{\prime}
if
m^{\prime}
is obtained from
m
by an isotopy relative to
\partial S
or by precomposition with an element of
\operatorname{Homeo}(T)

A
marked genus-zero surface
is a pair
\bigl((S,\delta,\{x_{i}\}),[m]\bigr)
consisting of a labelled genus-zero surface
(S,\delta,\{x_{i}\})
and a marking
m:T\hookrightarrow S
by a boundary-labelled tree
T
.

Any marked surface determines additional combinatorial structure on the underlying tree. Since
T
is embedded in the oriented surface
S
, the incident half-edges at each vertex inherit a cyclic ordering from the orientation of
S
. A graph equipped with a cyclic ordering of the incident half-edges at each vertex is called a
ribbon graph
, and the corresponding data is called a
ribbon structure
.

In addition, if
U_{T}
is a sufficiently small tubular neighbourhood of
T
in
S
, then
\partial U_{T}
is an oriented circle, and the legs of
T
meet
\partial U_{T}
in a cyclically ordered set. Via the boundary labelling
\ell_{T}
, this induces a cyclic order on the set of boundary labels
\{0,\ldots,n\}
.

Figure 2
.
Diagrammatic representation of a diffeomorphism
S\rightarrow S^{\prime}
.

Let
S=(S,\delta,\{x_{i}\}_{i=0}^{n})
and
S^{\prime}=(S^{\prime},\delta^{\prime},\{x^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i=0}^{n})
be parametrized labelled surfaces of type
(0,n+1)
, and let
[m]
and
[m^{\prime}]
be markings on
S
and
S^{\prime}
, respectively, that induce the same cyclic order on the boundary labels. Choose representatives

m:T\hookrightarrow S\qquad\text{and}\qquad m^{\prime}:T^{\prime}\hookrightarrow S^{\prime}

of the markings
[m]
and
[m^{\prime}]
. Using these representatives, we construct an orientation-preserving diffeomorphism
S\to S^{\prime}
up to isotopy relative to the boundary as follows.

Choose small tubular neighbourhoods
U_{T}\subset S
and
U_{T^{\prime}}\subset S^{\prime}
of the embedded trees
m(T)
and
m^{\prime}(T^{\prime})
. After smoothing corners, both
U_{T}
and
U_{T^{\prime}}
, as well as their complements

S\setminus\operatorname{int}(U_{T})\qquad\text{and}\qquad S^{\prime}\setminus\operatorname{int}(U_{T^{\prime}}),

are discs. The markings and boundary data determine an orientation-preserving homeomorphism

\partial U_{T}\longrightarrow\partial U_{T^{\prime}}

well-defined up to isotopy relative to the marked boundary points. Such a boundary map extends over each of the two discs uniquely up to isotopy relative to the boundary, since the mapping class group of the disc is trivial (Alexander’s lemma). We therefore obtain diffeomorphisms

\phi_{T,T^{\prime}}^{1}:U_{T}\to U_{T^{\prime}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\phi_{T,T^{\prime}}^{2}:S\setminus\operatorname{int}(U_{T})\to S^{\prime}\setminus\operatorname{int}(U_{T^{\prime}}).

Gluing these along
\partial U_{T}
produces an orientation-preserving diffeomorphism

\phi:S\to S^{\prime},

well-defined up to isotopy relative to
\partial S
. The following lemma shows that the isotopy class of
\phi:S\rightarrow S^{\prime}
depends only on the isotopy classes of the markings on
S
and
S^{\prime}
, respectively.

Lemma 2.34
.

Let
S=(S,\delta,\{x_{i}\}_{i=0}^{n})
and
S^{\prime}=(S^{\prime},\delta^{\prime},\{x^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i=0}^{n})
be two parameterized labelled surfaces of type
(0,n+1)
, and let
[m]
and
[m^{\prime}]

be markings on
S
and
S^{\prime}
, respectively, inducing the same cyclic order on the boundary labels
\{0,\ldots,n\}
.

Let

m:T\hookrightarrow S\qquad\text{and}\qquad\tilde{m}:\widetilde{T}\hookrightarrow S

be two representatives of the marking
[m]
, and let

m^{\prime}:T^{\prime}\hookrightarrow S^{\prime}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\tilde{m}^{\prime}:\widetilde{T}^{\prime}\hookrightarrow S^{\prime}

be two representatives of the marking
[m^{\prime}]
.
Then the diffeomorphisms obtained from the above construction using
(m,m^{\prime})
and
(\tilde{m},\tilde{m}^{\prime})
determine the same isotopy class of the diffeomorphism
\phi:S\to S^{\prime}.
∎

2.7.
The Complex of Markings

In the study of presentations of mapping class groups, it is standard to consider a
2
-dimensional CW complex whose vertices encode decompositions of a surface and whose edges correspond to elementary moves between them. To give a presentation of our modular operad of mapping class groups, we use a variant of the complex of maximal markings of
[
3
, Section 5]
.

A
cut system
on a surface
S
is a finite collection of pairwise disjoint simple loops
C=\{c_{1},\ldots,c_{k}\}
, up to isotopy, such that the complement is a disjoint union of genus-zero punctured surfaces. Each puncture can be canonically replaced by a boundary component, yielding a disjoint union of genus-zero surfaces with boundary. By abuse of notation, we denote the resulting disjoint union of surfaces by

\overline{S\setminus C}=\overline{S\setminus\bigcup_{i}c_{i}}\;=\;\coprod_{j}S_{j}.

Each surface
S_{j}
is canonically associated to a subsurface of
S
, and we refer to the surfaces
S_{j}
as the
cut-subsurfaces
determined by
C
. See, for example,
[
39
,
38
,
52
]
.

We will enhance a cut system by equipping the cut-subsurfaces with compatible markings. These markings assemble into an embedding of a graph
G
recording how the genus-zero pieces are connected: the vertices of
G
correspond to cut-subsurfaces, while the internal edges correspond to the cut curves.

Definition 2.35
.

[
3
, Definition 3.3]

Let
S=(S,\delta,\{x_{i}\}_{i=0}^{n})
be a labelled parameterized surface of type
(g,n+1)
with
n\geq 0
. A
marked cut system
on
S
consists of a pair
(C,[m])
, where:

•

C=\{c_{1},\ldots,c_{k}\}
is a cut system on
S
;

•

[m]
is an equivalence class of embeddings
m:G\hookrightarrow S
, where
m\sim m^{\prime}
if
m^{\prime}
is obtained from
m
by an isotopy relative to
\partial S
or by precomposition with an element of
\operatorname{Homeo}(G)
.

These data are required to satisfy the following conditions:

(1)

The embedding
m
is compatible with the decomposition determined by

C
in the following sense:

•

the internal edges
iE_{G}
of
G
are in bijection with the curves in
C
, and the image of each internal edge meets the corresponding curve transversely in a single point and is disjoint from all other curves in
C
;

•

the image of each vertex lies in a distinct cut-subsurface of
S
, and this determines a bijection of the vertices
V_{G}
with the connected components of
\overline{S\setminus C}
;

•

for each vertex
v\in V_{G}
, the half-edges incident to
v
are in bijection with the boundary components of the corresponding cut-subsurface
S_{v}
.

(2)

The embedding
m
sends each leg of
G
to a marked boundary point of
S
. Equivalently, for every leg
e\in\partial(G)
there exists
i\in\{0,\ldots,n\}
such that the endpoint of
e
is mapped to
x_{i}\in\delta(i)\subset\partial S
.

(3)

For each vertex
v\in V_{G}
, the restriction of
m
to the corolla subgraph
C_{v}\subset G
induces a marking

m_{v}:C_{v}\hookrightarrow S_{v}

in the sense of Definition
2.33
, where
S_{v}
is the cut-subsurface corresponding to
v
under (1).

A marked cut system
(C,[m])
is called a
marked pants decomposition
if every cut-subsurface
S_{j}
is a surface of type
(0,3)
.

Example 2.36
.

For the pair of pants
P
, markings are represented by embeddings of the corolla
m:C_{2}\hookrightarrow P
. Figure
3
depicts one such marking, which we call the
standard marking

m_{P}
on
P
.

The surfaces
S_{\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/fork1.pdf}}}
and
S_{\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/fork2.pdf}}}
are obtained by gluing two copies of
P
in the two ways shown in Figure
3
. The same figure shows the corresponding marked pants decompositions, which we call the
standard markings
on
S_{\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/fork1.pdf}}}
and
S_{\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/fork2.pdf}}}
.

The surface
S_{\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/contraction.pdf}}}
is obtained from a single copy of
P
by gluing the boundary components
\partial_{1}
and
\partial_{2}
. Its standard marking, also shown in Figure
3
, is induced by gluing the corresponding half-edges of the embedded corolla in
P
.

Figure 3
.
Standard markings on
P
,
S_{\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/fork1.pdf}}}
,
S_{\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/fork2.pdf}}}
, and

S_{\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/contraction.pdf}}}
.

The complex of markings is a CW complex whose vertices are marked cut systems. Its edges are generated by the following mapping classes, written using the diagrammatic representation introduced in
Section
2.6.2
.

Definition 2.37
.

Let
P
,
S_{\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/fork1.pdf}}}
,
S_{\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/fork2.pdf}}}
, and

be the surfaces described in Example
2.36
.

The standard

-move is the mapping class

\tau:P\longrightarrow P

represented in Figure

. This is precisely the Dehn twist about the boundary component labelled
0
.

•

The standard
B
-move on the pair of pants, is the mapping class

\beta:P\longrightarrow P

represented in Figure

. Note that this mapping class is actually an isotopy class of a diffeomorphism that does
not
fix the boundary of
P
. In fact, it exchanges boundaries labelled
1
and
2
.

and

•

The standard
A
-move is the mapping class

represented in Figure

.

The standard

\sigma:S_{\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/contraction.pdf}}}\longrightarrow S_{\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/contraction.pdf}}}

given by

\sigma=D_{a}D_{b}D_{a}\in\Gamma(S_{\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/contraction.pdf}}}),

where

and

are the curves represented in Figure

. It is simple to check that
\sigma
sends the marked cut system
(\{a\},[m_{\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/contraction.pdf}}}])
to
(\{b\},[\sigma\circ m_{\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/contraction.pdf}}}])
, as in Figure

.

.
