We now define the CW complex of markings used throughout the paper. It is a variant of the
complex of maximal markings
of
[
3
, Section 5]
. The main difference is that we restrict to markings over pants decompositions, so the cut-subsurfaces are all pairs of pants. We refer to Appendix
C
for the full list of
2
-cell relations, and record below those most relevant for this paper.

Definition 2.38
.

Let
S=(S,\delta,\{x_{i}\}_{i=0}^{n})
be a parametrized, labelled surface of type
(g,n+1)
, with
n\geq 0
. The
complex of markings over pants decompositions on
S
, denoted
\mathcal{M}(S)
, is the
2
-dimensional CW complex defined as follows.

Vertices.

The vertices of
\mathcal{M}(S)
are marked pants decompositions
(C,[m])
on
S
.

1
-cells.

The
1
-cells are generated by the following moves.

(1)

T
-moves:

Given marked pants decompositions
(C,[m])
and
(C^{\prime},[m^{\prime}])
, we define a
1
-cell

(C,[m])\overset{T_{a}}{\rightsquigarrow}(C^{\prime},[m^{\prime}])

whenever
C=C^{\prime}
and the markings
m
and
m^{\prime}
differ only on a cut-subsurface
S^{\prime}\subset\overline{S\setminus C}
having
a
as one of its boundary curves, in the following way: under an identification
\phi:S^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\cong}P
satisfying
\phi\circ m|_{S^{\prime}}=m_{P}
and
\phi(a)=\partial_{0}
,
we have

\phi\circ m^{\prime}|_{S^{\prime}}=\tau\circ m_{P}.

Thus a
T
-move keeps the underlying pants decomposition fixed and changes the
marking by a Dehn twist along one cut curve.

(2)

B
-moves:

Given marked pants decompositions
(C,[m])
and
(C^{\prime},[m^{\prime}])
, we define a
1
-cell

(C,[m])\overset{B_{a,b,c}}{\rightsquigarrow}(C^{\prime},[m^{\prime}])

whenever
C=C^{\prime}
and the markings
m
and
m^{\prime}
differ only on a cut-subsurface
S^{\prime}\subset\overline{S\setminus C}
with boundary curves
a,b,c
, in the following way: under an identification

\phi:S^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\cong}P
satisfying

\phi\circ m|_{S^{\prime}}=m_{P},\qquad\phi(a)=\partial_{1},\qquad\phi(b)=\partial_{2},\qquad\text{and}\qquad\phi(c)=\partial_{0},

we have

\phi\circ m^{\prime}|_{S^{\prime}}=\beta\circ m_{P}.

When the curves
a
and
b
uniquely determine the pair of pants
S^{\prime}
, we omit
c
from the notation and write
B_{a,b}
.

(3)

A
-moves:

Given marked pants decompositions
(C,[m])
and
(C^{\prime},[m^{\prime}])
, we define a
1
-cell

(C,[m])\overset{A_{a,b}}{\rightsquigarrow}(C^{\prime},[m^{\prime}])

whenever
C\setminus\{a\}=C^{\prime}\setminus\{b\}
and the markings
m
and
m^{\prime}
differ only on a cut-subsurface

S^{\prime}\subset\overline{S\setminus(C\setminus\{a\})}=\overline{S\setminus(C^{\prime}\setminus\{b\})}

of type
(0,4)
, in the following way: under an identification
\phi:S^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\cong}S_{\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/fork1.pdf}}}
taking the local marked cut system
(\{a\},m|_{S^{\prime}})
to the standard marking on
S_{\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/fork1.pdf}}}
, the composite

\alpha\circ\phi:S^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\cong}S_{\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/fork2.pdf}}}

takes the local marked cut system
(\{b\},m^{\prime}|_{S^{\prime}})
to the standard marking on
S_{\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/fork2.pdf}}}
.

(4)

S
-moves:

Given marked pants decompositions
(C,[m])
and
(C^{\prime},[m^{\prime}])
, we define a
1
-cell

(C,[m])\overset{S_{c^{\prime},c}}{\rightsquigarrow}(C^{\prime},[m^{\prime}])

whenever
C\setminus\{c\}=C^{\prime}\setminus\{c^{\prime}\}
and the markings
m
and
m^{\prime}
differ only on a cut-subsurface

S^{\prime}\subset\overline{S\setminus(C\setminus\{c\})}=\overline{S\setminus(C^{\prime}\setminus\{c^{\prime}\})}

of type
(1,1)
, in the following way: under an identification
S^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\cong}S_{\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/contraction.pdf}}}
taking the local marked cut system
(\{c\},m|_{S^{\prime}})
to the standard marking on
S_{\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/contraction.pdf}}}
, we have

c^{\prime}=\sigma(c)\qquad\text{and}\qquad m^{\prime}|_{S^{\prime}}=\sigma\circ m|_{S^{\prime}}.

2
-cells.

The
2
-cells are attached along loops expressing relations among the
1
-cells.
Most of these coincide with the relations in
[
3
]
; see also
Appendix
C
.

In describing these relations, we use the following conventions. For

f\in\Gamma(S)
and a marking
m:G\hookrightarrow S
, we write
f\circ m
for
the marking represented by
G\xrightarrow{m}S\xrightarrow{f}S
. For a subsurface

S^{\prime}\subset S
, we write
m|_{S^{\prime}}
for the induced marking obtained by restricting
the image of
m
to the corresponding subgraph. In particular, if
S^{\prime}
is a
cut-subsurface, then
m|_{S^{\prime}}
is the
corolla marking
m_{v}:C_{v}\hookrightarrow S^{\prime}
associated to the corresponding
vertex
v\in V_{G}
.

We include the following families of relations, which are described in detail in Appendix
C
:

(1)

commutativity of disjoint moves;

(2)

boundary twist relations;

(3)

pentagon relation;

(4)

two hexagon relations;

(5)

self-duality of associativity,

(6)

twist–braiding relation;

(7)

gluing relations for Dehn twists.

In addition to the local relations above, we impose the following genus-one relations. For surfaces of type
(1,1)
, we impose

(G1.1)

\displaystyle SSB

\displaystyle=\operatorname{id},

(G1.2)

\displaystyle T^{-1}ST^{-1}ST^{-1}

\displaystyle=S

shown in more detail in Figures
7(a)

and
7(b)
.

(a)

2
-cell (G1.1)

(b)

2
-cell (G1.2)

Figure 7
.
The genus-one
2
-cells (G1.1) and (G1.2).

For surfaces of type
(1,2)
, we also impose the relation shown in
Figure
8
. This relation differs slightly
from the corresponding relation in
[
3
]
.

Figure 8
.
2
-cell (G2)

The relations above are chosen so that the resulting complex has the expected connectivity properties. The result we will use later is the following.

Proposition 2.39
.

Let
S=(S,\delta,\{x_{i}\}_{i=0}^{n})
be a parameterized, labelled surface of type
(g,n+1)
, with
n\geq 0
. Then the complex of markings over pants decompositions on
S
,
\mathcal{M}(S),
is connected and simply connected.

A proof is given in Appendix
C
.

3.
A Modular Operad of Mapping Class Groupoids

In this section, we define a modular operad in groupoids
\mathbf{S}=\{\mathbf{S}(g,n+1)\}_{g\geq 0,\,n\geq 0},
which homotopically approximates a modular operad constructed from the mapping class groups in the sense that,

\mathrm{B}\mathbf{S}(g,n+1)\simeq\mathrm{B}\Gamma_{g,n+1}.

In Section
3.1.1
we will compare each groupoid
\mathbf{S}(g,n+1)
with the corresponding complex of markings. This comparison will allow us to give a finite presentation of
\mathbf{S}
by generators and relations.

3.1.
The Modular Operad
\mathbf{S}

Recall that we write
\Sigma_{n}^{+}=\operatorname{Aut}({0,1,\ldots,n})
for the extended symmetric group. If
C_{n+1}
is a corolla with (n+1) boundary legs, then a labelling of its boundary by the set
{0,1,\ldots,n}
is equivalent to a choice of element of
\Sigma_{n}^{+}
. Indeed, once a reference labelling is fixed, every other labelling is obtained by precomposition with a unique permutation in
\Sigma_{n}^{+}
.

In the case
n=2
, the alternating subgroup of
\Sigma_{2}^{+}=\operatorname{Aut}(\{0,1,2\})
is given by
A_{2}^{+}=\{\operatorname{id},(012),(021)\}
. The quotient
\Sigma_{2}^{+}/A_{2}^{+}
has two elements, represented by

\operatorname{id}\cdot A_{2}^{+}\qquad\text{and}\qquad(12)\cdot A_{2}^{+}.

The permutations in
A_{2}^{+}
preserve the cyclic ordering of the labels, so the three labellings in each coset determine the same cyclic ordering of the boundary legs.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
\Upsilon=\{\Upsilon(g,n+1)\}
be the free modular operad in
\mathrm{Set}
generated by the set

\Upsilon(0,3)=\Sigma_{2}^{+}/A_{2}^{+}.

The set
\Upsilon(0,3)
consists of the two planar trivalent corollas with boundary labelled by
\{0,1,2\}
, which we denote by

P=\operatorname{id}\cdot A_{2}^{+}\qquad\text{and}\qquad(12)^{*}P=(12)\cdot A_{2}^{+}.

Figure
9
depicts elements of the set
\Upsilon(0,3)
.

Figure 9
.
Generators of the modular operad
\Upsilon
.

Following Definition
2.25
, we see that an element of
\Upsilon(g,n+1)
is an isomorphism class of connected decorated graphs
G=(G,\lambda,\ell_{G},\epsilon,\underline{\upsilon})
where:

(1)

every vertex
v\in V_{G}
is trivalent and has genus
\epsilon(v)=0
;

(2)

The vertex decoration
\underline{\upsilon}=(\upsilon_{\lambda(1)},\ldots,\upsilon_{\lambda(k)})
consists of a sequence of elements of
\Upsilon(0,3)
. In other words, each vertex
v_{\lambda(i)}
is decorated by one of the two planar trivalent corollas in
\Upsilon(0,3)
, equivalently by a bijection

\ell_{v_{\lambda(i)}}:\{0,1,2\}\xrightarrow{\cong}nb(v_{\lambda(i)})

defined up to the action of
A_{2}^{+}
;

(3)

the boundary of
G
is equipped with a labelling

\ell_{G}:\{0,\ldots,n\}\xrightarrow{\cong}\partial(G);

(4)

the total genus of
G
is
g
, equivalently
\beta_{1}(G)=g
.

Condition (2) is equivalent to equipping each vertex of
G
with a cyclic ordering of its incident half-edges. In other words,
G
is a ribbon graph, and the isomorphism classes above are isomorphism classes of ribbon graphs. Accordingly, we will often regard elements of
\Upsilon(g,n+1)
as ribbon graphs with first Betti number
g
and
n+1
ordered legs. However, the interpretation of
\Upsilon(g,n+1)
as the free modular operad generated by a symmetric sequence will be essential in later sections.

Our next goal is to define, for each
g\geq 0
and
n\geq 1
, a groupoid
\mathbf{S}(g,n+1)
whose objects are the decorated graphs in
\Upsilon(g,n+1)
. The construction is arranged so that, for every
G\in\Upsilon(g,n+1)
, there is a natural isomorphism

\operatorname{Aut}_{\mathbf{S}(g,n+1)}(G)\cong\Gamma(S_{G}),

where
S_{G}
is the surface associated to
G
.

We now describe this surface. Let
G=(G,\lambda,\ell_{G},\epsilon,\underline{\upsilon})\in\Upsilon(g,n+1)

be a decorated graph. From
G
we construct a labelled, parametrized surface

S_{G}=(S_{G},\delta,\{x_{i}\}_{i=0}^{n})

of type
(g,n+1)
, together with a marking over a pants decomposition in the
sense of Definition
2.35
, as follows.

(1)

For each vertex
v_{\lambda(i)}\in V_{G}
, let
P_{v_{\lambda(i)}}=(P_{v_{\lambda(i)}},\delta_{v_{\lambda(i)}},\{x_{0},x_{1},x_{2}\})
denote a copy of the standard pair of pants corresponding to the decoration
\upsilon_{\lambda(i)}\in\Upsilon(0,3).
Thus the boundary components of
P_{v_{\lambda(i)}}
are labelled by
\{0,1,2\}
according to the labelling of the legs of the corolla decorated by
\upsilon_{\lambda(i)}
, i.e.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta:=\ell_{P_{v_{\lambda(i)}}}:\{0,1,2\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial(P_{v_{\lambda(i)}}).}

Moreover,
P_{v_{\lambda(i)}}
comes equipped with the standard marking (Figure
3
)

m_{v_{\lambda(i)}}:C_{v_{\lambda(i)}}\hookrightarrow P_{v_{\lambda(i)}},

where the image of the
j
-th leg determines the marked point
x_{j}
on the
j
th boundary component of
P_{v_{\lambda(i)}}
.

(2)

If two vertices of
G
are joined by an internal edge, we glue the corresponding boundary components of the associated pairs of pants. In this way one obtains a surface of type
(g,n+1)
, which we denote by
S_{G}
.

More precisely, if the graph
G
is presented as the coequalizer

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\coprod\limits_{e\in iE_{G}}\updownarrow}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\coprod\limits_{v_{\lambda(i)}\in V_{G}}C_{v_{\lambda(i)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G,}

as in Lemma
2.12
, then the associated surface
S_{G}
is the coequalizer

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\coprod\limits_{e\in iE_{G}}S^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\coprod\limits_{v_{\lambda(i)}\in V_{G}}P_{v_{\lambda(i)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{G}.}

We note that to ensure that
S_{G}
is a smooth surface one should perform the gluing along the collar neighbourhoods of the boundary components. We omit these technical details for brevity.

(3)

The boundary components of
S_{G}
are labelled via the boundary labelling of
G
, that is,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta:=\ell_{G}:\{0,1,\ldots,n\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial(S_{G}).}

For each
i\in\{0,\ldots,n\}
, let
x_{i}\in\delta(i)
denote the point where the marking
m_{G}
meets the boundary component
\delta(i)
.

(4)

By construction, the image of
\coprod_{e\in iE_{G}}S^{1}
in
S_{G}
defines a pants decomposition. The local markings
m_{v_{\lambda(i)}}:C_{v_{\lambda(i)}}\hookrightarrow P_{v_{\lambda(i)}}
glue together to give a natural embedding
m_{G}:G\hookrightarrow S_{G}.
Together this defines a marked pants decomposition
(iE_{G},[m_{G}])
of the surface
S_{G}
.

Definition 3.2
.

We define a family of groupoids
\mathbf{S}(g,n+1)
, for
2g+n\geq 1
, as follows.

For
(g,n+1)=(0,2)
, let
\mathbf{S}(0,2)
be the groupoid with a single object
|
and
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{S}(0,2)}(|,|)=\mathbb{Z}.
We regard this as the groupoid version of the mapping class group of a thin cylinder, whose morphisms correspond to Dehn twists along the boundary; e.g.
[
63
, 3.1.1]
.

For
g\geq 0
and
n\geq 1
, the objects of
\mathbf{S}(g,n+1)
are the decorated graphs in
\Upsilon(g,n+1)
:

\operatorname{ob}(\mathbf{S}(g,n+1))=\Upsilon(g,n+1).

Given any two graphs
G,G^{\prime}\in\Upsilon(g,n+1)
, the morphisms are isotopy classes of diffeomorphisms

\phi:S_{G}\to S_{G^{\prime}}

preserving the boundary labelling and sending the marked points on the boundary of
S_{G}
to the corresponding marked points on the boundary of
S_{G^{\prime}}
. Thus

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{S}(g,n+1)}(G,G^{\prime}):=\pi_{0}\operatorname{Diff}(S_{G},S_{G^{\prime}};\partial).

Composition in
\mathbf{S}(g,n+1)
is defined to be the composition of diffeomorphisms.

For
(g,n)=(0,2)
, the symmetric group
\Sigma_{1}^{+}
acts trivially on
\mathbf{S}(0,2)
. For all other pairs
(g,n)
, the extended symmetric group
\Sigma_{n}^{+}
acts on
\mathbf{S}(g,n+1)
by permuting the labels on the boundary legs of the underlying decorated graphs. Thus
\mathbf{S}=\{\mathbf{S}(g,n+1)\}_{g\geq 0,\;n\geq 0}
is a genus-graded sequence in groupoids.

We now define the modular operad structure. For
0\leq i\leq n
and

0\leq j\leq m
, the composition functor

\circ_{i}^{j}:\mathbf{S}(g,n+1)\times\mathbf{S}(h,m+1)\longrightarrow\mathbf{S}(g+h,n+m)

is defined on objects by grafting decorated graphs, so that

(G,H)
is sent to
G\circ_{i}^{j}H
, as in
Definition
2.25
.

For
(g,n)=(0,2)
, there is a single object
|
in
\mathbf{S}(0,2)
which acts as the identity for graph grafting. The generating morphism
1\in\mathbb{Z}=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{S}(0,2)}(|,|)
acts by Dehn twists: composing it at the
i
th input with a morphism
[\phi]:S_{G}\to S_{H}
produces the mapping class
[D_{\partial_{i}}\circ\phi]:S_{G}\to S_{H},

where
\partial_{i}
denotes the
i
th boundary component of
S_{H}
.

For all other pairs
(g,n)
, the associated surface
S_{G\circ_{i}^{j}H}
is obtained by gluing
S_{G}
and
S_{H}
along the boundary components labelled
\ell_{G}(i)
and
\ell_{H}(j)
. The same gluing operation defines the functor on morphisms. Namely, if
\phi:S_{G}\to S_{G^{\prime}}
and
\psi:S_{H}\to S_{H^{\prime}}
are boundary-label-preserving diffeomorphisms, then they carry the chosen boundary components to the corresponding boundary components of
S_{G^{\prime}}
and
S_{H^{\prime}}
. Hence they glue to a diffeomorphism
\phi\circ_{i}^{j}\psi:S_{G\circ_{i}^{j}H}\longrightarrow S_{G^{\prime}\circ_{i}^{j}H^{\prime}}.

This construction is compatible with isotopy classes, and therefore defines the desired functor.

Similarly, for distinct labels
i
and
j
, the contraction functor

\xi_{i}^{j}:\mathbf{S}(g,n+1)\longrightarrow\mathbf{S}(g+1,n-1)

is defined on objects by grafting together the two boundary legs of
G
labelled

\ell_{G}(i)
and
\ell_{G}(j)
. On surfaces, this is the operation of gluing the
two corresponding boundary components of
S_{G}
, thereby increasing the genus by
one and reducing the number of boundary components by two.

On morphisms, a boundary-label-preserving diffeomorphism

\phi:S_{G}\to S_{G^{\prime}}
glues along the corresponding boundary components to give
a diffeomorphism
S_{\xi_{i}^{j}G}\to S_{\xi_{i}^{j}G^{\prime}}
. This is again compatible with
isotopy classes, and hence defines the contraction functor.

Definition 3.3
.

The
modular operad of surfaces
,

\mathbf{S}=\{\mathbf{S}(g,n+1)\}_{2g+n\geq 1},

is the modular operad in groupoids whose
(g,n+1)
-component is the groupoid
\mathbf{S}(g,n+1)
of genus
g
surfaces with
n+1
marked boundary components. The modular operad structure is induced by gluing boundary components, or equivalently by the grafting and contraction operations on graphs.

The
cyclic operad of genus-zero surfaces
, denoted
\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{0}}\mathbf{S}
, is
obtained by restricting
\mathbf{S}
to genus zero. Its component with
n+1
boundary
components is

\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{0}}\mathbf{S}(n+1)=\mathbf{S}(0,n+1).

The cyclic operad structure is induced by the gluing operations in
\mathbf{S}
which glue distinct genus-zero surfaces along boundary components. On markings, these operations correspond to grafting the underlying labelled trees.

By construction, for every object
G\in\operatorname{ob}(\mathbf{S}(g,n+1))
, the automorphism group of

G
is the mapping class group of the associated surface:

\operatorname{Aut}_{\mathbf{S}(g,n+1)}(G)=\Gamma(S_{G})\cong\Gamma_{g,n+1}.

Since
\mathbf{S}(g,n+1)
is a connected groupoid, the following lemma follows
immediately.

Lemma 3.4
.

For each
g\geq 0
and
n\geq 0
satisfying
2g+n\geq 1
, there is a weak equivalence

\mathrm{B}\mathbf{S}(g,n+1)\simeq\mathrm{B}\Gamma_{g,n+1}.

Remark 3.5
.

The modular operad in Definition
3.3
is closely related to the surface operads of
[
61
, Construction 2.2]
and
[
63
, Section 3.1]
. These constructions all build surfaces by gluing basic pieces along boundary components, but they differ in which pieces are taken as “atomic”. In the constructions of Tillmann and Wahl, the atomic surfaces include the pair of pants, the one-holed torus, and the disc. In our setting, the pair of pants is the only atomic surface.

3.1.1.
A presentation of the modular operad
\mathbf{S}

Fix an object
G\in\operatorname{ob}(\mathbf{S}(g,n+1))
, regarded as a decorated graph, and let
S_{G}
denote the associated surface equipped with its standard marked pants
decomposition

(iE_{G},[m_{G}]:G\hookrightarrow S_{G}).

We compare the topological encoding of markings of
S_{G}
, given by the complex of markings, with the categorical encoding given by the coslice category
G\downarrow\mathbf{S}(g,n+1)
.

Recall the complex of markings
\mathcal{M}(S_{G})
from Definition
2.38
. We write
\mathbb{M}_{G}
for the groupoid presented by this
2
-complex. Its objects are the
0
-cells of
\mathcal{M}(S_{G})
, its generating morphisms are the edges of

\mathcal{M}(S_{G})
, and its relations are the boundaries of the
2
-cells. Equivalently,
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{M}_{G}}(v,w)
consists of homotopy classes, relative endpoints, of edge paths from
v
to
w
.

Definition 3.6
.

Fix a graph
G\in\operatorname{ob}(\mathbf{S}(g,n+1))
. The
coslice category

G\downarrow\mathbf{S}(g,n+1)
is the category whose objects are morphisms
\phi:G\to H
in
\mathbf{S}(g,n+1)
, as
H
ranges over the objects of
\mathbf{S}(g,n+1)
.

A morphism from
\phi_{1}:G\to H_{1}
to
\phi_{2}:G\to H_{2}
is a morphism
\psi:H_{1}\to H_{2}
in
\mathbf{S}(g,n+1)
such that
\psi\phi_{1}=\phi_{2}
. In other words, a morphism from
\phi_{1}\rightarrow\phi_{2}
is a morphism
\psi:H_{1}\to H_{2}
which makes the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}

commute.

Lemma 3.7
.

Fix a graph
G\in\operatorname{ob}(\mathbf{S}(g,n+1))
and let
S_{G}
be the associated labelled, parametrized surface. Then the coslice category
G\downarrow\mathbf{S}(g,n+1)
is isomorphic to the groupoid
\mathbb{M}_{G}
.

Proof.

We first construct a functor
I:\mathbb{M}_{G}\longrightarrow G\downarrow\mathbf{S}(g,n+1)
and then show that it is an isomorphism.

To define
I
on objects, let
(C,[m]:H\hookrightarrow S_{G})
be a vertex of the complex of markings
\mathcal{M}(S_{G})
. Since
C
is a pants decomposition, the components of
\overline{S_{G}\setminus C}
are pairs of pants. Thus the graph
H
is trivalent, with internal edges corresponding to the curves of
C
and vertices corresponding to the pair-of-pants
components. Since every pants decomposition of a surface of type
(g,n+1)
has
2g+n-1=|\chi(S_{G})|
pairs of pants, the graph
H
has
2g+n-1
trivalent vertices. An Euler characteristic argument then shows that
\beta_{1}(H)=g
.

The marking
[m]
determines the additional structure required to regard
H
as an object of
\mathbf{S}(g,n+1)
. Indeed, the orientation of
S_{G}
induces a cyclic ordering on the half-edges incident to each vertex, while the labelling of the boundary components of
S_{G}
induces a labelling of the boundary legs of
H
. Thus the marked pants decomposition determines an object of
\mathbf{S}(g,n+1)
, which we again denote by
H
.

The object
H
comes with its standard surface
S_{H}
and standard marked pants decomposition
(C_{H},[m_{H}])
. By construction, the marked pants decomposition
(C,[m])
of
S_{G}
and the standard marked pants decomposition of
S_{H}
have the same associated graph. Therefore the local identifications of the
corresponding pairs of pants glue to a boundary-label-preserving diffeomorphism
\phi:S_{G}\longrightarrow S_{H},
which is well defined up to isotopy. We set

I(C,[m])=[\phi:G\to H],

viewed as an object of the coslice category
G\downarrow\mathbf{S}(g,n+1)
.

We now define
I
on morphisms. Let
\alpha:(C,[m])\longrightarrow(C^{\prime},[m^{\prime}])
be a morphism in
\mathbb{M}_{G}
, and suppose

I(C,[m])=[\phi:G\to H]\qquad\text{and}\qquad I(C^{\prime},[m^{\prime}])=[\phi^{\prime}:G\to H^{\prime}].

There is a unique morphism in the coslice category from
[\phi]
to
[\phi^{\prime}]
, namely the mapping class

[\phi^{\prime}\phi^{-1}]:H\longrightarrow H^{\prime}.

We set
I(\alpha)=[\phi^{\prime}\phi^{-1}]
.

It remains to show that
I
is an isomorphism. On objects,
I
is bijective. Indeed, given an object
[\phi]:G\to H
of
G\downarrow\mathbf{S}(g,n+1)
, one can pull back the standard marked pants decomposition
(C_{H},[m_{H}])
of
S_{H}
along
\phi
to define a marked pants decomposition

(\phi^{-1}(C_{H}),[\phi^{-1}m_{H}])

of
S_{G}
. This construction is inverse to the one above.

For morphisms, first observe that the coslice category
G\downarrow\mathbf{S}(g,n+1)
has exactly one morphism between any two objects. Indeed, if the objects are
[\phi]:G\to H
and
[\phi^{\prime}]:G\to H^{\prime}
, then a morphism between them is a morphism
[\psi]:H\to H^{\prime}
satisfying
[\psi]\circ[\phi]=[\phi^{\prime}]
. Since
\mathbf{S}(g,n+1)
is a groupoid,
[\phi]
is invertible, so
[\psi]
is necessarily
[\phi^{\prime}]\circ[\phi]^{-1}
.

On the other hand,
\mathbb{M}_{G}
is the fundamental groupoid of
\mathcal{M}(S_{G})
on its vertices. Since
\mathcal{M}(S_{G})
is connected and simply connected by Proposition
2.39
, there is exactly one homotopy class of paths between any two vertices. Thus
\mathbb{M}_{G}
also has exactly one morphism between any two objects. Therefore
I
is bijective on all Hom-sets. Hence
I
is an isomorphism of categories. It follows that

\mathbb{M}_{G}\cong G\downarrow\mathbf{S}(g,n+1).

∎

Recall the mapping classes that described the standard
T
-,
B
-,
A
-, and
S
-moves in Definition
2.37
. We write

\tau\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{S}(0,2)}(|,|),\quad\beta\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{S}(0,3)}\bigl(\raisebox{-2.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/triv.pdf}},(12)^{*}\raisebox{-2.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/triv.pdf}}\bigr),\quad\alpha\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{S}(0,4)}\bigl(\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/fork1.pdf}},\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/fork2.pdf}}\bigr),\quad\sigma\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{S}(1,1)}\bigl(\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/contraction.pdf}},\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/contraction.pdf}}\bigr)

for the corresponding morphisms in
\mathbf{S}
.

Proposition 3.8
.

Every morphism in
\mathbf{S}
can be obtained from
\tau
,
\beta
,
\alpha
,
\sigma
, and their inverses using categorical composition, operadic composition, symmetric group actions, and contractions.

Proof.

Let
[\phi]:G\to H
be a morphism in
\mathbf{S}(g,n+1)
, represented by a boundary-label-preserving diffeomorphism

\phi:S_{G}\longrightarrow S_{H}.

By Lemma
3.7
, this morphism corresponds to the marked pants decomposition of
S_{G}
obtained by pulling back the standard marked pants decomposition of
S_{H}
along
\phi
.

We first translate a single edge of the complex of markings into a morphism of
\mathbf{S}
. Suppose that

(C,[m])\rightsquigarrow(C^{\prime},[m^{\prime}])

is an edge of
\mathcal{M}(S_{G})
of type
T
,
B
,
A
, or
S
. Let

[\phi]:G\to H\qquad\text{and}\qquad[\psi]:G\to H^{\prime}

be the objects of the coslice category
G\downarrow\mathbf{S}(g,n+1)
corresponding to
(C,[m])
and
(C^{\prime},[m^{\prime}])
, respectively, under Lemma
3.7
. The edge determines the unique morphism in the coslice category from
[\phi]
to
[\psi]
; equivalently, there is a unique morphism
[f]:H\to H^{\prime}
such that

[\psi]=[f]\circ[\phi].

We claim that
[f]
is obtained from identities and one of the standard generators
\tau,\beta,\alpha,\sigma
by operadic composition, symmetric group actions, and contractions.

We verify the claim for a
B
-move; the other cases are analogous. Suppose that

(C,[m]:H\hookrightarrow S_{G})\overset{B_{a,b,c}}{\rightsquigarrow}(C^{\prime},[m^{\prime}]:H^{\prime}\hookrightarrow S_{G})

is a
B
-move. Then
C=C^{\prime}
, so the two marked pants decompositions have the same underlying cut system. The move is supported on a single cut-subsurface

S^{\prime}\subset\overline{S_{G}\setminus C}

with boundary components
a,b,c
, and the markings
m
and
m^{\prime}
agree on the complement of
S^{\prime}
.

Choose an identification
\xi:S^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\cong}P
with the standard pair of pants such that

\xi\circ m|_{S^{\prime}}=m_{P},\qquad\xi(a)=\partial_{1},\qquad\xi(b)=\partial_{2},\qquad\xi(c)=\partial_{0}.

By the definition of the
B
-move, under this identification the new marking is given by

\xi\circ m^{\prime}|_{S^{\prime}}=\beta\circ\xi\circ m|_{S^{\prime}}.

Let
v\in V(H)
and
v^{\prime}\in V(H^{\prime})
be the vertices corresponding to the component
S^{\prime}
. Since the move is supported on
S^{\prime}
, the graphs
H
and
H^{\prime}
agree away from these vertices, with all boundary labels preserved. We therefore define a mapping class

f:S_{H}\longrightarrow S_{H^{\prime}}

by taking it to be the identity on every pair-of-pants component except the one corresponding to
v
, and by taking it on that component to be the standard
B
-move
\beta
under the above identifications. If the local identification with the standard pair of pants differs by a cyclic relabelling, then the local map is the corresponding cyclic translate of
\beta
.

Thus
[f]:H\to H^{\prime}
is obtained from
\beta
by operadic composition with identities, together with the relevant symmetric group action. Moreover, the marking corresponding to
[\psi]
is exactly the marking obtained from the one corresponding to
[\phi]
by applying
[f]
after
[\phi]
. Hence

[\psi]=[f]\circ[\phi].

We now complete the proof. Let
[\phi]\in\mathbf{S}(g,n+1)(G,H)
, and let
(C_{\phi},[m_{\phi}])
be the corresponding marked pants decomposition of
S_{G}
. Since
\mathcal{M}(S_{G})
is connected, there is an edge path

(C_{0},[m_{0}])\rightsquigarrow(C_{1},[m_{1}])\rightsquigarrow\cdots\rightsquigarrow(C_{k},[m_{k}])

from the standard marking
(C_{0},[m_{0}])=(iE_{G},[m_{G}])
to
(C_{k},[m_{k}])=(C_{\phi},[m_{\phi}])
.

Under Lemma
3.7
, the standard marking corresponds to the identity morphism
\operatorname{id}_{G}:G\to G
. By the single-edge argument above, each edge in the path corresponds to postcomposition with a morphism

[f_{i}]:H_{i-1}\to H_{i}

obtained from identities and one of
\tau,\beta,\alpha,\sigma
by operadic composition, symmetric group actions, and contractions. Hence

[\phi]=[f_{k}]\circ\cdots\circ[f_{1}]\circ\operatorname{id}_{G}.

Thus
[\phi]
lies in the subcategory generated by
\tau,\beta,\alpha,\sigma
under categorical composition, operadic composition, symmetric group actions, and contractions. Since
[\phi]
was arbitrary, the claim follows.
∎

The preceding proposition identifies the generators of the morphisms in
\mathbf{S}
. The relations among these generators are encoded by the
2
-cells of the complex of markings. Fix
G\in\operatorname{ob}(\mathbf{S}(g,n+1))
. A loop in
\mathcal{M}(S_{G})
based at the standard marking
(iE_{G},[m_{G}])
is represented by an edge path

(iE_{G},[m_{G}])\overset{e_{1}}{\rightsquigarrow}(C_{1},[m_{1}])\overset{e_{2}}{\rightsquigarrow}\cdots\overset{e_{k}}{\rightsquigarrow}(iE_{G},[m_{G}]).

Under Lemma
3.7
, this loop corresponds to a sequence of morphisms in
\mathbf{S}(g,n+1)
whose composite is the identity morphism of
G
:

[f_{k}]\circ\cdots\circ[f_{1}]=\operatorname{id}_{G}\qquad\text{in }\operatorname{Aut}_{\mathbf{S}(g,n+1)}(G).

Each edge
e_{i}
is one of the elementary moves
T
,
B
,
A
, or
S
, so each
[f_{i}]
is obtained from
\tau,\beta,\alpha,\sigma
by operadic composition, symmetric group actions, and contractions.

The boundaries of the
2
-cells in
\mathcal{M}(S_{G})
therefore give equations among
the generators
\tau,\beta,\alpha,\sigma
. The goal of the next few sections is
to show that these are the only equations satisfied by these generators. This
will give a presentation of
\mathbf{S}
, and hence a classification of maps out of the
modular operad
\mathbf{S}
.

Before proving this in full generality, we first study the genus-zero part of
\mathbf{S}
and relate it to operads whose presentations are already known.

3.2.
Genus-zero surfaces and cyclic ribbon braids

As a first step toward a presentation of the modular operad of surfaces, we study its genus-zero truncation. We use the known presentation of the operad of parenthesized ribbon braids to obtain a presentation of
\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{0}}\mathbf{S}
, and then show that this identification is compatible with the cyclic structures.

3.2.1.
Braids and ribbon braids

For
n\geq 1
, let
\mathsf{B}_{n}
denote the braid group on
n
strands. It may be defined as the fundamental group of the configuration space of
n
unordered distinct points in the complex plane. It admits the standard presentation

\mathsf{B}_{n}=\left\langle\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{n-1}\middle|\beta_{i}\beta_{j}=\beta_{j}\beta_{i}\ \text{for }|i-j|\geq 2,\beta_{i}\beta_{i+1}\beta_{i}=\beta_{i+1}\beta_{i}\beta_{i+1}\right\rangle.

For each
n\geq 1
, there is a short exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{PB}_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{B}_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

where
\pi
sends the braid generator
\beta_{i}
to the transposition
(i\ i+1)
. The kernel of
\pi
is the
pure braid group
, denoted
\mathsf{PB}_{n}
. It is generated by the elements

x_{ij}=\beta_{j-1}\cdots\beta_{i+1}\beta_{i}^{2}\beta_{i+1}^{-1}\cdots\beta_{j-1}^{-1},\qquad 1\leq i<j\leq n.

The
ribbon braid group
on
n
strands, denoted
\mathsf{RB}_{n}
, may be defined as the fundamental group of the configuration space of
n
unordered distinct points in the plane, each equipped with a framing (equivalently, each equipped a point of
S^{1}
). It is generated by the braid generators
\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{n-1}
together with twists
\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{n}
, subject to the braid relations above and the additional relations

\beta_{i}\tau_{j}=\tau_{j}\beta_{i}\quad\text{for }j\notin\{i,i+1\},\qquad\beta_{i}\tau_{i+1}=\tau_{i}\beta_{i},\qquad\tau_{i}\tau_{j}=\tau_{j}\tau_{i}\quad\text{for }i\neq j.

The
pure ribbon braid group
, denoted
\mathsf{PRB}_{n}
, is the kernel of the natural projection

\mathsf{RB}_{n}\longrightarrow\Sigma_{n}.

The ribbon braid groups are closely related to mapping class groups of genus-zero surfaces. Indeed, there is a natural homomorphism

\mathsf{PRB}_{n}\longrightarrow\Gamma_{0,n+1}

sending the pure braid generator
x_{ij}
to the Dehn twist about the simple closed curve separating the boundary components labelled
i
and
j
from the remaining boundary components, and sending the twist
\tau_{k}
to the full Dehn twist about the
k
th boundary component. The following proposition is standard, c.f., for example,
[
62
, Section 1.5]
.

Proposition 3.9
.

There is an isomorphism of groups

\mathsf{PRB}_{n}\cong\Gamma_{0,n+1}.

3.2.2.
The operad of parenthesized ribbon braids

The objects of the operad of parenthesized ribbon braids are parenthesized binary products. We encode these products by planar rooted binary trees.

Definition 3.10
.

The
magma operad

\Omega=\{\Omega(n)\}_{n\geq 1}
is the operad in
\mathrm{Set}
whose elements are isomorphism classes of planar rooted binary trees with
n
input legs, equipped with a labelling

\ell_{T}:\{0,1,\dots,n\}\xrightarrow{\cong}\partial(T),

where
\ell_{T}(0)
is the root.

Equivalently,
\Omega
is the free operad generated by the two rooted binary corollas

\mu=\mu(0;1,2)\qquad\text{and}\qquad(12)^{*}\mu=\mu(0;2,1).

These are the two corollas obtained by decorating the rooted binary corolla by the two elements of
\Sigma_{2}
, or equivalently by choosing the order of the two input legs relative to the fixed root. Operadic composition is given by grafting the root of one tree onto an input leg of another. For further details, see
[
25
, Section 6.1]
and
[
13
, Definition 6.10]
.

Remark 3.11
.

Elements of
\Omega(n)
may be identified with fully parenthesized words in the symbols
\{1,\dots,n\}
. Under this identification, the two generators
\mu
and
(12)^{*}\mu
correspond to the binary words
(12)
and
(21)
, respectively, and every parenthesized word is obtained from these generators by operadic composition. For example,

(((12)3)4),\qquad((32)(14))\qquad\text{and}\qquad(1((23)4))

are elements of
\Omega(4)
.

Definition 3.12
.

For each
n\geq 1
, let
\mathsf{PaRB}(n)
be the groupoid whose objects are the elements of
\Omega(n)
.

Given two objects
T_{1},T_{2}\in\Omega(n)
, their boundary labellings determine a unique bijection

\rho_{T_{1},T_{2}}:\partial(T_{1})\xrightarrow{\cong}\partial(T_{2})

such that
\rho_{T_{1},T_{2}}\circ\ell_{T_{1}}=\ell_{T_{2}}
. A morphism
r\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{PaRB}(n)}(T_{1},T_{2})
is a ribbon braid
r\in\mathsf{RB}_{n}
whose underlying permutation is the permutation of the input legs induced by
\rho_{T_{1},T_{2}}
.

Composition in
\mathsf{PaRB}(n)
is given by multiplication in the ribbon braid group
\mathsf{RB}_{n}
. In particular, for any object
T\in\Omega(n)
, the endomorphism group is naturally identified with the pure ribbon braid group:

\operatorname{Aut}_{\mathsf{PaRB}(n)}(T)\cong\mathsf{PRB}_{n}.

The symmetric group
\Sigma_{n}
acts on
\mathsf{PaRB}(n)
by permuting the labels of the input legs. The resulting symmetric sequence

\mathsf{PaRB}=\{\mathsf{PaRB}(n)\}_{n\geq 1}

forms an operad in groupoids. On objects, operadic composition is the grafting operation in
\Omega
. On morphisms, it is given by inserting a ribbon braid in
\mathsf{RB}_{m}
into the
i
th strand of a ribbon braid in
\mathsf{RB}_{n}
. See
[
13
, Definition 6.11]
for details.

An important feature of
\mathsf{PaRB}
is that its morphisms are generated by three elementary morphisms:

\tau\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{PaRB}(1)}(\mid,\mid),\qquad\beta\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{PaRB}(2)}(\mu,(12)^{*}\mu),\qquad\alpha\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathsf{PaRB}(3)}(\mu\circ_{1}\mu,\mu\circ_{2}\mu).

Here
\tau
is the strand twist,
\beta
is the braiding, and
\alpha
is the re-parenthesization isomorphism from
\mu\circ_{1}\mu
to
\mu\circ_{2}\mu
. More precisely, every morphism in
\mathsf{PaRB}(n)
can be written as an iterated categorical and operadic composite of these morphisms and their inverses; see
[
13
, Section 7]
, following the general framework of
[
25
, Chapter 6]
. This presentation above allows one to classify maps out of
\mathsf{PaRB}
.

The following is a restatement of Lemmas 7.1 and 7.4 of
[
13
]
, based on
[
25
, Theorem 6.2.4]
. We write
\cdot
for categorical composition of morphisms in the groupoids
\mathsf{PaRB}(n)
, and
\circ_{i}
for operadic composition.

Theorem 3.13
.

Let
\mathcal{Q}
be an operad in groupoids. To give an operad map

f:\mathsf{PaRB}\longrightarrow\mathcal{Q}

is equivalent to giving a quadruple
(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{t},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{a})
consisting of an object
f(\mu)=\mathbf{m}\in\operatorname{ob}(\mathcal{Q}(2))

and morphisms

f(\tau)=\mathbf{t}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{Q}(1)}(\mathbf{1},\mathbf{1}),\qquad f(\beta)=\mathbf{b}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{Q}(2)}(\mathbf{m},(12)^{*}\mathbf{m})\qquad\text{and}\qquad f(\alpha)=\mathbf{a}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{Q}(3)}(\mathbf{m}\circ_{1}\mathbf{m},\mathbf{m}\circ_{2}\mathbf{m}),

satisfying the relations below.

(T)

\mathbf{t}\circ_{1}1_{\mathbf{m}}=\mathbf{b}\cdot(12)^{*}\mathbf{b}\cdot\bigl(1_{\mathbf{m}}\circ(\mathbf{t},\mathbf{t})\bigr)\qquad\text{in }\mathcal{Q}(3).

(H1)

\bigl((213)^{*}(1_{\mathbf{m}}\circ_{1}\mathbf{b})\bigr)\cdot\bigl((213)^{*}\mathbf{a}\bigr)\cdot(1_{\mathbf{m}}\circ_{1}\mathbf{b})=\bigl((231)^{*}\mathbf{a}\bigr)\cdot(\mathbf{b}\circ_{2}1_{\mathbf{m}})\cdot\mathbf{a}\qquad\text{in }\mathcal{Q}(4).

(H2)

\bigl((132)^{*}(1_{\mathbf{m}}\circ_{1}\mathbf{b})\bigr)\cdot\bigl((132)^{*}\mathbf{a}^{-1}\bigr)\cdot(1_{\mathbf{m}}\circ_{1}\mathbf{b})=\bigl((312)^{*}\mathbf{a}^{-1}\bigr)\cdot(\mathbf{b}\circ_{1}1_{\mathbf{m}})\cdot\mathbf{a}^{-1}\qquad\text{in }\mathcal{Q}(4).

(P)

(\mathbf{a}\circ_{1}1_{\mathbf{m}})\cdot(\mathbf{a}\circ_{3}1_{\mathbf{m}})=(1_{\mathbf{m}}\circ_{1}\mathbf{a})\cdot(\mathbf{a}\circ_{2}1_{\mathbf{m}})\cdot(1_{\mathbf{m}}\circ_{2}\mathbf{a})\qquad\text{in }\mathcal{Q}(5).

Recall from Proposition
3.8
that the
genus-zero generating morphisms in
\mathbf{S}
are

\tau\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{S}(0,2)}(|,|),\quad\beta\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{S}(0,3)}\bigl(\raisebox{-2.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/triv.pdf}},(12)\raisebox{-2.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/triv.pdf}}\bigr)\quad\text{and}\quad\alpha\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{S}(0,4)}\bigl(\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/fork1.pdf}},\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/fork2.pdf}}\bigr),

where these are the mapping classes associated to the standard
T
-,
B
-, and
A
-moves in the complex of markings, respectively. To distinguish these mapping classes from the generating morphisms of
\mathsf{PaRB}
, throughout this subsection we denote the corresponding morphisms in
u^{*}\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{0}}\mathbf{S}
by

\mathbf{t}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{S}(0,2)}(|,|),\quad\mathbf{b}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{S}(0,3)}\bigl(\raisebox{-2.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/triv.pdf}},(12)^{*}\raisebox{-2.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/triv.pdf}}\bigr)\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbf{a}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{S}(0,4)}\bigl(\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/fork1.pdf}},\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/fork2.pdf}}\bigr).

The genus-zero truncation is naturally a cyclic operad, whose arity-
(n+1)
component is
\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{0}}\mathbf{S}(n+1)=\mathbf{S}(0,n+1)
. Its underlying ordinary operad
u^{*}\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{0}}\mathbf{S}
is obtained by choosing the boundary component labelled
0
as the root, so the same morphisms appear with one fewer input, i.e.
u^{*}\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{0}}\mathbf{S}(n)=\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{0}}\mathbf{S}(n+1)=\mathbf{S}(0,n+1)
. The main difference in these groupoids are the actions by the symmetric groups. Notably, they all have the same objects. In particular, the two generating objects in arity
2
are still
P
and
(12)^{*}P
, corresponding to the two rooted planar corollas obtained from the two classes in
\Sigma_{2}^{+}/A_{2}^{+}
after choosing the boundary labelled
0
as the root.

Lemma 3.14
.

There is an isomorphism of operads

I:\mathsf{PaRB}\longrightarrow u^{*}\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{0}}\mathbf{S}

defined on generators by

I(\mu)=P\in\operatorname{ob}\bigl(u^{*}\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{0}}\mathbf{S}(2)\bigr),\quad I(\tau)=\mathbf{t}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{u^{*}\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{0}}\mathbf{S}(1)}(|,|),

I(\beta)=\mathbf{b}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{u^{*}\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{0}}\mathbf{S}(2)}\bigl(\raisebox{-2.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/triv.pdf}},(12)^{*}\raisebox{-2.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/triv.pdf}}\bigr)\quad\text{and}\quad I(\alpha)=\mathbf{a}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{u^{*}\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{0}}\mathbf{S}(3)}\bigl(\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/fork1.pdf}},\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/fork2.pdf}}\bigr).

Proof.

By Theorem
3.13
, it is enough to check that the values assigned to the generators of the operad
\mathsf{PaRB}
satisfy the defining relations (
T
), (
H1
), (
H2
), and (
P
).

We first check the twist–braiding relation (
T
). Under the assignment in the statement of the lemma, this relation becomes

(3.1)

\mathbf{t}\circ_{1}\operatorname{id}_{P}=\mathbf{b}\cdot(12)^{*}\mathbf{b}\cdot\bigl(\operatorname{id}_{P}\circ(\mathbf{t},\mathbf{t})\bigr)

in
\operatorname{Aut}_{u^{*}\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{0}}\mathbf{S}(2)}(P)
. We interpret this equation using the equivalence
\mathbb{M}_{P}\cong P\downarrow\mathbf{S}(0,3)
from Lemma
3.7
. Under this equivalence, the left-hand side is the full Dehn twist about the boundary
component labelled
0
, which we denote by
T_{0}
. The right-hand side identifies with
B_{1,2}B_{2,1}T_{1}T_{2}
: here

(12)^{*}B_{1,2}=B_{2,1}
, and the two inserted twists in
\operatorname{id}_{P}\circ(\mathbf{t},\mathbf{t})
become the boundary twists
T_{1}
and

T_{2}
.

Thus (
3.1
) is equivalent to
T_{0}=B_{1,2}B_{2,1}T_{1}T_{2}.
Since Dehn twists about boundary components commute, this is equivalent to

B_{1,2}B_{2,1}=T_{0}T_{1}^{-1}T_{2}^{-1},

which is precisely the twist–braiding
2
-cell in the marking complex
\mathbb{M}_{P}
. Hence the image of (
T
) under
I
holds.

We next check the pentagon relation (
P
). We rewrite it as the statement that

(\alpha\circ_{1}\operatorname{id}_{\mu})^{-1}\cdot(\alpha\circ_{3}\operatorname{id}_{\mu})^{-1}\cdot(\operatorname{id}_{\mu}\circ_{2}\alpha)\cdot(\alpha\circ_{2}\operatorname{id}_{\mu})\cdot(\operatorname{id}_{\mu}\circ_{1}\alpha)=1

in
\operatorname{Aut}_{\mathsf{PaRB}(4)}((\mu\circ_{1}\mu)\circ_{1}\mu)
.

Let
\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/fork1.pdf}}=(P\circ_{1}P)\circ_{1}P
. After applying
I
, and then using the equivalence
\mathbb{M}_{S_{\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/fork1.pdf}}}}\cong\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/fork1.pdf}}\downarrow\mathbf{S}(0,5)
from Lemma
3.7
, the left-hand side is identified with the loop of
A
-moves

(A_{b,e})^{-1}(A_{a,d})^{-1}A_{c,e}A_{b,d}A_{a,c}

in the complex of markings. The curves defining these
A
-moves are shown in Figure
16
. This loop is the boundary of the pentagon
2
-cell, and hence represents the identity morphism in
\mathbb{M}_{S_{\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/fork1.pdf}}}}
. Therefore its image is the identity in
\operatorname{Aut}_{u^{*}\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{0}}\mathbf{S}(4)}(\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/fork1.pdf}})
, so
I
respects the pentagon relation (
P
).

The hexagon relations (
H1
) and (
H2
) are verified in the same way. Each may be rewritten as a closed composite in
\mathsf{PaRB}(3)(\mu\circ_{1}\mu,\mu\circ_{1}\mu)
and
\mathsf{PaRB}(3)(\mu\circ_{2}\mu,\mu\circ_{2}\mu)
, respectively. Applying
I
, and then using the corresponding equivalences with the fundamental groupoids of the marking complexes, these automorphisms are identified with the two hexagon loops shown in Figure
17
. Each loop bounds a hexagon
2
-cell in the complex of markings. Hence both automorphisms are trivial, and
I
respects the hexagon relations (
H1
) and (
H2
). It follows from Theorem
3.13

that the assignment on generators extends to a map of operads

I:\mathsf{PaRB}\longrightarrow u^{*}\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{0}}\mathbf{S}.

It remains to show that this map is an isomorphism. On objects,
I
is the identity. For every object
T\in\mathsf{PaRB}(n)
, the induced map on automorphism groups is the standard identification
\mathsf{PRB}_{n}\cong\Gamma_{0,n+1}
from Proposition
3.9
.
Thus each functor
I(n):\mathsf{PaRB}(n)\longrightarrow u^{*}\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{0}}\mathbf{S}(n)
is fully faithful. Since it is also bijective on objects, it is an isomorphism of groupoids for each
n
. Therefore
I
is an isomorphism of operads.
∎

The operad
\mathsf{PaRB}
admits a cyclic structure in the sense of Definition
2.20
; see
[
16
,
53
,
59
]
. We write
\mathsf{PaRB}^{\mathrm{cyc}}
for the resulting cyclic operad.

It is enough to describe the action of the transposition
(01)
, since this action, together with the ordinary symmetric group actions, determines the full extended symmetric group action. On objects,
(01)
changes the choice of root: the leg labelled
1
becomes the new root. In particular, on the generating object one has
(01)^{*}\mu=(12)^{*}\mu.
Since
\mathsf{PaRB}
admits a finite presentation, it remains to specify the action of
(01)
on the generating morphisms. This action is given by

(
\star
)

(01)^{*}(\tau)=\tau,\qquad(01)^{*}(\beta)=\beta^{-1}\cdot(\operatorname{id}_{\mu}\circ_{2}\tau^{-1})\qquad\text{and}\qquad(01)^{*}(\alpha)=(231)^{*}\alpha^{-1}.

We now show that the operad isomorphism constructed in Lemma
3.14
respects this root-changing action, and therefore upgrades to an isomorphism of cyclic operads.

Proposition 3.15
.

The isomorphism of operads
I:\mathsf{PaRB}\to u^{*}\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{0}}\mathbf{S}
is compatible with the cyclic structure on
\mathsf{PaRB}
. Hence it induces an isomorphism of cyclic operads

\mathsf{PaRB}^{\mathrm{cyc}}\cong\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{0}}\mathbf{S}.

Proof.

Since the
(01)
-action determines the cyclic structure on
\mathsf{PaRB}
, it suffices to verify
I((01)^{*}x)=(01)^{*}I(x)
for
x=\mu,\tau,\beta,\alpha
.

For the generating object, recall that
I(\mu)=P
, where
P
is the rooted trivalent corolla with boundary labels
\{0,1,2\}
. The objects of
\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{0}}\mathbf{S}
are generated by the two rooted planar corollas represented by
P=\operatorname{id}\cdot A_{2}^{+}
and
(12)^{*}P=(12)\cdot A_{2}^{+}
, where
A_{2}^{+}=\langle(012)\rangle
. Since
(01)
and
(12)
represent the same nontrivial coset modulo
A_{2}^{+}
, we have
(01)^{*}P=(12)^{*}P
. Therefore
I((01)^{*}\mu)=(01)^{*}I(\mu)
.

For the twist generator,
I(\tau)=\mathbf{t}
. Recall that, by definition, the transposition

(01)
acts trivially on
\mathbf{S}(0,2)
. Hence

I((01)^{*}\tau)=I(\tau)=\mathbf{t}=(01)^{*}I(\tau).

For the braiding generator, we compare the cyclic translate of
\mathbf{b}:P\to(12)^{*}P
using the marking complex for the correct source object. Applying
(01)^{*}
gives a morphism
(01)^{*}\mathbf{b}:(12)^{*}P\longrightarrow P.

Through the equivalence

\mathbb{M}_{(12)^{*}P}\cong(12)^{*}P\downarrow\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{0}}\mathbf{S}(3),

the morphism
(01)^{*}\mathbf{b}
is identified with the opposite
B
-move, together with the boundary-twist correction needed to rewrite the
(01)
-relabelled marking in standard form. With our conventions this gives

(01)^{*}B_{1,2}=T_{0}^{-1}T_{1}\cdot B_{2,1}.

Using the twist–braiding relation in the marking complex,

B_{1,2}B_{2,1}=T_{0}T_{1}^{-1}T_{2}^{-1},

we obtain

(01)^{*}B_{1,2}=T_{0}^{-1}T_{1}\cdot B_{1,2}^{-1}T_{0}T_{1}^{-1}T_{2}^{-1}=B_{1,2}^{-1}T_{2}^{-1}.

The twist
T_{2}^{-1}
is the twist inserted on the second input. In operadic notation, this says

(01)^{*}\mathbf{b}=\mathbf{b}^{-1}\cdot(\operatorname{id}_{P}\circ_{2}\mathbf{t}^{-1}).

This is exactly the image under
I
of the relation
(01)^{*}\beta=\beta^{-1}\cdot(\operatorname{id}_{\mu}\circ_{2}\tau^{-1}).
Therefore
I((01)^{*}\beta)=(01)^{*}I(\beta)
.

It remains to check the associativity generator. Applying
(01)^{*}
to the morphism
\mathbf{a}:P\circ_{1}P\longrightarrow P\circ_{2}P
in
\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{0}}\mathbf{S}(4)
gives a morphism

(01)^{*}\mathbf{a}:(01)^{*}(P\circ_{1}P)\longrightarrow(01)^{*}(P\circ_{2}P).

A direct calculation on labelled rooted corollas gives

(01)^{*}(P\circ_{1}P)=(231)^{*}(P\circ_{2}P)\qquad\text{and}\qquad(01)^{*}(P\circ_{2}P)=(231)^{*}(P\circ_{1}P).

Thus both
(01)^{*}\mathbf{a}
and
(231)^{*}\mathbf{a}^{-1}
are morphisms

(231)^{*}(P\circ_{2}P)\longrightarrow(231)^{*}(P\circ_{1}P).

To compare them, we compose with
(231)^{*}\mathbf{a}
and consider the resulting
automorphism:

(01)^{*}\mathbf{a}\cdot(231)^{*}\mathbf{a}:(231)^{*}(P\circ_{2}P)\rightarrow(231)^{*}(P\circ_{2}P).

Passing through the equivalence
\mathbb{M}_{(231)^{*}(P\circ_{2}P)}\cong(231)^{*}(P\circ_{2}P)\downarrow\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{0}}\mathbf{S}(4),
this loop is identified with the loop in the marking complex obtained by performing the
(01)
-relabelled
A
-move and then the corresponding

(231)
-relabelled
A
-move back. This is precisely the self-duality loop for the
A
-move. By the self-duality relation

A_{c,c^{\prime}}A_{c^{\prime},c}=\operatorname{id},

the loop is trivial. Hence
(01)^{*}\mathbf{a}=(231)^{*}\mathbf{a}^{-1}.
Therefore
I((01)^{*}\alpha)=(01)^{*}I(\alpha)
.

It follows that
I
is compatible with the cyclic structures and thus induces an isomorphism
\mathsf{PaRB}^{\mathrm{cyc}}\cong\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{0}}\mathbf{S}.

∎

Remark 3.16
.

In the proof of Lemma
3.14
, we freely used diagrammatic depictions of genus-zero mapping classes and their corresponding paths in the complexes of markings. This is justified by Lemma
3.7
, together with the discussion in Section
2.6.2
, which shows that these diagrammatic representatives are well defined. For example, Figure
10
depicts the action of the
(01)
-transposition on the
B
- and
A
-moves.

Figure 10
.
Action of the
(01)
-transposition on the
B
- and
A
-moves.

3.3.
A presentation for maps out of
\mathbf{S}

We use
\cdot
for categorical composition in the groupoids
\mathbf{S}(g,n+1)
, write
\circ_{j}^{i}
for cyclic operadic composition, and write
\xi_{i}^{j}
for
contractions. When only the underlying operad structure is involved, we use the convention
\circ_{j}^{0}=\circ_{j}
.

The goal of this subsection is to prove the main presentation theorem for maps out of the modular operad of surfaces
\mathbf{S}
. As in the genus-zero case, the proof uses the comparison between the groupoids
\mathbf{S}(g,n+1)
and the
2
-dimensional CW complexes of reduced marked cut systems on surfaces of type
(g,n+1)
. This comparison is established in Lemma
3.7
. It allows us to apply Proposition
2.39
, which states that
\mathcal{M}(S)
is connected and simply connected, and hence to obtain a presentation for the morphisms of
\mathbf{S}(g,n+1)
.

Theorem 3.17
.

Let
\mathcal{Q}
be a modular operad in groupoids. A map of modular operads
f:\mathbf{S}\longrightarrow\mathcal{Q}
is uniquely determined by a tuple

(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{t},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{a},\mathbf{s})

consisting of an object
f(P)=\mathbf{m}\in\operatorname{ob}(\mathcal{Q}(0,3))

and morphisms

f(\tau)=\mathbf{t}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{Q}(0,2)}(|,|),\quad f(\beta)=\mathbf{b}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{Q}(0,3)}(\mathbf{m},(12)^{*}\mathbf{m}),

f(\alpha)=\mathbf{a}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{Q}(0,4)}(\mathbf{m}\circ_{1}\mathbf{m},\mathbf{m}\circ_{2}\mathbf{m}),\quad f(\sigma)=\mathbf{s}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{Q}(1,1)}(\xi_{12}\mathbf{m},\xi_{12}\mathbf{m}),

satisfying the following relations.

(S)

(012)^{*}(\mathbf{m})=\mathbf{m}\qquad\text{in }\operatorname{ob}(\mathcal{Q}(0,3)).

(T)

(\mathbf{t}\circ_{1}\operatorname{id}_{\mathbf{m}})=\mathbf{b}\cdot(12)^{*}\mathbf{b}\cdot(\operatorname{id}_{\mathbf{m}}\circ(\mathbf{t},\mathbf{t}))\qquad\text{in }\mathcal{Q}(0,3).

(H1)

((213)^{*}(\operatorname{id}_{\mathbf{m}}\circ_{1}^{0}\mathbf{b}))\cdot((213)^{*}\mathbf{a})\cdot(\operatorname{id}_{\mathbf{m}}\circ_{1}^{0}\mathbf{b})=((231)^{*}\mathbf{a})\cdot(\mathbf{b}\circ_{2}^{0}\operatorname{id}_{\mathbf{m}})\cdot\mathbf{a}\qquad\text{in }\mathcal{Q}(0,4).

(H2)

((132)^{*}(\operatorname{id}_{\mathbf{m}}\circ_{1}^{0}\mathbf{b}))\cdot((132)^{*}\mathbf{a}^{-1})\cdot(\operatorname{id}_{\mathbf{m}}\circ_{1}^{0}\mathbf{b})=((312)^{*}\mathbf{a}^{-1})\cdot(\mathbf{b}\circ_{1}^{0}\operatorname{id}_{\mathbf{m}})\cdot\mathbf{a}^{-1}\qquad\text{in }\mathcal{Q}(0,4).

(P)

(\mathbf{a}\circ_{1}\operatorname{id}_{\mathbf{m}})\cdot(\mathbf{a}\circ_{3}\operatorname{id}_{\mathbf{m}})=(\operatorname{id}_{\mathbf{m}}\circ_{1}\mathbf{a})\cdot(\mathbf{a}\circ_{2}\operatorname{id}_{\mathbf{m}})\cdot(\operatorname{id}_{\mathbf{m}}\circ_{2}\mathbf{a})\qquad\text{in }\mathcal{Q}(0,5).

(G1a)

\mathbf{s}^{2}=\xi_{0,2}\bigl(\mathbf{b}^{-1}\bigr)\qquad\text{in }\mathcal{Q}(1,1).

(G1b)

\mathbf{d}^{-1}\cdot\mathbf{s}\cdot\mathbf{d}^{-1}\cdot\mathbf{s}\cdot\mathbf{d}^{-1}=\mathbf{s}\qquad\text{in }\mathcal{Q}(1,1),

where
\mathbf{d}=\xi_{12}(\operatorname{id}_{\mathbf{m}}\circ_{1}\mathbf{t}).

(3.2)

\xi_{12}\bigl((012)^{*}\mathbf{b}\circ_{2}(\mathbf{t}^{-1},\mathbf{t}^{-1})\circ\mathbf{b}\bigr)\\
=\xi_{12}(\mathbf{a})\cdot(\operatorname{id}_{\mathbf{m}}\circ_{1}\mathbf{s})\cdot\xi_{12}(\mathbf{a}^{-1})\cdot\xi_{12}\bigl(\operatorname{id}_{\mathbf{m}}\circ_{2}(\mathbf{t}^{-1},\mathbf{t})\circ\operatorname{id}_{\mathbf{m}}\bigr)\\
\cdot\xi_{12}(\mathbf{a})\cdot(\operatorname{id}_{\mathbf{m}}\circ_{1}\mathbf{s})\cdot\xi_{12}(\mathbf{a}^{-1})\qquad\text{in }\mathcal{Q}(1,2).

Relations
G1a
,
G1b
and
3.2
are added to include the higher genus structure. For instance,
equation
3.2
can be pictured diagrammatically as in Figure
11
.

Figure 11
.
Diagram of relation (G2)

Proof.

A map of modular operads
f:\mathbf{S}\to\mathcal{Q}
necessarily determines the images of the generating morphisms:

f(\mu)=\mathbf{m},\quad f(\tau)=\mathbf{t},\quad f(\beta)=\mathbf{b},\quad f(\alpha)=\mathbf{a},\quad f(\sigma)=\mathbf{s}.

Moreover, such a map consists of functors
f(g,n+1):\mathbf{S}(g,n+1)\longrightarrow\mathcal{Q}(g,n+1)
compatible with the symmetric group actions, operadic compositions, and contractions. Under the equivalence of Lemma
3.7
, the boundaries of the relevant
2
-cells in the marking complexes
\mathcal{M}(S_{G})
are identified with the relations (
S
), (
T
), (
P
), (
H1
), (
H2
), (
G1a
), (
G1b
), and (
3.2
). Therefore any map
f:\mathbf{S}\to\mathcal{Q}
gives a tuple
(\mathbf{m},\mathbf{t},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{a},\mathbf{s})
satisfying the stated relations.

We now prove the converse. Suppose we are given
\mathbf{m},\mathbf{t},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{a},\mathbf{s}
in
\mathcal{Q}
satisfying the stated relations. Since
\operatorname{ob}(\mathbf{S})
is the free modular operad in sets generated by the genus-zero corolla
P
and
(12)^{*}P
, the assignment
\operatorname{ob}(f)(P)=\mathbf{m}\in\operatorname{ob}(\mathcal{Q}(0,3))
extends uniquely to a map of modular operads in sets

\operatorname{ob}(f):\operatorname{ob}(\mathbf{S})\longrightarrow\operatorname{ob}(\mathcal{Q}).

In particular, the object assignment is compatible with the extended symmetric group actions, operadic compositions, and contractions. Note that since the object
P\in\mathbf{S}(0,3)
is represented by the coset
\operatorname{id}\cdot A_{2}^{+}
, where
A_{2}^{+}=\langle(012)\rangle
. Thus
(012)^{*}P=P
, and the condition

(012)^{*}\mathbf{m}=\mathbf{m}

is precisely the condition needed for the assignment
P\mapsto\mathbf{m}
to respect the stabiliser of the generating object.

Using the cyclic operad isomorphism
\mathsf{PaRB}^{\mathrm{cyc}}\cong\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{0}}\mathbf{S}
from Proposition
3.15
, together with Theorem
3.13
, the assignments

\mu\mapsto P,\qquad\tau\mapsto\mathbf{t},\qquad\beta\mapsto\mathbf{b},\qquad\text{and}\qquad\alpha\mapsto\mathbf{a}

satisfying (
T
), (
P
), (
H1
), and (
H2
) define the genus-zero
part of
f
, namely a map of cyclic operads
\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{0}}f:\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{0}}\mathbf{S}\longrightarrow\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{0}}\mathcal{Q}.

It remains to extend the genus-zero map to all of
\mathbf{S}
. By Proposition
3.8
, every morphism in
\mathbf{S}
is obtained from the generating morphisms
\alpha,\beta,\tau,\sigma
by modular operad operations and categorical composition. Thus, for a morphism

\phi\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{S}(g,n+1)}(G,G^{\prime}),

we choose an expression for
\phi
in terms of these generators and define its image in
\mathcal{Q}(g,n+1)
by replacing
\alpha,\beta,\tau,
and
\sigma
by
\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{t}
and
\mathbf{s}
.

We must check that this description of
f(\phi)
is independent of the chosen expression. So suppose that
\phi
has two expressions

\phi=\phi^{1}_{k}\cdots\phi^{1}_{1}=\phi^{2}_{m}\cdots\phi^{2}_{1},

in
\mathbf{S}(g,n+1)
, where each
\phi^{r}_{i}
is one of the generating morphisms of
\mathbf{S}
. Then

(\phi^{2}_{m}\cdots\phi^{2}_{1})^{-1}(\phi^{1}_{k}\cdots\phi^{1}_{1})=\operatorname{id}_{G}\ \in\operatorname{Aut}_{\mathbf{S}(g,n+1)}(G).

Under the equivalence of Lemma
3.7
, this relation is represented by a loop in the marking complex
\mathcal{M}(S_{G})
. Since
\mathcal{M}(S_{G})
is connected and simply connected
[
3
, Theorem 5.1]
, this loop is generated by the boundaries of the
2
-cells of
\mathcal{M}(S_{G})
. The boundary relations of these
2
-cells are exactly the relations imposed above, together with the formal relations already forced by the modular operad axioms.

More precisely, the twist–braiding, pentagon, and two hexagon cells are encoded by (
T
), (
P
), (
H1
), and (
H2
). The self-duality of the associativity move follows from Mac Lane coherence, and therefore from the pentagon and hexagon relations already imposed. The genus-one cells in types
(1,1)
and
(1,2)
are encoded by (
G1a
), (
G1b
), and (
3.2
). The remaining cells express formal compatibility relations for disjointly supported moves and for the modular operad operations. These hold in
\mathcal{Q}
because
\mathcal{Q}
is a modular operad.

It follows that

(f(\phi^{2}_{m})\cdots f(\phi^{2}_{1}))^{-1}(f(\phi^{1}_{k})\cdots f(\phi^{1}_{1}))=\operatorname{id}_{f(G)}.

Thus the two expressions for
\phi
have the same image in
\mathcal{Q}
, so the assignment on morphisms is well defined. By construction it is compatible with categorical composition, symmetric group actions, operadic compositions, and contractions. Hence it defines a map of modular operads
f:\mathbf{S}\longrightarrow\mathcal{Q}.
The theorem follows.

∎

4.
The operadic two-level principle

Grothendieck’s
two-level principle
predicts that Galois actions on the Teichmüller tower should be determined by their effect in the first two levels,
namely on

\widehat{\Gamma}_{0,4},\quad\widehat{\Gamma}_{0,5},\quad\widehat{\Gamma}_{1,1},\quad\text{and}\quad\widehat{\Gamma}_{1,2}.

In this section we formulate and prove an operadic version of this principle. Our main goal is to show that there exists an action of
\mathbf{\mathrm{Gal}}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q})
on the profinite completion of the surface modular operad
\widehat{\mathbf{S}}
(Theorem
4.8
). Moreover, this action is completely determined by the genus one truncation of
\widehat{\mathbf{S}}
(Corollary
4.3
).

We begin with a brief discussion of profinite completion for modular operads in groupoids.

4.1.
Profinite completion of modular operads in groupoids

Proposition
2.6
allows us to define profinite completion entrywise for modular operads in groupoids satisfying the relevant finiteness hypotheses.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
\mathcal{P}=\{\mathcal{P}(g,n+1)\}
be a modular operad in groupoids such that each
\mathcal{P}(g,n+1)
has finitely many objects. Its profinite completion is the modular operad in profinite groupoids

\widehat{\mathcal{P}}=\{\widehat{\mathcal{P}}(g,n+1)\}.

The structure maps are obtained by applying profinite completion to the structure maps of
\mathcal{P}
, using the product comparison to identify products of completions with completions of products.

More precisely, the partial composition

\widehat{\mathcal{P}}(g,n+1)\times\widehat{\mathcal{P}}(h,m+1)\longrightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{P}}(g+h,n+m)

is the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathcal{P}}(g,n+1)\times\widehat{\mathcal{P}}(h,m+1)}

\mathcal{P}(g,n+1)\times\mathcal{P}(h,m+1)

\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathcal{P}}(g+h,n+m).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{(\circ_{i}^{j})}}

Here the first map is the inverse of the product isomorphism from Proposition
2.6
, and the second map is obtained by applying profinite completion to the composition map of
\mathcal{P}
. The contraction maps

\widehat{(\xi_{i}^{j})}:\widehat{\mathcal{P}}(g,n+1)\longrightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{P}}(g+1,n-1)

are defined by applying profinite completion to the contraction maps of
\mathcal{P}
.

4.1.1.
The genus-one truncation

The presentation theorem (Theorem
3.17
) shows that maps out of
\mathbf{S}
are determined by the images of the generators and relations appearing in genus at most one. We express this using the truncation formalism introduced in Section
2.5.1
and developed further in Appendix
A.1
:
\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{1}}\mathcal{P}
denotes the genus-one truncation of a modular operad
\mathcal{P}
, and the truncation functor admits a left adjoint
(\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{1}})_{!}
.

Corollary 4.2
.

The counit of the truncation adjunction gives an isomorphism of modular operads in groupoids

(\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{1}})_{!}(\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{1}}\mathbf{S})\cong\mathbf{S}.

Proof.

By Theorem
3.17
, a map out of
\mathbf{S}
is uniquely determined by the images of the generators appearing in
\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{1}}\mathbf{S}
, and every choice of such images satisfying the stated relations extends uniquely to a map out of
\mathbf{S}
. Thus, for every modular operad
\mathcal{Q}
, restriction induces a natural bijection

\operatorname{Map}_{\mathrm{Mod}(\mathscr{G})}(\mathbf{S},\mathcal{Q})\cong\operatorname{Map}_{\mathrm{Mod}_{\leq 1}(\mathscr{G})}(\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{1}}\mathbf{S},\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{1}}\mathcal{Q}).

By the adjunction
(\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{1}})_{!}\dashv\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{1}}
, the right-hand side is naturally isomorphic to
\operatorname{Map}_{\mathrm{Mod}(\mathscr{G})}((\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{1}})_{!}(\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{1}}\mathbf{S}),\mathcal{Q}).
Under these identifications, the natural bijection is induced by precomposition with the counit

(\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{1}})_{!}(\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{1}}\mathbf{S})\longrightarrow\mathbf{S}.

Therefore, by Yoneda, the counit is an isomorphism.
∎

We will use this after profinite completion. Let
\eta_{\mathcal{P}}:\mathcal{P}\to|\widehat{\mathcal{P}}|
denote the unit of the profinite-completion adjunction, where
|-|:\widehat{\mathscr{G}}\to\mathscr{G}
is the forgetful functor. Thus, for any modular operad
\mathcal{R}
in profinite groupoids, composition with
\eta_{\mathcal{P}}
gives a natural bijection

\operatorname{Map}_{\mathrm{Mod}(\widehat{\mathscr{G}})}(\widehat{\mathcal{P}},\mathcal{R})\cong\operatorname{Map}_{\mathrm{Mod}(\mathscr{G})}(\mathcal{P},|\mathcal{R}|).

Equivalently, every map
f:\mathcal{P}\to|\mathcal{R}|
extends uniquely to a continuous map
\widetilde{f}:\widehat{\mathcal{P}}\to\mathcal{R}
with
|\widetilde{f}|\circ\eta_{\mathcal{P}}=f
.

In particular, taking
\mathcal{P}=\mathbf{S}
and
\mathcal{R}=\widehat{\mathbf{S}}
, the presentation theorem describes maps
\mathbf{S}\to|\widehat{\mathbf{S}}|
, and the adjunction upgrades each such map uniquely to a continuous endomorphism
\widehat{\mathbf{S}}\to\widehat{\mathbf{S}}
.

Corollary 4.3
.

The truncation functor
\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{1}}
induces an isomorphism of monoids

\operatorname{End}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbf{S}})\xrightarrow{\cong}\operatorname{End}_{0}(\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{1}}\widehat{\mathbf{S}}).

Proof.

By the profinite-completion adjunction, endomorphisms of
\widehat{\mathbf{S}}
are equivalent to maps
\mathbf{S}\to|\widehat{\mathbf{S}}|
. Since the unit
\eta_{\mathbf{S}}:\mathbf{S}\to|\widehat{\mathbf{S}}|
does not change the object set, this bijection restricts to object-fixing maps:

\operatorname{End}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbf{S}})\cong\operatorname{Map}_{0}(\mathbf{S},|\widehat{\mathbf{S}}|).

Corollary
4.2
identifies this set with
\operatorname{Map}_{0}(\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{1}}\mathbf{S},|\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{1}}\widehat{\mathbf{S}}|)
. Since profinite completion is applied entrywise, it commutes with genus truncation, so
\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{1}}\widehat{\mathbf{S}}
is the profinite completion of
\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{1}}\mathbf{S}
. A second application of the profinite-completion adjunction gives

\operatorname{Map}_{0}(\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{1}}\mathbf{S},|\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{1}}\widehat{\mathbf{S}}|)\cong\operatorname{End}_{0}(\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{1}}\widehat{\mathbf{S}}).

Combining these bijections gives the desired isomorphism of monoids.
∎

4.2.
The action of
\mathbf{\mathrm{Gal}}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q})
on
\widehat{\mathbf{S}}

To study Galois actions on higher-genus mapping class groups, we work with explicit profinite groups containing
\mathbf{\mathrm{Gal}}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q})
and admitting well-understood presentations. The most prominent example is the Grothendieck–Teichmüller group
\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}
, introduced by Drinfeld
[
19
, Section 4]
.
In their study of Galois actions on higher-genus mapping class groups, Nakamura and Schneps introduced in
[
54
]
a subgroup
\widehat{\mathsf{\Gamma}}
of the Grothendieck–Teichmüller group, satisfying

\mathbf{\mathrm{Gal}}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q})\hookrightarrow\widehat{\mathsf{\Gamma}}\hookrightarrow\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}.

Rather than defining the Galois action directly on the full profinite modular operad
\widehat{\mathbf{S}}
, we construct it via these explicit profinite groups.

Let
\widehat{\mathsf{F}}_{2}
denote the profinite completion of the free group on two generators
x
and
y
. We use the following standard convention: if
f=f(x,y)\in\widehat{\mathsf{F}}_{2}
and
a,b
are elements of a profinite group
\mathsf{G}
, then
f(a,b)
denotes the image of
f
under the unique
continuous homomorphism
\widehat{\mathsf{F}}_{2}\longrightarrow\mathsf{G}
sending
x\mapsto a
and
y\mapsto b
.

Definition 4.4
.

The
Grothendieck–Teichmüller monoid

\underline{\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}}
consists of pairs
(\lambda,f)\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\times\widehat{\mathsf{F}}_{2}
such that the assignment

x\longmapsto f(x,y)^{-1}x^{\lambda}f(x,y),\qquad y\longmapsto y^{\lambda}

defines a continuous endomorphism
\varphi_{(\lambda,f)}\colon\widehat{\mathsf{F}}_{2}\longrightarrow\widehat{\mathsf{F}}_{2}

and the following equations hold:

(I)

f(x,y)f(y,x)=1,

(II)

f(z,x)z^{\nu}f(y,z)y^{\nu}f(x,y)x^{\nu}=1,\qquad z=(xy)^{-1},\qquad\nu=(\lambda-1)/2.

and

(III)

f(x_{12},x_{23}x_{24})f(x_{13}x_{23},x_{34})=f(x_{23},x_{34})f(x_{12}x_{13},x_{24}x_{34})f(x_{12},x_{23}).

The first two equations hold in
\widehat{F}_{2}
, and the last equation holds
in the profinite completion of the pure braid group
\widehat{\mathsf{PB}}_{4}
. The multiplication is defined by composition of the associated endomorphisms.
Thus, for
F=(\lambda,f)
and
G=(\mu,g)
, we set

F\cdot G=(\lambda,f)\cdot(\mu,g):=\left(\lambda\mu,\,g(x,y)\,f\bigl(g(x,y)^{-1}x^{\mu}g(x,y),\,y^{\mu}\bigr)\right),

so that

\varphi_{F\cdot G}=\varphi_{G}\circ\varphi_{F}.

The Grothendieck–Teichmüller group
\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}
is the group of invertible elements of the monoid
\underline{\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}}
.

Remark 4.5
.

The reader familiar with
\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}
might note that our multiplication convention on
\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}
differs slightly from that of
[
19
]
. We follow the composition convention used in
[
38
]
, where the product is defined so that the associated endomorphisms of
\widehat{\mathsf{F}}_{2}
compose by
\varphi_{F\cdot G}=\varphi_{G}\circ\varphi_{F}.

Drinfeld described the action of
\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}
on profinite braid groups in his original definition of the Grothendieck–Teichmüller group
[
19
]
. An operadic interpretation of this action was developed in
[
42
]
, building on work such as
[
5
,
25
]
. This was extended to an action on profinite ribbon braid operads by the second author and collaborators in
[
13
]
. The compatibility with the cyclic structure on
\mathsf{PaRB}
was recently established by the second author and Singh
[
59
]
.

By Proposition
3.15
, there is an isomorphism of cyclic operads

\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{0}}\widehat{\mathbf{S}}\cong\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}^{\mathrm{cyc}}.

We use this identification to regard the genus-zero
\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}
-action on
\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}^{\mathrm{cyc}}
as an action on
\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{0}}\widehat{\mathbf{S}}
. Explicitly, an element
F=(\lambda,f)\in\underline{\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}}
determines an object-fixing endomorphism
\iota_{F}\in\operatorname{End}_{0}(\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{0}}\widehat{\mathbf{S}})
by its action on the standard generators
\tau,\beta,\alpha
:

\iota_{F}(\tau)=\tau^{\lambda},\qquad\iota_{F}(\beta)=\beta\cdot\bigl(\beta\cdot(12)^{*}\beta\bigr)^{\nu},\qquad\iota_{F}(\alpha)=\alpha\cdot f(D_{c^{\prime}},D_{c}),

where
\nu=(\lambda-1)/2
, and where
c
and
c^{\prime}
are the curves shown in Figure
5
.

Proposition 4.6
.

There is an isomorphism of monoids

\operatorname{End}_{0}(\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{0}}\widehat{\mathbf{S}})\cong\underline{\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}}.

Proof.

This follows from the identification
\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{0}}\widehat{\mathbf{S}}\cong\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}^{\mathrm{cyc}}
above and the isomorphism
\underline{\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}}\cong\operatorname{End}_{0}(\widehat{\mathsf{PaRB}}^{\mathrm{cyc}})
proved in
[
59
, Theorem 5.4]
.
∎

In
[
54
]
, Nakamura and Schneps introduced a subgroup
\widehat{\mathsf{\Gamma}}\subseteq\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}
by imposing two additional relations, discovered through comparisons of Galois representations in
\widehat{\Gamma}_{0,5}
and
\widehat{\Gamma}_{1,2}
. These relations encode the genus-one compatibility needed to extend the genus-zero Grothendieck–Teichmüller action to the full tower of profinite mapping class groups.

Definition 4.7
.

The monoid
\underline{\widehat{\mathsf{\Gamma}}}\subseteq\underline{\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}}
consists of those elements
(\lambda,f)\in\underline{\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}}
satisfying the following two additional relations:

(NS I)

f(\beta_{1}\beta_{3},\beta_{2}^{2})(\beta_{1}\beta_{2}\beta_{3})^{4\rho_{2}}=h((\beta_{1}\beta_{2})^{3},(\beta_{2}\beta_{3})^{3})f(\beta_{1}^{2},\beta_{2}^{2})f(\beta_{3}^{2},(\beta_{1}\beta_{2})^{3})\quad\text{in }\widehat{\Gamma}_{0,5},

and

(NS II)

f(\beta_{1},\beta_{2}^{4})=\beta_{2}^{8\rho_{2}}f(\beta_{1}^{2},\beta_{2}^{2})\beta^{4}_{1}(\beta_{1}\beta_{2})^{-6\rho_{2}}\quad\text{in }\widehat{\mathsf{B}}_{3}/\mathbb{Z}\cong\widehat{\Gamma}_{1,1}.

Here
h=h(x,y)
is the unique element of
\widehat{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
such that

f(x,y)=h(y,x)^{-1}h(x,y)

[
47
]
, and
\rho_{2}
denotes the Kummer
1
-cocycle with respect to the roots of
2
. Equivalently,

h(x,y)\equiv(xy)^{\rho_{2}}

in the abelianisation
\widehat{\mathbb{F}}_{2}^{ab}\cong\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\times\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}
(see
[
54
, Definition 5.1]
). The group
\widehat{\mathsf{\Gamma}}
is the group of invertible elements of of the monoid
\underline{\widehat{\mathsf{\Gamma}}}
.

In (
NS I
), the elements
\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\beta_{3}
are the standard generators of the braid group
\mathsf{B}_{4}
, viewed as elements of the mapping class group
\Gamma_{0,5}
; see
[
54
, Proposition 5.4]
. Nakamura and Schneps prove that
\widehat{\mathsf{\Gamma}}
is a subgroup of
\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}
and that the absolute Galois group maps into
\widehat{\mathsf{\Gamma}}

[
54
, Theorem 1.2]
. More explicitly, for
g\in\mathbf{\mathrm{Gal}}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q})
, the corresponding element is
(\lambda_{g},f_{g})
, where
\lambda_{g}
is the cyclotomic character and
f_{g}(x,y)
lies in the commutator subgroup of
\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{et}}(\mathcal{M}_{0,4})\cong\widehat{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
.

Nakamura and Schneps construct actions of
\widehat{\mathsf{\Gamma}}
on profinite mapping class groups using surfaces equipped with quilt, or seam, decompositions. For the purposes of the formulae below, a marking
m:G\hookrightarrow S
provides the same data: it determines the standard curves and Dehn twist generators on which the Nakamura–Schneps action is defined. In
[
54
, Theorem 11.2]
, they give explicit formulae for the action of
\widehat{\mathsf{\Gamma}}
on these Dehn twists. We translate those formulae into the language of marked surfaces and use them to construct an action on the profinite modular operad
\widehat{\mathbf{S}}
. The resulting action extends the genus-zero
\underline{\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}}
-action identified in
Proposition
4.6
.

Theorem 4.8
.

There is an inclusion of monoids
\underline{\widehat{\mathsf{\Gamma}}}\hookrightarrow\operatorname{End}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbf{S}})
whose restriction to genus zero agrees with the isomorphism

\underline{\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}}\cong\operatorname{End}_{0}(\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{0}}\widehat{\mathbf{S}})

from Proposition
4.6
, after restricting along
\underline{\widehat{\mathsf{\Gamma}}}\hookrightarrow\underline{\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}}
.

Proof.

Let
F=(\lambda,f)\in\underline{\widehat{\mathsf{\Gamma}}}
. Recall that an object-fixing map of modular operads
\iota_{F}:\mathbf{S}\longrightarrow|\widehat{\mathbf{S}}|
extends uniquely to a continuous object-fixing endomorphism
\hat{\iota}_{F}:\widehat{\mathbf{S}}\longrightarrow\widehat{\mathbf{S}}.
Thus it is enough, using Theorem
3.17
, to choose morphisms

\hat{\iota}_{F}(\tau)=\mathbf{t}_{F}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\widehat{\mathbf{S}}(0,2)}(|,|),\qquad\hat{\iota}_{F}(\beta)\mathbf{b}_{F}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\widehat{\mathbf{S}}(0,3)}(P,(12)^{*}P),

and

\hat{\iota}_{F}(\alpha)=\mathbf{a}_{F}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\widehat{\mathbf{S}}(0,4)}(P\circ_{1}P,P\circ_{2}P),\qquad\hat{\iota}_{F}(\sigma)=\mathbf{s}_{F}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\widehat{\mathbf{S}}(1,1)}(\xi_{12}P,\xi_{12}P)

and check that these choices satisfy the defining relations. Since
\iota_{F}
is object-fixing, the object-level symmetry relation (
S
) is automatic. Thus it remains to check the morphism relations (
T
), (
P
), (
H1
), (
H2
), (
G1a
), (
G1b
), and (
3.2
).

We chose the values of our generating morphisms using the action described in
[
54
]
. Explicitly, letting
\nu=(\lambda-1)/2
, we set

\displaystyle\mathbf{t}_{F}

\displaystyle=\tau^{\lambda}

\displaystyle\mathbf{b}_{F}

\displaystyle=\beta\cdot(\beta\cdot(12)^{*}\beta)^{\nu}

(4.1)

\displaystyle\mathbf{a}_{F}

\displaystyle=\alpha\cdot f(D_{d},D_{c})

\displaystyle\mathbf{s}_{F}

\displaystyle=\sigma^{\lambda}\cdot D_{b}^{8\rho_{2}(F)}\cdot f(D_{b}^{2},D_{a}^{2})\cdot D_{a}^{-8\rho_{2}(F)}.

Here
\rho_{2}(F)
denotes the value, uniquely determined by
F=(\lambda,f)
, of the Nakamura–Schneps extension of the Kummer
1
-cocycle with respect to the roots of
2
. The symbol
D_{i}
denotes the Dehn twist about the curve

. The curves

appearing in (
4.1

Figure 12
.

We now check the defining relations. The genus-zero relations can be dealt with first. By Proposition 8.1 of
[

and Proposition

, the object-fixing endomorphisms of

acts on the generators by the formulae defining

\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{0}}(\iota_{F}):\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{0}}\mathbf{S}\longrightarrow|\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{0}}\widehat{\mathbf{S}}|,

and the genus-zero relations (

), (
H1

It remains to check the genuinely genus-one relations (

3.2

We first check the two relations in type

We will use the curves shown in Figure

\displaystyle\hat{\iota}_{F}(G1a)

Checking relation (

\displaystyle\iota_{F}(G1b)

Using that

We start by noting the following equality on mapping class groups

\xi_{12}[(z_{3}^{*}\beta)\circ_{2}(\tau^{-1},\tau^{-1})\circ\beta)]=\partial_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}\partial_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}

\iota_{F}(G2)=\partial_{0}^{-\frac{\lambda}{2}}\partial_{1}^{-\frac{\lambda}{2}}f(a_{1},e_{3})\cdot(a_{3}a_{2}a_{3})^{\lambda}a_{2}^{8\rho_{2}}f(a_{2}^{2},a_{3}^{2})a_{3}^{-8\rho_{2}}\cdot f(e_{3},a_{1})\cdot a_{3}^{-\lambda}a_{1}^{\lambda}\cdot f(a_{1},e_{3})\cdot(a_{3}a_{2}a_{3})^{\lambda}a_{2}^{8\rho_{2}}f(a_{2}^{2},a_{3}^{2})a_{3}^{-8\rho_{2}}\cdot f(e_{3},a_{1})

\iota_{F}(G2)=\partial_{0}^{-\frac{\lambda}{2}}\partial_{1}^{-\frac{\lambda}{2}}f(a_{1},e_{3})\cdot(a_{3}a_{2}a_{3})^{\lambda}a_{2}^{8\rho_{2}}f(a_{2}^{2},a_{3}^{2})a_{3}^{-8\rho_{2}}\cdot f(e_{3},a_{1})\cdot a_{3}^{-\lambda}a_{1}^{\lambda}\cdot f(a_{1},e_{3})\cdot\\
\mathbf{(a_{3}a_{2}a_{3})}\cdot(a_{3}a_{2}a_{3})^{2\nu}a_{2}^{8\rho_{2}}f(a_{2}^{2},a_{3}^{2})a_{3}^{-8\rho_{2}}\cdot f(e_{3},a_{1})

Now using that

\displaystyle(a_{3}a_{2}a_{3})^{-1}e_{3}(a_{3}a_{2}a_{3})

\displaystyle=e_{3}

\displaystyle(a_{3}a_{2}a_{3})^{-1}a_{1}(a_{3}a_{2}a_{3})

\displaystyle=e_{2}

\displaystyle(a_{3}a_{2}a_{3})^{-1}a_{3}(a_{3}a_{2}a_{3})

\displaystyle=a_{2}

we get

\iota_{F}(G2)=\partial_{0}^{-\frac{\lambda}{2}}\partial_{1}^{-\frac{\lambda}{2}}f(a_{1},e_{3})\cdot(a_{3}a_{2}a_{3})^{\lambda}a_{2}^{8\rho_{2}}f(a_{2}^{2},a_{3}^{2})a_{3}^{-8\rho_{2}}\cdot f(e_{3},a_{1})\cdot\mathbf{[a_{3}^{-1}a_{1}(a_{3}a_{2}a_{3})]}\cdot a_{2}^{-2\nu}e_{2}^{2\nu}\cdot\\
f(e_{2},e_{3})\cdot(a_{3}a_{2}a_{3})^{2\nu}a_{2}^{8\rho_{2}}f(a_{2}^{2},a_{3}^{2})a_{3}^{-8\rho_{2}}\cdot f(e_{3},a_{1})

Since
a_{1}
and
a_{3}
are disjoint, they commute. Hence the expression in bold is equal to
a_{1}a_{2}a_{3}
. We now use the following relations in the mapping class group

\displaystyle(a_{1}a_{2}a_{3})^{-1}a_{1}(a_{1}a_{2}a_{3})

\displaystyle=e_{2}

\displaystyle(a_{1}a_{2}a_{3})^{-1}e_{3}(a_{1}a_{2}a_{3})

\displaystyle=e_{1}

\displaystyle(a_{1}a_{2}a_{3})^{-1}a_{3}(a_{1}a_{2}a_{3})

\displaystyle=a_{2}

\displaystyle(a_{1}a_{2}a_{3})^{-1}a_{2}(a_{1}a_{2}a_{3})

\displaystyle=a_{1}

then pulling the bold terms to the left, we get

\iota_{F}(G2)=\partial_{0}^{-\frac{\lambda}{2}}\partial_{1}^{-\frac{\lambda}{2}}f(a_{1},e_{3})\cdot\mathbf{[(a_{3}a_{2}a_{3})(a_{1}a_{2}a_{3})]}\cdot(a_{2}a_{1}a_{2})^{2\nu}a_{1}^{8\rho_{2}}f(a_{1}^{2},a_{2}^{2})a_{2}^{-8\rho_{2}}\cdot f(e_{1},e_{2})\cdot a_{2}^{-2\nu}e_{2}^{2\nu}\cdot\\
f(e_{2},e_{3})\cdot(a_{3}a_{2}a_{3})^{2\nu}a_{2}^{8\rho_{2}}f(a_{2}^{2},a_{3}^{2})a_{3}^{-8\rho_{2}}\cdot f(e_{3},a_{1})

Now note that

\displaystyle[(a_{3}a_{2}a_{3})(a_{1}a_{2}a_{3})]^{-1}e_{3}[(a_{3}a_{2}a_{3})(a_{1}a_{2}a_{3})]

\displaystyle=e_{1}

\displaystyle[(a_{3}a_{2}a_{3})(a_{1}a_{2}a_{3})]^{-1}a_{1}[(a_{3}a_{2}a_{3})(a_{1}a_{2}a_{3})]

\displaystyle=a_{3}

And we get

\iota_{F}(G2)=\partial_{0}^{-\frac{\lambda}{2}}\partial_{1}^{-\frac{\lambda}{2}}\mathbf{[(a_{3}a_{2}a_{3})(a_{1}a_{2}a_{3})]}\cdot f(a_{3},e_{1})\cdot(a_{2}a_{1}a_{2})^{2\nu}a_{1}^{8\rho_{2}}f(a_{1}^{2},a_{2}^{2})a_{2}^{-8\rho_{2}}\cdot f(e_{1},e_{2})\cdot a_{2}^{-2\nu}e_{2}^{2\nu}\cdot\\
f(e_{2},e_{3})\cdot(a_{3}a_{2}a_{3})^{2\nu}a_{2}^{8\rho_{2}}f(a_{2}^{2},a_{3}^{2})a_{3}^{-8\rho_{2}}\cdot f(e_{3},a_{1})

Since

\displaystyle(a_{3}a_{2}a_{3})(a_{1}a_{2}a_{3})

\displaystyle=\partial_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}\partial_{2}^{\frac{1}{2}},

our expression for
\iota_{F}(G2)
simplifies to

\iota_{F}(G2)=\partial_{0}^{-\nu}\partial_{1}^{-\nu}\cdot f(a_{3},e_{1})\cdot(a_{2}a_{1}a_{2})^{2\nu}a_{1}^{8\rho_{2}}f(a_{1}^{2},a_{2}^{2})a_{2}^{-8\rho_{2}}\cdot f(e_{1},e_{2})\cdot a_{2}^{-2\nu}e_{2}^{2\nu}\cdot\\
f(e_{2},e_{3})\cdot(a_{3}a_{2}a_{3})^{2\nu}a_{2}^{8\rho_{2}}f(a_{2}^{2},a_{3}^{2})a_{3}^{-8\rho_{2}}\cdot f(e_{3},a_{1})

which is shown to be trivial in
[
54
, Proof of Claim 8.3, relation (6AS), end of page 542]
(recall that in that paper they compose mapping classes in the opposite order).

Homomorphism:
We have now constructed a map
\widehat{\mathsf{\Gamma}}\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Aut}_{0}(\hat{S})
and we finish by showing it preserves the composition in
