\widehat{\mathsf{\Gamma}}
. Recall that, for
F=(\lambda,f)
and
G=(\mu,g)
, our multiplication
convention is defined by

F\cdot G=(\lambda\mu,\,g(x,y)\,f(g(x,y)^{-1}x^{\mu}g(x,y),y^{\mu})),

so that
\varphi_{F\cdot G}=\varphi_{G}\circ\varphi_{F}
. It remains to verify that the composition
\iota_{G}\circ\iota_{F}
agrees with
\iota_{F\cdot G}
on the generators
\tau
,
\beta
,
\alpha
, and
\sigma
. The verification for
\tau
and
\beta
is immediate. The arguments for
\alpha
and
\sigma
are similar, so we only discuss the latter, which is slightly more involved.

We now show that

\iota_{G}\circ\iota_{F}(\sigma)=\iota_{F\cdot G}(\sigma).

The left-hand side is equal to

(4.2)

\displaystyle\iota_{G}(\sigma^{\lambda}\cdot{b}^{8\rho_{2}(F)}\cdot f({b}^{2},a^{2})\cdot a^{-8\rho_{2}(F)})=\iota_{G}(\sigma)^{\lambda}\cdot{\iota_{G}(b)}^{8\rho_{2}(F)}\cdot f({\iota_{G}(b)}^{2},\iota_{G}(a)^{2})\cdot\iota_{G}(a)^{-8\rho_{2}(F)}

The term
\iota_{G}(\sigma)
is explicitly described in (
4.1
) and, as discussed in the proof of (
3.2
), we have
\iota_{G}(a)=a^{\mu}
. Moreover, since
b=\sigma^{-1}a\sigma
, we get

\displaystyle\iota_{G}(b)

\displaystyle=\iota_{G}(\sigma)^{-1}\cdot\iota_{G}(a)\cdot\iota_{G}(\sigma)

\displaystyle=[\sigma^{\mu}\cdot b^{8\rho_{2}(G)}\cdot g(b^{2},a^{2})\cdot a^{-8\rho_{2}(G)}]^{-1}\cdot a^{\mu}\cdot[\sigma^{\mu}\cdot b^{8\rho_{2}(G)}\cdot g(b^{2},a^{2})\cdot a^{-8\rho_{2}(G)}]

\displaystyle=a^{8\rho_{2}(G)}\cdot g(b^{2},a^{2})^{-1}\cdot b^{-8\rho_{2}(G)}\cdot\sigma^{-\mu}\cdot a^{\mu}\cdot\sigma^{\mu}\cdot b^{8\rho_{2}(G)}\cdot g(b^{2},a^{2})\cdot a^{-8\rho_{2}(G)}.

Since
\mu
is of the form
2\nu^{\prime}+1
, and that
\sigma^{2}
commutes with
a
and
b
, we get

\displaystyle\iota_{G}(b)

\displaystyle=a^{8\rho_{2}(G)}\cdot g(b^{2},a^{2})^{-1}\cdot b^{-8\rho_{2}(G)}\cdot b^{\mu}\cdot b^{8\rho_{2}(G)}\cdot g(b^{2},a^{2})\cdot a^{-8\rho_{2}(G)}

\displaystyle=a^{8\rho_{2}(G)}g(b^{2},a^{2})^{-1}b^{\mu}g(b^{2},a^{2})a^{-8\rho_{2}(G)}.

Substituting these expressions into (
4.2
), we obtain

(4.3)

\iota_{G}\circ\iota_{F}(\sigma)=[\sigma^{\mu}\cdot b^{8\rho_{2}(G)}\cdot g(b^{2},a^{2})\cdot a^{-8\rho_{2}(G)}]^{\lambda}\cdot a^{8\rho_{2}(G)}g(b^{2},a^{2})^{-1}b^{8\rho_{2}(F)\mu}g(b^{2},a^{2})a^{-8\rho_{2}(G)}\cdot\\
\cdot f(a^{8\rho_{2}(G)}g(b^{2},a^{2})^{-1}b^{2\mu}g(b^{2},a^{2})a^{-8\rho_{2}(G)},a^{2\mu})\cdot a^{-8\rho_{2}(F)\mu}

We begin by simplifying the first factor on the right-hand side using that:

\displaystyle[\sigma^{\mu}\cdot b^{8\rho_{2}(G)}\cdot g(b^{2},a^{2})\cdot a^{-8\rho_{2}(G)}]^{2}

\displaystyle=\sigma^{\mu}\cdot b^{8\rho_{2}(G)}\cdot g(b^{2},a^{2})\cdot a^{-8\rho_{2}(G)}\cdot\sigma^{\mu}\cdot b^{8\rho_{2}(G)}\cdot g(b^{2},a^{2})\cdot a^{-8\rho_{2}(G)}

\displaystyle=\sigma^{2\mu}\cdot a^{8\rho_{2}(G)}\cdot g(a^{2},b^{2})\cdot b^{-8\rho_{2}(G)}\cdot b^{8\rho_{2}(G)}\cdot g(b^{2},a^{2})\cdot a^{-8\rho_{2}(G)}

\displaystyle=\sigma^{2\mu}

Since
\lambda=2\nu+1
, equation (
4.3
) becomes

\iota_{G}\circ\iota_{F}(\sigma)=[\sigma^{\lambda\mu}\cdot b^{8\rho_{2}(G)}\cdot g(b^{2},a^{2})\cdot a^{-8\rho_{2}(G)}]\cdot a^{8\rho_{2}(G)}g(b^{2},a^{2})^{-1}b^{8\rho_{2}(F)\mu}g(b^{2},a^{2})a^{-8\rho_{2}(G)}\cdot\\
\cdot f(a^{8\rho_{2}(G)}g(b^{2},a^{2})^{-1}b^{2\mu}g(b^{2},a^{2})a^{-8\rho_{2}(G)},a^{2\mu})\cdot a^{-8\rho_{2}(F)\mu}.

After cancellation, this simplifies to

\displaystyle\iota_{G}\circ\iota_{F}(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\sigma^{\lambda\mu}\cdot b^{8(\rho_{2}(G)+\rho_{2}(F)\mu)}\cdot g(b^{2},a^{2})\cdot f(g(b^{2},a^{2})^{-1}b^{2\mu}g(b^{2},a^{2}),a^{2\mu})\cdot a^{-8(\rho_{2}(G)+\rho_{2}(F)\mu)}.

Using that
\rho_{2}(F\cdot G)=\rho_{2}(G)+\rho_{2}(F)\mu
, as discussed in
[
54
, Corollary 5.2]
, we see that this is precisely the formula defining
\iota_{F\cdot G}(\sigma)
, as required. Finally, the homomorphism is injective because its restriction to the
genus-zero truncation agrees with the inclusion

\underline{\widehat{\mathsf{\Gamma}}}\subseteq\underline{\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}}
under the isomorphism

\underline{\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}}\cong\operatorname{End}_{0}(\operatorname{tr}_{\leq 0}\widehat{\mathbf{S}})

of Proposition
4.6
.
∎

Remark 4.9
.

The curve
e_{2}
in Figure
14
differs slightly from the curve labelled
e_{2}
in
[
54
]
. The relation used in their calculation, however, is

(a_{2}a_{3})^{-1}a_{1}(a_{2}a_{3})=e_{2},

which is the relation satisfied by the curve
e_{2}
drawn here. Thus our figure should be read as using the curve determined by this conjugation relation.

We also note that Nakamura–Schneps write
\mu
for the quantity we denote by
\nu=(\lambda-1)/2
, since
\mu
is already reserved in this paper for the generating object of
\mathsf{PaRB}
.

Theorem
4.8
, together with the inclusion
\mathbf{\mathrm{Gal}}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q})\hookrightarrow\widehat{\mathsf{\Gamma}}
constructed by Nakamura–Schneps, immediately gives the corresponding Galois action on the profinite modular operad of
surfaces.

Corollary 4.10
.

The absolute Galois group
\mathbf{\mathrm{Gal}}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q})
acts on
\widehat{\mathbf{S}}
.

Proof.

This follows from Theorem
4.8
and the inclusion

\mathbf{\mathrm{Gal}}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q})\hookrightarrow\widehat{\mathsf{\Gamma}}
of Nakamura–Schneps.
∎

5.
Modular
\infty
-operads

To take profinite completions of modular operads in spaces, we need a model for homotopy-coherent modular operads. The relevant indexing category is the modular dendroidal category
\mathscr{M}
, whose objects are connected genus-graded labelled graphs with non-empty boundary. Strict modular operads can be regarded as certain presheaves on
\mathscr{M}
: their value on a graph is determined by their values on the corollas at its vertices. More precisely, the modular dendroidal nerve of a strict modular operad satisfies

X_{G}\cong\prod_{v\in V_{G}}X_{C_{v}}.

See,
[
35
, Theorem 3.6]
. A modular
\infty
-operad is obtained by replacing this strict product condition by a Segal condition.

We first recall the modular dendroidal category. Let
G=(G,\lambda,\ell,\epsilon)
be a genus-graded labelled graph. For each vertex
v\in V_{G}
, let
H_{v}
be a genus-graded labelled graph whose boundary is identified with the half-edges incident to
v
. We require
H_{v}
to have total genus
\epsilon(v)
, equivalently

\epsilon(v)=\beta_{1}(H_{v})+\sum_{w\in V_{H_{v}}}\epsilon_{H_{v}}(w),

where
\epsilon_{H_{v}}
denotes the genus function on
H_{v}
. Then we can define a new graph
G\{H_{v}\}_{v\in V_{G}}
by the coequaliser

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\coprod_{e\in iE_{G}}\updownarrow}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\coprod_{v\in V_{G}}H_{v}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\{H_{v}\}_{v\in V_{G}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

Here, the maps
u
and
d
record the two incidences of each internal edge of
G
. The coequaliser glues the corresponding boundary legs of the graphs
H_{v}
. This is the
graph substitution
construction of
[
34
, Construction 1.18]
.

Definition 5.1
.

The
modular dendroidal category

\mathscr{M}
is the category whose objects are connected genus-graded labelled graphs with non-empty boundary. Its morphisms, called
graphical maps
, are generated by isomorphisms and graph substitutions. More precisely, a graphical map records the data of replacing vertices of a graph by graphs with matching boundary and genus data. See
[
34
, Definition 1.31]
for the full definition.

\displaystyle v
\displaystyle w
\displaystyle u
\displaystyle H_{v}
\displaystyle H_{w}
\displaystyle H_{u}

Figure 15
.
An example of a graphical map

The category of
modular dendroidal objects
in
\mathscr{E}
, denoted
\mathrm{dMod}(\mathscr{E})
, is the category of contravariant functors
X:\mathscr{M}^{\mathrm{op}}\longrightarrow\mathscr{E}
and natural transformations. We write
X_{G}
for the value of
X\in\mathrm{dMod}(\mathscr{E})
on a graph
G\in\mathscr{M}
, and we write

\mathscr{M}[G]:=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathscr{M}}(-,G)

for the representable functor at
G
.

Definition 5.2
.

[
34
, Section 3.1]

Let
G
be a graph with at least one vertex. The
Segal core
of
G
is the coequaliser in
\mathrm{dMod}(\mathrm{Set})

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\coprod_{e\in iE_{G}}\mathscr{M}[\updownarrow]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\coprod_{v\in V_{G}}\mathscr{M}[C_{v}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Sc}[G].}

The two arrows record the two incidences of each internal edge with the corollas at its endpoints. The Segal core is a subobject of the representable
\mathscr{M}[G]
, with inclusion
\mathrm{Sc}[G]\longrightarrow\mathscr{M}[G]
induced by the inclusions of the local vertex data
\mathscr{M}[C_{v}]\to\mathscr{M}[G]
.

The Segal core detects whether the value of a modular dendroidal object on a graph is determined, up to homotopy, by its values on the corollas at the vertices. When
\mathscr{E}
is a Cartesian monoidal model category, the category
\mathrm{dMod}(\mathscr{E})
admits the projective model structure, in which a map
X\to Y
is a weak equivalence, respectively a fibration, if and only if
X_{G}\longrightarrow Y_{G}
is a weak equivalence, respectively a fibration, in
\mathscr{E}
for every
G\in\mathscr{M}
; see
[
41
, Theorem 11.6.1]
.

A modular dendroidal object
X
is called
Segal
if, for every
G\in\mathscr{M}
, the map induced by the Segal core inclusion

\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}(\mathscr{M}[G],X)\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}(\mathrm{Sc}[G],X)

is a weak equivalence of spaces.
5
5

5

Here
\mathscr{M}[G]
and
\mathrm{Sc}[G]
are regarded as discrete objects of
\mathrm{dMod}(\mathscr{E})
via the functor
\mathrm{Set}\to\mathscr{E}
.
A modular dendroidal object
X
is called
reduced
if
X_{\updownarrow}=*
.

Definition 5.3
.

A
modular
\infty
-operad
in
\mathscr{E}
is a modular dendroidal object

X:\mathscr{M}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathscr{E}
satisfying:

•

X_{\updownarrow}=*
;

•

for every graph
G\in\mathscr{M}
, the Segal map

\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}(\mathscr{M}[G],X)\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}(\mathrm{Sc}[G],X)

is a weak equivalence of spaces.

The condition
X_{\updownarrow}=*
means that we are considering one-coloured modular
\infty
-operads. It also simplifies the Segal condition. Indeed, for a reduced object
X
, the edge-compatibility conditions in the Segal core are trivial, since each internal edge contributes a copy of
X_{\updownarrow}=*
. Thus, after replacing
X
by an entrywise fibrant object if necessary, the Segal map may be identified with the comparison

X_{G}\longrightarrow\prod_{v\in V_{G}}(X_{C_{v}})_{f}.

Here
(X_{C_{v}})_{f}
denotes a fibrant replacement in
\mathscr{E}
, so that the product computes the corresponding homotopy product. Equivalently, a reduced
modular dendroidal object is a modular
\infty
-operad precisely when, for every graph
G
, the value
X_{G}
is weakly equivalent to the homotopy product
of its values on the corollas at the vertices of
G
.

In the cases used in this paper we do not need to worry much about fibrant replacements. If
\mathscr{E}=\mathscr{G}
, every object is fibrant, so the homotopy product is computed by the ordinary product. If
\mathscr{E}=\mathscr{S}
and
X
is entrywise fibrant, for example if
X
is fibrant in the localised Reedy model structure, the ordinary product again computes the homotopy product. In this situation the Segal condition can be written simply as requiring

X_{G}\longrightarrow\prod_{v\in V_{G}}X_{C_{v}}

to be a weak equivalence.

5.1.
The modular dendroidal nerve

Let
\mathrm{Mod}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})
denote the
\infty
-category of modular
\infty
-operads, as in Definition
B.1
.
A strict modular operad assigns operations to genus-graded corollas. Viewing a general genus-graded graph as a pattern for composing such operations, one obtains a modular dendroidal object by decorating each vertex with an operation of the corresponding genus and arity. This construction defines the modular dendroidal
nerve
functor

\mathrm{N}:\mathrm{Mod}(\mathscr{E})\longrightarrow\mathrm{Mod}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E}).

Definition 5.4
.

The
nerve
of a modular operad
\mathcal{P}
is the contravariant functor
\mathrm{N}\mathcal{P}:\mathscr{M}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathscr{E}
defined on a graph
G=(G,\lambda,\ell,\epsilon)
by

(\mathrm{N}\mathcal{P})_{G}:=\prod_{v\in V_{G}}\mathcal{P}\bigl(\epsilon(v),|nb(v)|\bigr).

In particular, if
C_{v}
is the corolla at a vertex
v
, then
(\mathrm{N}\mathcal{P})_{C_{v}}=\mathcal{P}\bigl(\epsilon(v),|nb(v)|\bigr).

For a strict modular operad
\mathcal{P}
, the Segal map is an isomorphism rather than just a weak equivalence. Indeed, for every graph
G
, the definition of the nerve identifies

(\mathrm{N}\mathcal{P})_{G}\cong\prod_{v\in V_{G}}(\mathrm{N}\mathcal{P})_{C_{v}}.

Thus the nerve of a strict modular operad satisfies the Segal condition strictly. In particular, when ordinary finite products compute finite homotopy products in
\mathscr{E}
, the object
\mathrm{N}\mathcal{P}
is a modular
\infty
-operad. This applies in the cases used in this paper, for example when
\mathscr{E}=\mathscr{G}
, and for entrywise fibrant objects in
\mathscr{S}
.

The nerve is fully faithful and admits a left adjoint
\tau:\mathrm{dMod}(\mathscr{E})\longrightarrow\mathrm{Mod}(\mathscr{E})
such that
\tau\mathrm{N}\cong\operatorname{id}_{\mathrm{Mod}(\mathscr{E})}
(
[
35
, Theorem 3.6]
). In Appendix
B
, we prove a homotopical analogue of this statement. Namely, if
\mathscr{E}=\mathscr{S}
or
\mathscr{G}
, then the modular dendroidal nerve is homotopically fully faithful. Thus, for any
\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q}\in\mathrm{Mod}(\mathscr{E})
, the induced map

\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}_{\mathrm{Mod}(\mathscr{E})}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}_{\mathrm{Mod}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})}(\mathrm{N}\mathcal{P},\mathrm{N}\mathcal{Q})

is a weak equivalence of spaces.

6.
From profinite groupoids to profinite spaces

In previous sections, we showed that the modular operad in profinite groupoids
\widehat{\mathbf{S}}
admits an action of the monoid
{\widehat{\mathsf{\Gamma}}}
(Theorem
4.8
). The goal of this section is to show that
\widehat{\mathrm{B}\mathrm{N}\mathbf{S}}
forms a modular
\infty
-operad in profinite spaces admitting an induced action of
{\widehat{\mathsf{\Gamma}}}
. This modular
\infty
-operad may be regarded both as the profinite completion of the modular operad
\mathrm{B}\mathbf{S}
of moduli spaces of surfaces and as a model for a Teichmüller tower built from étale homotopy types.

In this setting, the strict action of
{\widehat{\mathsf{\Gamma}}}
on
\widehat{\mathbf{S}}
induces an action in the homotopy category of modular
\infty
-operads. We therefore begin by explaining the passage from strict endomorphisms to homotopy endomorphisms.

6.1.
Profinite completion of modular
\infty
-operads

We first explain how profinite completion is applied to modular dendroidal objects. Let
\mathscr{E}
be either
\mathscr{S}
or
\mathscr{G}
, and let
\mathscr{C}\subseteq\mathscr{E}
denote the full subcategory of
\pi
-finite spaces or finite groupoids, respectively.

We use the profinite completion of diagram categories developed in
[
12
, Theorem 2.4]
. For a suitably filtered small category
\mathscr{K}
, the pro-
\mathscr{C}
completion of the category of coskeletal diagrams in
\operatorname{Fun}(\mathscr{K}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{E})
can be identified with the category of
\operatorname{Pro}(\mathscr{C})
-valued diagrams
\operatorname{Fun}(\mathscr{K}^{\mathrm{op}},\operatorname{Pro}(\mathscr{C}))
. In this sense, profinite completion of coskeletal diagrams is computed entrywise.

We apply this result with
\mathscr{K}=\mathscr{M}
. The graphical category
\mathscr{M}
is a generalised Reedy category
[
34
, Theorem 4.10 and Remark 4.17]
, and diagrams
X\in\operatorname{Fun}(\mathscr{M}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{E})
are coskeletal in the relevant sense; see
[
58
]
and
[
10
, Section 6]
. Hence the construction applies to modular dendroidal objects.

Definition 6.1
.

Let
X:\mathscr{M}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathscr{E}
be a modular dendroidal object. The
profinite completion
of
X
is the modular dendroidal object

\widehat{X}:\mathscr{M}^{\mathrm{op}}\longrightarrow\operatorname{Pro}(\mathscr{C})

obtained by applying pro-
\mathscr{C}
completion entrywise.

Since profinite completion is a left adjoint, it does not in general preserve finite limits. Thus, even if a modular dendroidal object
X
satisfies the Segal condition, its entrywise profinite completion

\widehat{X}:\mathscr{M}^{\mathrm{op}}\longrightarrow\operatorname{Pro}(\mathscr{C})

need not satisfy the Segal condition. In general, profinite completion produces a modular dendroidal object in profinite spaces or profinite groupoids, rather
than a profinite modular
\infty
-operad.

There is, however, a useful exception in the groupoid case. As discussed in Section
4.1
, and in particular in Proposition
2.6
, profinite completion is compatible with finite products under suitable finiteness hypotheses. The following lemma applies this product comparison to the Segal condition for modular
\infty
-operads in groupoids.

Recall that we write
C_{(g,n+1)}
for the corolla of genus
g
with
n+1
legs. Thus, if
v
is a vertex of a graph
G
, the corolla at
v
is
C_{(\epsilon(v),|nb(v)|)}
, with the labelling and genus grading inherited from
G
.

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
X:\mathscr{M}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathscr{G}
be a modular
\infty
-operad such that each corolla value

X_{C_{(g,n+1)}}
has finitely many objects. Then entrywise profinite completion defines a modular
\infty
-operad

\widehat{X}:\mathscr{M}^{\mathrm{op}}\longrightarrow\widehat{\mathscr{G}}.

Proof.

Since profinite completion sends the trivial groupoid to the trivial profinite groupoid, we have
\widehat{X}_{\updownarrow}=*
.

It remains to check the Segal condition. For every graph
G
, the Segal map for
X
is an equivalence of groupoids

X_{G}\longrightarrow\prod_{v\in V_{G}}X_{C_{(\epsilon(v),|nb(v)|)}}.

Applying profinite completion gives an equivalence in
\widehat{\mathscr{G}}

\widehat{X}_{G}\longrightarrow\left(\prod_{v\in V_{G}}X_{C_{(\epsilon(v),|nb(v)|)}}\right)^{\wedge}.

The graph
G
has finitely many vertices, and each corolla value

X_{C_{(\epsilon(v),|nb(v)|)}}
has finitely many objects. Hence Proposition
2.6
identifies the target naturally with
\prod_{v\in V_{G}}\widehat{X}_{C_{(\epsilon(v),|nb(v)|)}}.

Thus the completed Segal map

\widehat{X}_{G}\longrightarrow\prod_{v\in V_{G}}\widehat{X}_{C_{(\epsilon(v),|nb(v)|)}}

is a weak equivalence in
\widehat{\mathscr{G}}
. Therefore
\widehat{X}
satisfies the Segal condition, and hence is a modular
\infty
-operad in profinite groupoids.
∎

6.2.
From endomorphisms to homotopy endomorphisms

We now compare strict endomorphisms of the profinite modular operad
\widehat{\mathbf{S}}
with homotopy endomorphisms of the completed modular dendroidal nerve. The first point is that, under the relevant finiteness hypotheses, profinite completion commutes with the modular dendroidal nerve.

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be a modular operad in groupoids such that
\mathcal{P}(g,n+1)
has finitely many objects for all
g\geq 0
and
n\geq 0
. Then there is a natural isomorphism of modular
\infty
-operads

\widehat{\mathrm{N}\mathcal{P}}\cong\mathrm{N}\widehat{\mathcal{P}}.

Proof.

Let
\mathrm{Mod}(\mathscr{G})_{\mathrm{fin}}
denote the full subcategory of
\mathrm{Mod}(\mathscr{G})
spanned by modular operads
\mathcal{P}
such that each groupoid
\mathcal{P}(g,n+1)
has finitely many objects. We show that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Mod}(\mathscr{G})_{\mathrm{fin}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Mod}_{\infty}(\mathscr{G})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Mod}(\widehat{\mathscr{G}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Mod}_{\infty}(\widehat{\mathscr{G}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{(-)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{N}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{(-)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{N}}

commutes up to natural isomorphism.

Going around the diagram counterclockwise, let
\widehat{\mathcal{P}}
be the modular operad in
\widehat{\mathscr{G}}
obtained from
\mathcal{P}
by entrywise profinite completion, as in Definition
4.1
. Its modular dendroidal nerve is the functor

\mathrm{N}\widehat{\mathcal{P}}:\mathscr{M}^{\mathrm{op}}\longrightarrow\widehat{\mathscr{G}}

defined, for each graph
G\in\mathscr{M}
, by

(\mathrm{N}\widehat{\mathcal{P}})_{G}=\prod_{v\in V_{G}}\widehat{\mathcal{P}}\bigl(\epsilon(v),|nb(v)|\bigr).

Going around the diagram in the other direction, profinite completion of the modular dendroidal object

\mathrm{N}\mathcal{P}:\mathscr{M}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathscr{G}
is defined entrywise. Hence, for each graph
G\in\mathscr{M}
,

(\widehat{\mathrm{N}\mathcal{P}})_{G}=\left(\prod_{v\in V_{G}}\mathcal{P}\bigl(\epsilon(v),|nb(v)|\bigr)\right)^{\wedge},

where
(-)^{\wedge}
denotes profinite completion. Since
G
has finitely many vertices and each groupoid
\mathcal{P}(g,n+1)
has finitely many objects, Proposition
2.6
identifies this groupoid naturally with

\prod_{v\in V_{G}}\widehat{\mathcal{P}}\bigl(\epsilon(v),|nb(v)|\bigr)=(\mathrm{N}\widehat{\mathcal{P}})_{G}.

These isomorphisms are natural in
G
, and therefore assemble to an isomorphism of modular dendroidal objects

\widehat{\mathrm{N}\mathcal{P}}\cong\mathrm{N}\widehat{\mathcal{P}}.

By Lemma
6.2
, the left-hand side is a modular
\infty
-operad in profinite groupoids, and the right-hand side is the nerve of a modular operad in profinite groupoids. Hence this is an isomorphism of modular
\infty
-operads.
∎

We will use the following elementary path-object notion of homotopy for modular operads in groupoids and profinite groupoids. Let
\mathscr{I}
denote the finite groupoid with objects
\{0,1\}
and a unique morphism between any two objects. If
\mathcal{Q}
is a modular operad in groupoids, or in profinite groupoids, we write
\mathcal{Q}^{\mathscr{I}}
for the modular operad defined entrywise by

(\mathcal{Q}^{\mathscr{I}})(g,n+1)=\mathcal{Q}(g,n+1)^{\mathscr{I}}.

The two objects of
\mathscr{I}
induce evaluation maps
d_{0},d_{1}\colon\mathcal{Q}^{\mathscr{I}}\to\mathcal{Q}
. Given morphisms of modular operads
f,g\colon\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{Q}
, a
homotopy
from
f
to
g
is a morphism of modular operads
H\colon\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{Q}^{\mathscr{I}}
such that
d_{0}H=f
and
d_{1}H=g
.

Because weak equivalences and fibrations of groupoids and profinite groupoids are defined entrywise,
\mathcal{Q}^{\mathscr{I}}
is an entrywise path object for
\mathcal{Q}
. Thus this agrees with the usual model-categorical right homotopy relation in the corresponding category of modular operads. The relation is compatible with composition. We write
\operatorname{HoAut}(\mathcal{P})
for the group of automorphisms of
\mathcal{P}
modulo this homotopy relation, and we write
\operatorname{Aut}_{0}(\mathcal{P})
for the group of object-fixing automorphisms.

Proposition 6.4
.

The map

\operatorname{Aut}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbf{S}})\to\operatorname{HoAut}(\widehat{\mathbf{S}})

is injective.

Before we prove Proposition
6.4
we require the following Lemma about
\widehat{\Gamma}_{1,1}\cong\widehat{\Gamma(S_{\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/contraction.pdf}}})}
. We use as a reference Figure
6
.

Lemma 6.5
.

Let
a
denote the curve in
S_{\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/contraction.pdf}}}
depicted in Figure
6
. Then we have the following relation between centralisers

C_{\widehat{\Gamma}_{1,1}}(D_{a})=\overline{C_{\Gamma_{1,1}}(D_{a})}=\overline{\langle D_{\partial},D_{a},(D_{a}D_{b}D_{a})^{2}\rangle}.

Proof.

The capping of the boundary by gluing a punctured disc induces a short exact sequence

1\to\langle D_{\partial}\rangle\to\Gamma_{1,1}\xrightarrow{\pi}\Gamma_{1}^{1}\to 1

where
\Gamma_{1}^{1}
denotes the mapping class group of the one-punctured torus.

As profinite completion is right-exact, this gives an exact sequence

\widehat{\langle D_{\partial}\rangle}\to\widehat{\Gamma}_{1,1}\xrightarrow{\pi}\widehat{\Gamma}_{1}^{1}\to 1.

Restricting to the normaliser
N_{\widehat{\Gamma}_{1,1}}(\overline{\langle D_{a}\rangle})
of
\overline{\langle D_{a}\rangle}
in
\widehat{\Gamma}_{1,1}
gives us the exact sequence

(6.1)

\displaystyle\widehat{\langle D_{\partial}\rangle}\to N_{\widehat{\Gamma}_{1,1}}(\overline{\langle D_{a}\rangle})\xrightarrow{\pi}\pi(N_{\widehat{\Gamma}_{1,1}}(\overline{\langle D_{a}\rangle}))\to 1.

It is clear that

(6.2)

\displaystyle\pi(N_{\widehat{\Gamma}_{1,1}}(\overline{\langle D_{a}\rangle}))\subset N_{\widehat{\Gamma}_{1}^{1}}(\overline{\langle\pi(D_{a})\rangle})

but we will show this is in fact an equality. As
\Gamma_{1}^{1}\cong SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
is virtually free, by
[
57
, Corollary 2.9]
we have that

N_{\widehat{\Gamma}_{1}^{1}}(\overline{\langle\pi(D_{a})\rangle})=\overline{N_{\Gamma_{1}^{1}}(\langle\pi(D_{a})\rangle)}.

We now describe the normaliser of
\langle\pi(D_{a})\rangle
in
\Gamma_{1}^{1}
. Note that
\pi(D_{a})
is simply the Dehn twist in the once-punctured torus along the curve corresponding to
a
, so we will abuse notation and denote it also by
D_{a}
.

The normaliser of
\langle D_{a}\rangle
in
\Gamma_{1}^{1}
consists of those mapping classes
f
such that
f\cdot D_{a}\cdot f^{-1}
is a power of
D_{a}
. However, by Lemma
2.32
, such a conjugation is simply the Dehn twist
D_{f(a)}
. Since a power
D_{a}^{k}
is only a Dehn twist if
k=1
, we conclude that any element in the normaliser of
D_{a}

fixes

a
, and hence
D_{a}
. In particular, this means that this normaliser is simply the centraliser of
D_{a}
.

N_{\Gamma_{1}^{1}}(\langle\pi(D_{a})\rangle)=C_{\Gamma_{1}^{1}}(D_{a})

As discussed above, this centraliser consists precisely of those mapping classes
f
that fix the curve
a
. This can be seen to be precisely the subgroup generated by
D_{a}
and
(D_{a}D_{b}D_{a})^{2}
, for instance by thinking of
f
as a mapping class on the surface obtained by cutting along
a
which is allowed to permute the two new boundary components corresponding to
a
.

Putting this into (
6.2
), this gives us

\pi(N_{\widehat{\Gamma}_{1,1}}(\overline{\langle D_{a}\rangle}))\subset\overline{\langle D_{a},(D_{a}D_{b}D_{a})^{2}\rangle}.

Moreover, both
D_{a}
and
(D_{a}D_{b}D_{a})^{2}
in
\Gamma_{1}^{1}
are images of the corresponding elements of
\Gamma_{1,1}
, and these elements clearly lie in

N_{\widehat{\Gamma}_{1,1}}(\overline{\langle D_{a}\rangle})
.
Hence

\langle D_{a},(D_{a}D_{b}D_{a})^{2}\rangle\subset\pi(N_{\widehat{\Gamma}_{1,1}}(\overline{\langle D_{a}\rangle})).

Since normalisers of closed subgroups are closed in profinite groups, the normaliser

N_{\widehat{\Gamma}_{1,1}}(\overline{\langle D_{a}\rangle})
is closed, hence compact. Therefore its image under
\pi
is closed and we obtain

\overline{\langle D_{a},(D_{a}D_{b}D_{a})^{2}\rangle}\subset\pi(N_{\widehat{\Gamma}_{1,1}}(\overline{\langle D_{a}\rangle})).

Together with the previous inclusion, this proves equality.

Putting this together with (
6.1
) gives us a right exact sequence

\displaystyle\widehat{\langle D_{\partial}\rangle}\to N_{\widehat{\Gamma}_{1,1}}(\overline{\langle D_{a}\rangle})\xrightarrow{\pi}\overline{\langle D_{a},(D_{a}D_{b}D_{a})^{2}\rangle}.

As this is right exact and the elements
D_{a},(D_{a}D_{b}D_{a})^{2}\in\Gamma_{1,1}
project to the corresponding elements in
\Gamma_{1}^{1}
, we get that
N_{\widehat{\Gamma}_{1,1}}(\overline{\langle D_{a}\rangle})
is generated by

D_{\partial},\quad D_{a},\quad(D_{a}D_{b}D_{a})^{2}.

Note that all these generators commute with
D_{a}
in
\Gamma_{1,1}
. In particular, this means that the entire normaliser of
\overline{\langle D_{a}\rangle}
is simply just the centraliser of
D_{a}
in
\widehat{\Gamma}_{1,1}
and we have the equalities

C_{\widehat{\Gamma}_{1,1}}(D_{a})=\overline{C_{\Gamma_{1,1}}(D_{a})}=\overline{\langle D_{\partial},D_{a},(D_{a}D_{b}D_{a})^{2}\rangle}.

∎

We are now ready to return to Proposition
6.4
:

Proof of Proposition
6.4
.

Suppose that
F:\widehat{\mathbf{S}}\to\widehat{\mathbf{S}}
is an object-fixing automorphism together with a natural transformation
H:F\Rightarrow\operatorname{id}_{\widehat{\mathbf{S}}}.
We will show that
F=\operatorname{id}_{\widehat{\mathbf{S}}}
.

We start by noting that the truncation
\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{0}}
induces a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Aut}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbf{S}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{HoAut}(\widehat{\mathbf{S}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Aut}_{0}(\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{0}}\widehat{\mathbf{S}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{HoAut}(\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{0}}\widehat{\mathbf{S}})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{0}}}

By Proposition
3.15
and
[
13
, Proposition 7.8]
, the bottom map is injective. Since
H:F\Rightarrow\operatorname{id}_{\widehat{\mathbf{S}}}
is a natural transformation, the induced map
\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{0}}F
is homotopic to
\operatorname{id}_{\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{0}}\widehat{\mathbf{S}}}
. Injectivity of the bottom map therefore implies

\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{0}}F=\operatorname{id}_{\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{0}}\widehat{\mathbf{S}}}.

Since we know
F
restricts to the identity in genus zero, by Theorem
3.17
, to prove that the automorphism
F
is the identity on
\widehat{\mathbf{S}}
, it suffices to show
F(\sigma)=\sigma
, where
\sigma
is the standard
S
-move seen as a morphism of
\widehat{\mathbf{S}}(1,1)
.

Recall that
\widehat{\mathbf{S}}(1,1)
has a single object
and morphism group
\widehat{\Gamma}_{1,1}
. Let
a
denote the curve in
S_{\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/contraction.pdf}}}
depicted in Figure
6
, and let
\sigma=D_{a}D_{b}D_{a}

be the standard
S
-move defined in Definition
2.37
.

The natural transformation
H:F\Rightarrow\operatorname{id}_{\widehat{\mathbf{S}}}
implies there is a morphism
H_{\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/contraction.pdf}}}
in
\widehat{\mathbf{S}}(1,1)
making the following squares commute

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(D_{a})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/contraction.pdf}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/contraction.pdf}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{a}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\sigma)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/contraction.pdf}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/contraction.pdf}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}

Since
D_{a}=\xi_{12}(\operatorname{id}_{P}\circ_{1}\tau)
, the mapping class
D_{a}
is obtained from genus-zero morphisms. Since
F
restricts to the identity in genus zero, we conclude that
F(D_{a})=D_{a}
.

In particular, then the left-hand commutative square implies that
H_{\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/contraction.pdf}}}
is in the centraliser of
D_{a}
in
\widehat{\Gamma}_{1,1}
.

By Lemma
6.5
, we get that
C_{\widehat{\Gamma}_{1,1}}(D_{a})
is generated by
D_{\partial}
,
(D_{a}D_{b}D_{a})^{2}
, and
D_{a},

which gives that

H_{\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/contraction.pdf}}}=(D_{a}D_{b}D_{a})^{2\chi}\cdot D_{\partial}^{\mu}\cdot D_{a}^{\nu}.\qquad\text{ for some }\chi,\mu,\nu\in\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}

and

F(\sigma)=((D_{a}D_{b}D_{a})^{2\chi}\cdot D_{\partial}^{\mu}\cdot D_{a}^{\nu})^{-1}\sigma((D_{a}D_{b}D_{a})^{2\chi}\cdot D_{\partial}^{\mu}\cdot D_{a}^{\nu})

As
\sigma=D_{a}D_{b}D_{a}
commutes with
(D_{a}D_{b}D_{a})^{2}
and
D_{\partial}
is in the centre of
\Gamma_{1,1}
, we get

F(\sigma)=D_{a}^{-\nu}\cdot\sigma\cdot D_{a}^{\nu}=D_{a}^{-\nu}\cdot(D_{a}D_{b}D_{a})\cdot D_{a}^{\nu}

As
F
must satisfy relation (
3.2
), using that
F
is the identity in genus zero and the above formula for
F(\sigma)
, we get (using the curves and notation in Figure
14
)

(6.3)

\displaystyle D_{\partial_{0}}^{\frac{1}{2}}D_{\partial_{1}}^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=(D_{a_{3}}^{-\nu}\cdot(D_{a_{3}}D_{a_{2}}D_{a_{3}})\cdot D_{a_{3}}^{\nu})\cdot D_{a_{3}}^{-1}D_{a_{1}}\cdot(D_{a_{3}}^{-\nu}\cdot(D_{a_{3}}D_{a_{2}}D_{a_{3}})\cdot D_{a_{3}}^{\nu})

(6.4)

\displaystyle=D_{a_{3}}^{-\nu}\cdot((D_{a_{3}}D_{a_{2}}D_{a_{3}})\cdot D_{a_{3}}^{-1}D_{a_{1}}\cdot(D_{a_{3}}D_{a_{2}}D_{a_{3}}))\cdot D_{a_{3}}^{\nu}

(6.5)

\displaystyle=D_{a_{3}}^{-\nu}\cdot(D_{a_{2}}D_{a_{3}}D_{a_{1}}D_{a_{2}}D_{a_{3}}D_{a_{2}})\cdot D_{a_{3}}^{\nu}.

Now recall that
D_{\partial_{0}}^{\frac{1}{2}}D_{\partial_{1}}^{\frac{1}{2}}=(D_{a_{2}}D_{a_{3}}D_{a_{1}}D_{a_{2}}D_{a_{3}}D_{a_{2}})
in
\Gamma_{1,1}
and that, by the Lemma
2.32
, we have

(D_{\partial_{0}}^{\frac{1}{2}}D_{\partial_{1}}^{\frac{1}{2}})D_{a_{3}}(D_{\partial_{0}}^{\frac{1}{2}}D_{\partial_{1}}^{\frac{1}{2}})^{-1}=D_{a_{1}}.

Putting these in equation (
6.5
), we get

\displaystyle D_{a_{2}}D_{a_{3}}D_{a_{1}}D_{a_{2}}D_{a_{3}}D_{a_{2}}

\displaystyle=D_{a_{3}}^{-\nu}D_{a_{1}}^{\nu}\cdot(D_{a_{2}}D_{a_{3}}D_{a_{1}}D_{a_{2}}D_{a_{3}}D_{a_{2}}),

giving that

1=D_{a_{3}}^{-\nu}D_{a_{1}}^{\nu}.

Since
a_{1}
and
a_{3}
are distinct non-intersecting curves, this forces
\nu=0
. Therefore
F(\sigma)=\sigma
, as required.
∎

We next compare object-fixing automorphisms of
\widehat{\mathbf{S}}
with homotopy automorphisms of the modular
\infty
-operad
\widehat{\mathrm{N}\mathbf{S}}
. Following
[
22
, 1.2]
, we write
\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Aut}(\mathcal{P})\subseteq\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P})
for the union of those connected components whose images in
\pi_{0}\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P})
are invertible.

Proposition 6.6
.

There is an isomorphism of groups

\operatorname{Aut}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbf{S}})\cong\pi_{0}\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Aut}_{\mathrm{Mod}_{\infty}(\widehat{\mathscr{G}})}(\widehat{\mathrm{N}\mathbf{S}}).

Proof.

We first compare the homotopy automorphisms of
\widehat{\mathrm{N}\mathbf{S}}
with the homotopy automorphisms of the strict profinite modular operad
\widehat{\mathbf{S}}
. Since profinite completion is applied entrywise to modular dendroidal objects, the profinite completion adjunction gives a weak equivalence

\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}_{\mathrm{Mod}_{\infty}(\widehat{\mathscr{G}})}(\widehat{\mathrm{N}\mathbf{S}},\widehat{\mathrm{N}\mathbf{S}})\simeq\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}_{\mathrm{Mod}_{\infty}(\mathscr{G})}(\mathrm{N}\mathbf{S},|\widehat{\mathrm{N}\mathbf{S}}|).

By Lemma
6.3
,
\widehat{\mathrm{N}\mathbf{S}}\cong\mathrm{N}\widehat{\mathbf{S}}
. Since the limit functor commutes with the modular dendroidal nerve,
|\widehat{\mathrm{N}\mathbf{S}}|\cong\mathrm{N}|\widehat{\mathbf{S}}|
. Hence the right-hand side above is weakly equivalent to
\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}_{\mathrm{Mod}_{\infty}(\mathscr{G})}(\mathrm{N}\mathbf{S},\mathrm{N}|\widehat{\mathbf{S}}|).
By Theorem
B.2
, we have a weak equivalence
\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}_{\mathrm{Mod}_{\infty}(\mathscr{G})}(\mathrm{N}\mathbf{S},\mathrm{N}|\widehat{\mathbf{S}}|)\simeq\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}_{\mathrm{Mod}(\mathscr{G})}(\mathbf{S},|\widehat{\mathbf{S}}|)
.

Since
\mathbf{S}
is cofibrant by Proposition
A.6
, and every modular operad in groupoids is fibrant, Theorem
2.3
identifies

\pi_{0}\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}_{\mathrm{Mod}(\mathscr{G})}(\mathbf{S},|\widehat{\mathbf{S}}|)\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{Ho}(\mathrm{Mod}(\mathscr{G}))}(\mathbf{S},|\widehat{\mathbf{S}}|).

Moreover, since entrywise profinite completion is the left adjoint in the Quillen adjunction
(-)^{\wedge}:\mathrm{Mod}(\mathscr{G})\rightleftarrows\mathrm{Mod}(\widehat{\mathscr{G}}):|-|
, the associated derived adjunction identifies the right-hand side with
\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{Ho}(\mathrm{Mod}(\widehat{\mathscr{G}}))}(\widehat{\mathbf{S}},\widehat{\mathbf{S}})
. It follows that

\pi_{0}\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}_{\mathrm{Mod}_{\infty}(\widehat{\mathscr{G}})}(\widehat{\mathrm{N}\mathbf{S}},\widehat{\mathrm{N}\mathbf{S}})\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{Ho}(\mathrm{Mod}(\widehat{\mathscr{G}}))}(\widehat{\mathbf{S}},\widehat{\mathbf{S}}).

Under the identifications above, composition is preserved. Hence the invertible components of

\pi_{0}\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}_{\mathrm{Mod}_{\infty}(\widehat{\mathscr{G}})}(\widehat{\mathrm{N}\mathbf{S}},\widehat{\mathrm{N}\mathbf{S}})

correspond to the automorphisms of
\widehat{\mathbf{S}}
in the homotopy category. Therefore

\pi_{0}\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Aut}_{\mathrm{Mod}_{\infty}(\widehat{\mathscr{G}})}(\widehat{\mathrm{N}\mathbf{S}})\cong\operatorname{HoAut}(\widehat{\mathbf{S}}).

It remains to show that the map
\operatorname{Aut}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbf{S}})\to\operatorname{HoAut}(\widehat{\mathbf{S}})
is an isomorphism. Injectivity was proven in Proposition
6.4
.

To prove this map is surjective, it suffices to show that every automorphism of
\widehat{\mathbf{S}}
is homotopic to one inducing the identity on objects. This can be constructed exactly as in
[
42
, Theorem 7.8]
: the modular operad
\operatorname{ob}\widehat{\mathbf{S}}
is freely generated by the object
P
in
\operatorname{ob}\widehat{\mathbf{S}}(0,3)
. Hence the action of an automorphism
F:\widehat{\mathbf{S}}\to\widehat{\mathbf{S}}
on objects is completely determined by the image of
P
. If
F(P)=P
, then
F
already fixes all objects. Otherwise, we have
F(P)=(12)^{*}P
. In this case we construct another automorphism
F^{\prime}:\widehat{\mathbf{S}}\to\widehat{\mathbf{S}}
which is the identity on objects together with a homotopy
H:F^{\prime}\Rightarrow F
, which in this case is simply a natural transformation.

To construct
F^{\prime}
, we pick a morphism
w:P\to(12)^{*}P
in
\widehat{\mathbf{S}}(0,3)
(for instance
\beta
). Define
H_{P}:P\to(12)^{*}P
to be this chosen morphism
w
. This induces a unique map of modular operads
H:\operatorname{ob}\widehat{\mathbf{S}}\to\operatorname{ob}\widehat{\mathbf{S}}
and, in particular maps
H_{G}:G\to F(G)
for any object
G
in
\widehat{\mathbf{S}}
. Now if
\phi:G\to G^{\prime}
is any morphism in
\widehat{\mathbf{S}}
, we define

F^{\prime}(\phi)=H_{G^{\prime}}\cdot\phi\cdot H_{G}^{-1}.

It is simple to see that
F^{\prime}
indeed preserves the modular operadic compositions, since
H
and
F
do, and that
H_{G}
assemble into a natural transformation
F^{\prime}\Rightarrow F
. It follows that we have an isomorphism
\operatorname{Aut}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbf{S}})\cong\operatorname{HoAut}(\widehat{\mathbf{S}})
.

∎

Theorem
4.8
gives an object-fixing action of the monoid
\underline{\widehat{\mathsf{\Gamma}}}
on
\widehat{\mathbf{S}}
. Restricting to invertible elements gives an action of the group
\widehat{\mathsf{\Gamma}}
by object-fixing automorphisms of
\widehat{\mathbf{S}}
. Proposition
6.6
identifies these strict object-fixing automorphisms with homotopy automorphisms of the profinite modular
\infty
-operad
\widehat{\mathrm{N}\mathbf{S}}
.

Corollary 6.7
.

The action of
\widehat{\mathsf{\Gamma}}
on
\widehat{\mathbf{S}}
induces a faithful action of
\widehat{\mathsf{\Gamma}}
on
\widehat{\mathrm{N}\mathbf{S}}
by homotopy automorphisms. Equivalently, there is an injective homomorphism

\widehat{\mathsf{\Gamma}}\longrightarrow\pi_{0}\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Aut}_{\mathrm{Mod}_{\infty}(\widehat{\mathscr{G}})}(\widehat{\mathrm{N}\mathbf{S}}).

Proof.

By Theorem
4.8
, the group
\widehat{\mathsf{\Gamma}}
acts on
\widehat{\mathbf{S}}
by object-fixing automorphisms. Proposition
6.6
identifies

\operatorname{Aut}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbf{S}})\cong\pi_{0}\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Aut}_{\mathrm{Mod}_{\infty}(\widehat{\mathscr{G}})}(\widehat{\mathrm{N}\mathbf{S}}).

Composing with the action map
\widehat{\mathsf{\Gamma}}\longrightarrow\operatorname{Aut}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbf{S}})
with this identification gives the desired homomorphism.

Restricting the action above to the genus zero truncation recovers an action of the Grothendieck–Teichmüller group
\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}
through homotopy automorphisms, i.e.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathsf{\Gamma}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi_{0}\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Aut}_{\mathrm{Mod}_{\infty}(\widehat{\mathscr{G}})}(\widehat{\mathrm{N}\mathbf{S}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi_{0}\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Aut}_{\mathrm{Mod}_{\infty}(\widehat{\mathscr{G}})}(\widehat{\mathrm{N}\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{0}}{\mathbf{S}}}).}}

By
[
13
, Theorem 8.4]
, the corresponding action of
\widehat{\mathsf{GT}}
on the profinite genus zero surface operad is faithful, and therefore so is the action of
\widehat{\mathsf{\Gamma}}
.
∎

6.3.
Genus-one truncations of modular dendroidal spaces

Profinite completion of spaces does not generally preserve products. This creates a difficulty for modular
\infty
-operads in profinite spaces, because the Segal condition is formulated using products indexed by the vertices of a graph. In
[
13
, Proposition 3.9]
, Boavida, Horel and the second author gave a sufficient criterion for profinite completion to commute with such products: if
X
and
Y
are connected spaces whose homotopy groups are good in the sense of Definition
6.9
, then the natural map

\widehat{X\times Y}\longrightarrow\widehat{X}\times\widehat{Y}

is a weak equivalence of profinite spaces.

To extend the
\widehat{\mathsf{\Gamma}}
-action on
\widehat{\mathbf{S}}
to an action in the homotopy category of profinite spaces, we need to establish compatibility between profinite completion and the products appearing in the Segal condition. The available product comparison requires goodness of the relevant homotopy groups. Since the mapping class groups are known to be good only in genus at most one, we first work with genus-one truncated modular dendroidal objects.

We begin by introducing genus truncations for modular dendroidal objects. Let
\mathscr{M}_{\leq g}\subseteq\mathscr{M}
denote the full subcategory spanned by genus-graded graphs of total genus at most
g
.

Lemma 6.8
.

The full subcategory
\mathscr{M}_{\leq g}
is a sieve on
\mathscr{M}
. That is, if
\phi\colon G\to H
is a morphism in
\mathscr{M}
and
H\in\mathscr{M}_{\leq g}
, then
G\in\mathscr{M}_{\leq g}
.

Proof.

We use the description of graphical maps by graph substitution from
[
34
, Theorem 2.7]
. A morphism
\phi\colon G\to H
factors through a substituted graph
G\{K_{v}\}_{v\in V_{G}}
, where each graph
K_{v}
has total genus equal to the genus assigned to the vertex
v
of
G
, and the remaining map preserves genus.

The total genus of the substituted graph is

\beta_{1}(G)+\sum_{v\in V_{G}}g(K_{v})=\beta_{1}(G)+\sum_{v\in V_{G}}\epsilon_{G}(v),

which is precisely the total genus of
G
. Since the map
G\{K_{v}\}_{v\in V_{G}}\to H
preserves genus, the total genus of
G
agrees with the total genus of its image in
H
, and in particular is at most the total genus of
H
. Thus, if
H
has total genus at most
g
, then so does
G
.
∎

Let
i_{g}\colon\mathscr{M}_{\leq g}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{M}
denote the inclusion. Restriction along
i_{g}
defines a functor

i_{g}^{*}\colon\mathrm{dMod}(\mathscr{E})\longrightarrow\mathrm{dMod}_{\leq g}(\mathscr{E}).

Here, we recall that
\mathrm{dMod}(\mathscr{E})=\operatorname{Fun}(\mathscr{M}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{E})
denotes the category of modular dendroidal objects in
\mathscr{E}
, and
\mathrm{dMod}_{\leq g}(\mathscr{E})=\operatorname{Fun}(\mathscr{M}_{\leq g}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathscr{E})
denotes the category of genus-
g
truncated modular dendroidal objects in
\mathscr{E}
. We call
i_{g}^{*}X
the genus-
g
truncation of
X
and write it as
\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{g}}X
.

We now turn to the comparison between profinite completion of groupoids and profinite completion of their classifying spaces. There are two natural ways to pass from a groupoid to a profinite space: one can first take the classifying space and then profinitely complete, or one can first profinitely complete the groupoid and then take its classifying space. These two constructions are related by a natural comparison map, represented by the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathscr{G}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathscr{S}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{(-)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{(-)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{B}}

At a groupoid
\mathscr{E}
, this comparison is the canonical map
\widehat{\mathrm{B}\mathscr{E}}\to\mathrm{B}\widehat{\mathscr{E}}
.

For a general groupoid
\mathscr{E}
, this map need not be a weak equivalence. A sufficient condition is goodness in the sense of Serre.

Definition 6.9
.

A discrete group
\mathsf{G}
is
good
if, for every finite discrete
\mathsf{G}
-module
\mathsf{M}
, the natural map
H^{*}_{\mathrm{cts}}(\widehat{\mathsf{G}},\mathsf{M})\to H^{*}(\mathsf{G},\mathsf{M})
is an isomorphism. A groupoid with finitely many objects is called good if the automorphism group of each object is good.

By
[
42
, Proposition 5.9]
, if
\mathscr{E}
is a good groupoid with finitely many objects, then the comparison map
\widehat{\mathrm{B}\mathscr{E}}\to\mathrm{B}\widehat{\mathscr{E}}
is a weak equivalence of profinite spaces.

Lemma 6.10
.

For each
g\leq 1
and
n\geq 0
satisfying
2g+n\geq 1
, the canonical map

(\mathrm{B}\mathbf{S}(g,n+1))^{\wedge}\longrightarrow\mathrm{B}(\widehat{\mathbf{S}}(g,n+1))

is a weak equivalence of profinite spaces.

Proof.

The groupoid
\mathbf{S}(g,n+1)
has finitely many objects. For every object
A\in\operatorname{ob}(\mathbf{S}(g,n+1))
, the automorphism group
\operatorname{Aut}_{\mathbf{S}(g,n+1)}(A)
is isomorphic to the mapping class group
\Gamma_{g,n+1}
. These groups are good for
g\leq 1
; see
[
55
, p. 94]
. Hence
\mathbf{S}(g,n+1)
is a good groupoid and the result therefore follows from
[
42
, Proposition 5.9]
.
∎

Proposition 6.11
.

There is an equivalence of genus-one truncated modular
\infty
-operads in profinite spaces

(\mathrm{B}\mathrm{N}(\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{1}}\mathbf{S}))^{\wedge}\simeq\mathrm{B}\mathrm{N}(\widehat{\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{1}}\mathbf{S}}).

Proof.

Both sides are functors
\mathscr{M}_{\leq 1}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\widehat{\mathtt{S}}
. We first verify that both satisfy the truncated Segal condition.

Let
G\in\mathscr{M}_{\leq 1}
. Since
\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{1}}\mathbf{S}
is a truncated modular operad, its modular dendroidal nerve satisfies the strict Segal formula

(\mathrm{N}\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{1}}\mathbf{S})_{G}\cong\prod_{v\in V_{G}}(\mathrm{N}\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{1}}\mathbf{S})_{C_{(\epsilon_{G}(v),|nb_{G}(v)|)}}\cong\prod_{v\in V_{G}}\mathbf{S}(\epsilon_{G}(v),|nb_{G}(v)|),

where the second isomorphism follows from the definition of the modular dendroidal nerve. Since the classifying space functor preserves finite products, we have

(\mathrm{B}\mathrm{N}\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{1}}\mathbf{S})_{G}\cong\prod_{v\in V_{G}}\mathrm{B}\mathbf{S}(\epsilon_{G}(v),|nb_{G}(v)|).

Thus the Segal map for
(\mathrm{B}\mathrm{N}(\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{1}}\mathbf{S}))^{\wedge}
at
G
is the natural map

\left(\prod_{v\in V_{G}}\mathrm{B}\mathbf{S}(\epsilon_{G}(v),|nb_{G}(v)|)\right)^{\wedge}\longrightarrow\prod_{v\in V_{G}}\bigl(\mathrm{B}\mathbf{S}(\epsilon_{G}(v),|nb_{G}(v)|)\bigr)^{\wedge}.

Since
G
has total genus at most one, every vertex has genus at most one. For each
v\in V_{G}
, the groupoid
\mathbf{S}(\epsilon_{G}(v),|nb_{G}(v)|)
is connected, and the automorphism group of each object is isomorphic to
\Gamma_{\epsilon_{G}(v),|nb_{G}(v)|}
and these mapping class groups are good under our assumptions. Hence the spaces
\mathrm{B}\mathbf{S}(\epsilon_{G}(v),|nb_{G}(v)|)
satisfy the hypotheses of
[
13
, Proposition 3.9]
. Applying that product comparison iteratively, the displayed Segal map is a weak equivalence.

For the right-hand side, the nerve of the truncated profinite modular operad
\widehat{\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{1}}\mathbf{S}}
satisfies the Segal condition,

(\mathrm{N}\widehat{\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{1}}\mathbf{S}})_{G}\cong\prod_{v\in V_{G}}(\mathrm{N}\widehat{\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{1}}\mathbf{S}})_{C_{(\epsilon_{G}(v),|nb_{G}(v)|)}}\cong\prod_{v\in V_{G}}\widehat{\mathbf{S}}(\epsilon_{G}(v),|nb_{G}(v)|).

Again noting that the classifying space functor preserves this finite products, we have that
\mathrm{B}\mathrm{N}(\widehat{\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{1}}\mathbf{S}})
also satisfies the truncated Segal condition.

On a corolla
C_{(g,n+1)}
with
g\leq 1
, the comparison is the canonical map

(\mathrm{B}(\mathbf{S}(g,n+1)))^{\wedge}\to\mathrm{B}(\widehat{\mathbf{S}}(g,n+1))
, which is a weak equivalence by Lemma
6.10
. The comparison therefore extends to an equivalence on arbitrary graphs by the truncated Segal condition. Indeed, for every
G\in\mathscr{M}_{\leq 1}
, it fits into a commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces((\mathrm{B}\mathrm{N}(\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{1}}\mathbf{S}))^{\wedge})_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathrm{B}\mathrm{N}(\widehat{\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{1}}\mathbf{S}}))_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\prod_{v\in V_{G}}((\mathrm{B}\mathrm{N}(\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{1}}\mathbf{S}))^{\wedge})_{C_{(\epsilon_{G}(v),|nb_{G}(v)|)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\prod_{v\in V_{G}}(\mathrm{B}\mathrm{N}(\widehat{\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{1}}\mathbf{S}}))_{C_{(\epsilon_{G}(v),|nb_{G}(v)|)}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

The vertical maps are the Segal equivalences established above, and the bottom horizontal map is the product of the comparison maps on the corresponding corollas. Hence the bottom horizontal map is a weak equivalence, and thus so is the top horizontal map.

It follows that
(\mathrm{B}\mathrm{N}(\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{1}}\mathbf{S}))^{\wedge}\to\mathrm{B}\mathrm{N}(\widehat{\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{1}}\mathbf{S}})
is an equivalence of genus-one truncated modular
\infty
-operads in profinite spaces.
∎

The preceding comparison allows us to transport the genus-one part of the
\underline{\widehat{\mathsf{\Gamma}}}
-action from profinite groupoids to profinite spaces.

Proposition 6.12
.

The action of
\widehat{\mathsf{\Gamma}}
on
\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{1}}\widehat{\mathbf{S}}
induces a faithful action on
(\mathrm{B}\mathrm{N}(\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{1}}\mathbf{S}))^{\wedge}
by automorphisms in the homotopy category of genus-one truncated modular
\infty
-operads in profinite spaces. More precisely, there is an injective homomorphism

\widehat{\mathsf{\Gamma}}\longrightarrow\pi_{0}\mathbb{\operatorname{Aut}}_{\mathrm{dMod}_{\leq 1}(\widehat{\mathtt{S}})}\bigl(\widehat{\mathrm{B}\mathrm{N}(\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{1}}\mathbf{S})}\bigr).

Proof.

By Theorem
4.8
, the group
\widehat{\mathsf{\Gamma}}
acts on
\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{1}}\widehat{\mathbf{S}}
by object-fixing endomorphisms. The genus-one restriction of Proposition
6.6
gives a homomorphism

(6.6)

{\widehat{\mathsf{\Gamma}}}\longrightarrow\pi_{0}\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Aut}_{\mathrm{dMod}_{\leq 1}(\widehat{\mathscr{G}})}\bigl(\widehat{\mathrm{N}(\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{1}}\mathbf{S})}\bigr).

By Lemma
6.3
, we identify
\widehat{\mathrm{N}(\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{1}}\mathbf{S})}
with
\mathrm{N}(\widehat{\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{1}}\mathbf{S}})
.

Applying the classifying space functor entrywise, and using the homotopical full faithfulness of
\mathrm{B}
recalled in Section
2.1
, the homomorphism (
6.6
) induces

{\widehat{\mathsf{\Gamma}}}\longrightarrow\pi_{0}\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Aut}_{\mathrm{dMod}_{\leq 1}(\widehat{\mathtt{S}})}\bigl(\mathrm{B}\mathrm{N}(\widehat{\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{1}}\mathbf{S}})\bigr).

The comparison map of Proposition
6.11
identifies this target with

\pi_{0}\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Aut}_{\mathrm{dMod}_{\leq 1}(\widehat{\mathtt{S}})}\bigl(\widehat{\mathrm{B}\mathrm{N}(\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{1}}\mathbf{S})}\bigr)
,
giving the stated homomorphism.
∎

6.4.
Étale homotopy types and the profinite-space model

We now show that the entrywise profinite completion of
\mathrm{B}\mathrm{N}\mathbf{S}
assembles into a modular
\infty
-operad in profinite spaces. This subsection addresses a different issue from the genus-one comparison above: here we need to verify that profinite completion still satisfies the Segal conditions. Since profinite completion of spaces does not generally preserve products, this requires a separate product-comparison argument.

We write
\mathtt{S}
for the
\infty
-category of spaces, and let
\mathtt{S}_{\pi}\subset\mathtt{S}
denote the full subcategory of
\pi
-finite spaces. We write
\widehat{\mathtt{S}}:=\operatorname{Pro}(\mathtt{S}_{\pi})
for the
\infty
-category of profinite spaces, and

\widehat{(-)}\colon\operatorname{Pro}(\mathtt{S})\to\widehat{\mathtt{S}}
for profinite completion.

Let
\mathtt{S}_{<\infty}\subset\mathtt{S}
denote the full subcategory of truncated spaces. Following
[
37
, Notation 2.11]
, let
\mathcal{H}\subset\operatorname{Pro}(\mathtt{S}_{<\infty})
denote the smallest full subcategory containing

\widehat{\mathtt{S}}
and closed under geometric realizations, retracts, and cofiltered limits. We call a prospace
X\in\operatorname{Pro}(\mathtt{S})

admissible
if its protruncation
\tau_{<\infty}X
lies in
\mathcal{H}
.

Proposition 6.13
.

Let
X_{1},\ldots,X_{r}
be admissible prospaces. Then the natural map

\left(\prod_{i=1}^{r}X_{i}\right)^{\wedge}\longrightarrow\prod_{i=1}^{r}\widehat{X_{i}}

is an equivalence in profinite spaces.

Proof.

Profinite completion factors through protruncation, and protruncation preserves finite products. Thus it suffices to prove the corresponding statement for objects of
\mathcal{H}\subset\operatorname{Pro}(\mathtt{S}_{<\infty})
. This is the all-primes finite-product case of
[
37
, Theorem 2.13]
, applied iteratively.
∎

For a quasi-compact and quasi-separated (qcqs) scheme
X
, the protruncated étale homotopy type
\Pi^{\acute{e}t}_{<\infty}(X):=\tau_{<\infty}\Pi^{\acute{e}t}_{\infty}(X)
admits a
\operatorname{Pro}(\mathtt{S}_{\pi})
-resolution. Equivalently,
\Pi^{\acute{e}t}_{<\infty}(X)
belongs to
\mathcal{H}
. Thus
\Pi^{\acute{e}t}_{\infty}(X)
is admissible. Haine records the corresponding product comparison for two qcqs schemes in
[
37
, Example 2.17]
; the finite-product version follows from Proposition
6.13
.

Corollary 6.14
.

Let
X_{1},\ldots,X_{r}
be quasi-compact and quasi-separated schemes. Then the natural map

\left(\prod_{i=1}^{r}\Pi^{\acute{e}t}_{\infty}(X_{i})\right)^{\wedge}\longrightarrow\prod_{i=1}^{r}\widehat{\Pi^{\acute{e}t}_{\infty}(X_{i})}

is an equivalence in profinite spaces.

As in Example
2.7
, the profinite étale homotopy type of the moduli stack
\mathcal{M}_{g}^{n}
of smooth genus
g
curves with
n
ordered marked points identifies with the classifying space of the profinite completed marked-point mapping class group:

\Pi^{\acute{e}t}_{\infty}(\mathcal{M}_{g}^{n})\simeq\widehat{\mathrm{B}\Gamma_{g}^{n}}.

Our modular operad, however, involves the mapping class groups
\Gamma_{g,n}
of surfaces with
n
boundary components. To pass from marked points to boundary components, we add a non-zero tangent vector at each marked point.

Let
\mathcal{M}_{g,n}^{\tan}
denote the stack of smooth genus
g
curves with
n
ordered marked points equipped with non-zero tangent vectors at the marked points. Equivalently,
\mathcal{M}_{g,n}^{\tan}
is the
(\mathbb{G}_{m})^{n}
-torsor over
\mathcal{M}_{g}^{n}
obtained by deleting the zero sections from the cotangent-line bundles. Topologically, this replaces
\Gamma_{g}^{n}
by the central extension

1\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{n}\longrightarrow\Gamma_{g,n}\longrightarrow\Gamma_{g}^{n}\longrightarrow 1,

where the central subgroup is generated by the boundary Dehn twists.

The stack
\mathcal{M}_{g,n}^{\tan}
is an algebraic stack of finite type over the base field. Since it is not generally a scheme, we cannot apply Haine’s scheme-level product comparison directly. Instead, we first verify that its étale homotopy type is admissible in the sense above.

Lemma 6.15
.

For every pair
(g,n)
, the prospace

\Pi_{\infty}^{\acute{e}t}(\mathcal{M}_{g,n}^{\tan})

is admissible.

Proof.

The stack
\mathcal{M}_{g,n}^{\tan}
is a quasi-compact algebraic stack of finite type over the base field. Since it is quasi-compact, we can choose a smooth surjective atlas
U\to\mathcal{M}_{g,n}^{\tan}
with
U
an affine scheme
[
60
,
04YC
]
. We may choose
U
to be of finite type over the base field, hence quasi-compact and quasi-separated. Let
U_{\bullet}
be the Cech nerve of this atlas.

Since smooth morphisms admit étale local sections, the atlas
U\to\mathcal{M}_{g,n}^{\tan}
is an effective epimorphism for the étale stack topology. Thus
\mathcal{M}_{g,n}^{\tan}
is the geometric realization of the simplicial stack
U_{\bullet}
. By
[
17
, Lemma 2.26]
, the assignment
X\mapsto\operatorname{Sh}_{\infty}(X_{\acute{e}t})
extends from affine schemes to higher stacks as a colimit-preserving functor to
\infty
-topoi. Since the shape functor preserves colimits, and since
\Pi_{\infty}^{\acute{e}t}
is defined as the shape of the small étale
\infty
-topos, we obtain

\Pi_{\infty}^{\acute{e}t}(\mathcal{M}_{g,n}^{\tan})\simeq\left|\,\Pi_{\infty}^{\acute{e}t}(U_{\bullet})\,\right|

in
\operatorname{Pro}(\mathtt{S})
.

It remains to note that the terms
U_{k}
are quasi-compact and quasi-separated schemes. The diagonal of
\mathcal{M}_{g,n}^{\tan}
is representable by algebraic spaces and locally of finite type
[
60
,
04XS
]
. In this moduli problem, the diagonal is in fact represented by the Isom scheme between pointed smooth curves equipped with non-zero tangent vectors. Hence the iterated fibre products

U_{k}=U\times_{\mathcal{M}_{g,n}^{\tan}}\cdots\times_{\mathcal{M}_{g,n}^{\tan}}U

are schemes. Since
U
is of finite type over the base field and the relevant Isom schemes are of finite type, each
U_{k}
is of finite type over the base field. In particular, each
U_{k}
is quasi-compact and quasi-separated.

For each
k
, the scheme
U_{k}
is qcqs, so its étale
\infty
-topos is spectral. Hence
\tau_{<\infty}\Pi_{\infty}^{\acute{e}t}(U_{k})
admits a natural
\operatorname{Pro}(\mathtt{S}_{\pi})
-resolution by
[
37
, Example A.9]
; equivalently,
\Pi_{\infty}^{\acute{e}t}(U_{k})
is admissible in the sense above. Since protruncation preserves geometric realizations, we have

\tau_{<\infty}\Pi_{\infty}^{\acute{e}t}(\mathcal{M}_{g,n}^{\tan})\simeq\left|\,\tau_{<\infty}\Pi_{\infty}^{\acute{e}t}(U_{\bullet})\,\right|.

The right-hand side lies in
\mathcal{H}
, because
\mathcal{H}
is closed under geometric realizations. Therefore
\Pi_{\infty}^{\acute{e}t}(\mathcal{M}_{g,n}^{\tan})
is admissible.
∎

Now that admissibility is established, we can compare the profinite étale homotopy types of these stacks with the profinite completed classifying spaces of the corresponding mapping class groups. This comparison is the topological input that connects the algebro-geometric side of the construction to the modular operadic one.

Lemma 6.16
.

For every
g\geq 0
and
n\geq 0
satisfying
2g+n\geq 1
, there is an equivalence in profinite spaces

\widehat{\Pi_{\infty}^{\acute{e}t}(\mathcal{M}_{g,n+1}^{\tan})}\simeq\widehat{\mathrm{B}\Gamma}_{g,n+1}.

Proof.

By the comparison theorem for algebraic stacks locally of finite type over
\mathbb{C}
, the profinite étale homotopy type of
\mathcal{M}_{g,n+1}^{\tan}
agrees with the profinite completion of the homotopy type of its associated topological stack. The topological stack is presented by the Teichmüller space of genus
g
surfaces with
n+1
tangent directions modulo the mapping class group
\Gamma_{g,n+1}
. Since this Teichmüller space is contractible, the weak homotopy type of the topological stack is
\mathrm{B}\Gamma_{g,n+1}
. Hence

\widehat{\Pi_{\infty}^{\acute{e}t}(\mathcal{M}_{g,n+1}^{\tan})}\simeq\widehat{\mathrm{B}\Gamma}_{g,n+1}.

∎

With the above comparison in hand, we can show that profinite completion of classifying spaces of mapping class groups behaves well on the products that appear in the Segal maps.

Proposition 6.17
.

Let
g_{1},\ldots,g_{r}\geq 0
and
n_{1},\ldots,n_{r}\geq 0
. Then the natural map

\left(\prod_{i=1}^{r}\mathrm{B}\Gamma_{g_{i},n_{i}+1}\right)^{\wedge}\longrightarrow\prod_{i=1}^{r}(\mathrm{B}\Gamma_{g_{i},n_{i}+1})^{\wedge}

is an equivalence in profinite spaces.

Proof.

By Lemma
6.15
, each prospace

\Pi_{\infty}^{\acute{e}t}(\mathcal{M}_{g_{i},n_{i}+1}^{\tan})
is admissible. Proposition
6.13
therefore gives an equivalence

\left(\prod_{i=1}^{r}\Pi_{\infty}^{\acute{e}t}(\mathcal{M}_{g_{i},n_{i}+1}^{\tan})\right)^{\wedge}\simeq\prod_{i=1}^{r}\widehat{\Pi_{\infty}^{\acute{e}t}(\mathcal{M}_{g_{i},n_{i}+1}^{\tan})}.

Using Lemma
6.16
on each factor identifies the right-hand side with

\prod_{i=1}^{r}(\mathrm{B}\Gamma_{g_{i},n_{i}+1})^{\wedge}.

The left-hand side is identified with

\left(\prod_{i=1}^{r}\mathrm{B}\Gamma_{g_{i},n_{i}+1}\right)^{\wedge}

by applying the same comparison to the product of the corresponding tangent-direction stacks. This gives the desired product comparison.
∎

This product comparison is exactly what is needed to verify the Segal condition for the profinite completion of the modular dendroidal nerve of
\mathbf{S}
.

Lemma 6.18
.

The composite

\mathscr{M}^{\mathrm{op}}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{N}\mathbf{S}}\mathscr{G}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{B}}\mathscr{S}\xrightarrow{\widehat{(-)}}\widehat{\mathtt{S}}

defines a reduced modular
\infty
-operad in profinite spaces.

Proof.

Let
G
be a graph with vertices
v_{1},\ldots,v_{r}
, and write

\epsilon(v_{i})=g_{i}
and
|nb(v_{i})|=n_{i}+1
. Since
\mathrm{B}
preserves finite products and weak equivalences, the Segal equivalence for the modular dendroidal nerve gives

(\mathrm{B}\mathrm{N}\mathbf{S})_{G}\simeq\prod_{i=1}^{r}\mathrm{B}\mathbf{S}(g_{i},n_{i}+1).

The groupoid
\mathbf{S}(g_{i},n_{i}+1)
is connected with automorphism group
\Gamma_{g_{i},n_{i}+1}
, so

\mathrm{B}\mathbf{S}(g_{i},n_{i}+1)\simeq\mathrm{B}\Gamma_{g_{i},n_{i}+1}
. Proposition
6.17
then identifies the profinite completion of the product above with the product of the completed corolla values. Thus the Segal map

(\widehat{\mathrm{B}\mathrm{N}\mathbf{S}})_{G}\longrightarrow\prod_{i=1}^{r}(\widehat{\mathrm{B}\mathrm{N}\mathbf{S}})_{C_{(g_{i},n_{i}+1)}}

is a weak equivalence for every graph
G
. The exceptional edge is still sent to the point. Hence
\widehat{\mathrm{B}\mathrm{N}\mathbf{S}}
is a modular
\infty
-operad in profinite spaces.
∎

Having constructed the profinite-space analogue of our modular operad in groupoids, we can now revisit the two-level principle in this homotopy-coherent setting. The statement for
\mathbf{S}
says that genus-one data controls the entire tower (Corollary
4.3
). The same phenomenon persists after passing to the modular
\infty
-operad in profinite spaces.

Proposition 6.19
.

Restriction to the genus-one truncation induces an isomorphism

\pi_{0}\mathbb{R}\operatorname{End}_{\mathrm{dMod}(\widehat{\mathtt{S}})}(\widehat{\mathrm{B}\mathrm{N}\mathbf{S}})\longrightarrow\pi_{0}\mathbb{R}\operatorname{End}_{\mathrm{dMod}_{\leq 1}(\widehat{\mathtt{S}})}(\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{1}}\widehat{\mathrm{B}\mathrm{N}\mathbf{S}}).

Proof.

This is the profinite-space form of the two-level principle. The strict two-level result, Corollary
4.2
, says that maps out of
\mathbf{S}
are determined by their restrictions to the genus-one truncation because all generators and defining relations occur in genus at most one. Passing to the modular dendroidal nerve replaces this strict presentation by the corresponding homotopy-coherent Segal presentation. By Lemma
6.18
, the profinite completion
\widehat{\mathrm{B}\mathrm{N}\mathbf{S}}
satisfies the Segal condition, so the higher-genus values are recovered from the same genus-one generators and relations by homotopy-coherent composition. Therefore restriction to
\mathscr{M}_{\leq 1}
induces an isomorphism on
\pi_{0}
of derived endomorphism monoids.
∎

The action of
\widehat{\mathsf{\Gamma}}
on
\widehat{\mathbf{S}}
was constructed using the two-level principle for
\mathbf{S}
, namely the fact that the genus-zero and genus-one generators and relations determine the full modular operad. After applying the modular dendroidal nerve and profinite classifying spaces, the comparison of strict automorphisms with homotopy automorphisms gives an action on the profinite-space model.

Theorem 6.20
.

The action of
\widehat{\mathsf{\Gamma}}
on
\widehat{\mathbf{S}}
induces an action of
\widehat{\mathsf{\Gamma}}
on
\widehat{\mathrm{B}\mathrm{N}\mathbf{S}}
by automorphisms in the homotopy category of modular
\infty
-operads in profinite spaces. More precisely, there is a homomorphism

\widehat{\mathsf{\Gamma}}\longrightarrow\pi_{0}\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Aut}_{\mathrm{dMod}(\widehat{\mathtt{S}})}(\widehat{\mathrm{B}\mathrm{N}\mathbf{S}}).

Proof.

By Theorem
4.8
, the group
\widehat{\mathsf{\Gamma}}
acts on
\widehat{\mathbf{S}}
by object-fixing automorphisms. Applying the modular dendroidal nerve and profinite classifying spaces gives self-maps of
\widehat{\mathrm{B}\mathrm{N}\mathbf{S}}
. Lemma
6.18
shows that
\widehat{\mathrm{B}\mathrm{N}\mathbf{S}}
is a modular
\infty
-operad in profinite spaces. The comparison between strict object-fixing automorphisms and homotopy automorphisms, together with the classifying-space functor, therefore sends the action map
\widehat{\mathsf{\Gamma}}\longrightarrow\operatorname{Aut}_{0}(\widehat{\mathbf{S}})
to a homomorphism

\widehat{\mathsf{\Gamma}}\longrightarrow\pi_{0}\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Aut}_{\mathrm{dMod}(\widehat{\mathtt{S}})}(\widehat{\mathrm{B}\mathrm{N}\mathbf{S}}).

∎

Appendix A
Homotopy Theory of Modular Operads

Modular operads can be viewed as algebras over a certain coloured operad (cf.
[
21
, Appendix A]
). This perspective allows us to apply Theorem 2.1 of
[
9
]
, which ensures that, under suitable hypotheses on the base category
\mathscr{E}
, categories of algebras over coloured operads inherit transferred model structures. More precisely, if
\mathscr{E}
is a cofibrantly generated symmetric monoidal model category with cofibrant unit, a symmetric monoidal fibrant replacement functor, and a cocommutative coalgebra interval, then the category of algebras over such a coloured operad admits a cofibrantly generated model structure in which weak equivalences and fibrations are defined entrywise in
\mathscr{E}
.

Since our arguments rely on several specific features of the coloured operad governing modular operads, we begin with a brief review of coloured operads and
their algebras.

Definition A.1
.

Let
\mathfrak{C}
be a nonempty set of
colours
. A
\mathfrak{C}
-coloured
sequence
in
\mathscr{E}
is a collection of objects

\mathcal{P}(c_{0};c_{1},\ldots,c_{k})

indexed by lists
(c_{0};c_{1},\ldots,c_{k})
of colours in
\mathfrak{C}
, together with right actions of the symmetric groups. Thus, for each
\sigma\in\Sigma_{k}
, there is an isomorphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}(c_{0};c_{1},\ldots,c_{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}(c_{0};c_{\sigma(1)},\ldots,c_{\sigma(k)}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma^{*}}

A
\mathfrak{C}
-coloured
operad
is a
\mathfrak{C}
-coloured sequence
\mathcal{P}
equipped with:

(1)

distinguished unit objects
\iota_{c}\in\mathcal{P}(c;c)
;

(2)

composition maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}(c_{0};c_{1},\ldots,c_{k})\times\mathcal{P}(d_{0};d_{1},\ldots,d_{j})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}(c_{0};c_{1},\ldots,c_{i-1},d_{1},\ldots,d_{j},c_{i+1},\ldots,c_{k}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ_{i}}

defined whenever
c_{i}=d_{0}
and
1\leq i\leq k
.

These data are required to satisfy the standard associativity, unitality, and equivariance axioms.

A map
f:\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{Q}
of coloured operads is a map of the underlying coloured sequences that respects the units, compositions, and symmetric group actions. The category of
\mathfrak{C}
-coloured operads in
\mathscr{E}
is denoted
\mathrm{Op}_{\mathfrak{C}}(\mathscr{E})
. For further details, see
[
9
, Definition 1.1]
or
[
20
, §2.2]
.

Definition A.2
.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be a
\mathfrak{C}
-coloured operad. A
\mathcal{P}
-algebra
in
\mathscr{E}
is a collection of objects
\{X(c)\}_{c\in\mathfrak{C}}
together with structure maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}(c_{0};c_{1},\ldots,c_{k})\times X(c_{1})\times\cdots\times X(c_{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(c_{0})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}

satisfying associativity, unitality, and equivariance with respect to the operad structure. The category of such algebras is denoted
\operatorname{Alg}_{\mathcal{P}}(\mathscr{E})
.

Example A.3
.

The
operad of modular operads
is an
\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
-coloured operad
\mathcal{MOp}
. For a list of pairs
\bigl((g,r);(g_{1},r_{1}),\ldots,(g_{n},r_{n})\bigr),
the object

\mathcal{MOp}\bigl((g,r);(g_{1},r_{1}),\ldots,(g_{n},r_{n})\bigr)

is the set of strict isomorphism classes of connected genus-graded, labelled graphs
G=(G,\lambda,\ell,\epsilon)
with
n
vertices and
r
legs, satisfying:

•

the vertex labelled by
i
has valence
|nb(v_{\lambda(i)})|=r_{i}+1
and genus
\epsilon(v_{\lambda(i)})=g_{i}
;

•

the total genus of
G
is
g
, equivalently

g=\beta_{1}(G)+\sum_{i=1}^{n}g_{i}.

The symmetric group
\Sigma_{n}
acts on
\mathcal{MOp}\bigl((g,r);(g_{1},r_{1}),\ldots,(g_{n},r_{n})\bigr)
by permuting the vertex labelling
\lambda
.

Operadic composition is defined by graph substitution. Given

G\in\mathcal{MOp}\bigl((g,r);(g_{1},r_{1}),\ldots,(g_{n},r_{n})\bigr)\quad\text{and}\quad H_{v_{\lambda(i)}}\in\mathcal{MOp}\bigl((g_{i},r_{i});(h_{1},s_{1}),\ldots,(h_{m},s_{m})\bigr),

the composite
G\circ_{i}H_{v_{\lambda(i)}}
is obtained by substituting the
graph
H_{v_{\lambda(i)}}
into the vertex
v_{\lambda(i)}
of
G
:

G\circ_{i}H_{v_{\lambda(i)}}:=G\{H_{v_{\lambda(i)}}\},

as in
[
34
, Construction 1.18]
. This gives an element of

\mathcal{MOp}\bigl((g,r);(g_{1},r_{1}),\ldots,(g_{i-1},r_{i-1}),(h_{1},s_{1}),\ldots,(h_{m},s_{m}),(g_{i+1},r_{i+1}),\ldots,(g_{n},r_{n})\bigr).

Associativity, equivariance, and unitality follow from the corresponding properties of graph substitution. For a detailed construction and verification, see
[
21
, Appendix A]
or
[
45
, §13.4]
.

Proposition A.4
.

The category of algebras over the coloured operad
\mathcal{MOp}
is naturally isomorphic to the category of modular operads in
\mathscr{E}
:

\operatorname{Alg}_{\mathcal{MOp}}(\mathscr{E})\cong\mathrm{Mod}(\mathscr{E}).

This identification allows us to import general results about algebras over coloured operads. In particular, under suitable hypotheses on the base category
\mathscr{E}
, it gives a transferred model structure on
\mathrm{Mod}(\mathscr{E})
.

We will use this when
\mathscr{E}
is one of the following Cartesian monoidal model categories:

•

the category of simplicial sets
\mathscr{S}
, equipped with the Kan–Quillen model structure;

•

the category of groupoids
\mathscr{G}
, equipped with the canonical model structure in which weak equivalences are equivalences of categories
[
1
]
;

•

the category of profinite groupoids
\widehat{\mathscr{G}}
, equipped with the cofibrantly generated model structure described by Horel
[
42
, Theorem 4.12]
.

In each case,
\mathscr{E}
is a cofibrantly generated Cartesian monoidal model category with cofibrant unit, a symmetric monoidal fibrant replacement functor, and a cocommutative coalgebra interval. Therefore Theorem 2.1 of
[
9
]
applies and we have the following.

Theorem A.5
.

For each of the categories
\mathscr{E}
listed above, the category
\mathrm{Mod}(\mathscr{E})
admits a cofibrantly generated model category structure in which a map of modular operads

f:\mathcal{P}\longrightarrow\mathcal{Q}
is a weak equivalence, respectively a fibration, if and only if each component

f(g,n+1):\mathcal{P}(g,n+1)\longrightarrow\mathcal{Q}(g,n+1)

is a weak equivalence, respectively a fibration, in
\mathscr{E}
, for all
g\geq 0
and
n\geq 0
.

In this model structure, fibrant objects are precisely those modular operads that are entrywise fibrant in
\mathscr{E}
. In particular, since every groupoid is fibrant in the canonical model structure, every modular operad
\mathcal{P}\in\mathrm{Mod}(\mathscr{G})
is fibrant.

Proposition A.6
.

Let
\mathcal{P}\in\mathrm{Mod}(\mathscr{G})
be a modular operad. If the underlying modular operad in sets
\operatorname{ob}(\mathcal{P})\in\mathrm{Mod}(\mathrm{Set})
is free, then
\mathcal{P}
is cofibrant in
\mathrm{Mod}(\mathscr{G})
.

Proof.

This follows by the same argument as for operads in groupoids; see
[
42
, Proposition 6.8]
.
∎

A map of coloured operads
f:\mathcal{P}\longrightarrow\mathcal{Q}
satisfying the
monoidal extension property
(see
[
21
, Definition 1.1]
) induces an adjunction between categories of algebras:

(A.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Alg}_{\mathcal{P}}(\mathscr{E})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Alg}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\mathscr{E})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}

See, for example,
[
9
]
or
[
21
, Remark 2.26]
.

A coloured operad is said to be
\Sigma
-cofibrant
if it is cofibrant as a coloured sequence, that is, cofibrant in the underlying model category of
\mathfrak{C}
-coloured symmetric sequences in
\mathscr{E}
(cf.
[
33
, Definition 4.7]
). When
\mathcal{P}
is
\Sigma
-cofibrant, any sufficiently well-behaved symmetric monoidal Quillen adjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}}

lifts to a Quillen adjunction between categories of algebras:

(A.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Alg}_{\mathcal{P}}(\mathscr{E})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Alg}_{\mathcal{P}}(\mathscr{D})}

Moreover, if the base adjunction is a Quillen equivalence, then so is the induced adjunction (
A.2
); see
[
33
, Theorem 5.8]
.

These results make
\Sigma
-cofibrant operads especially well behaved from a homotopical perspective. In particular, the operad governing modular operads has this property.

Lemma A.7
.

The coloured operad
\mathcal{MOp}
governing modular operads is
\Sigma
-cofibrant.

Proof.

It suffices to show that the symmetric group
\Sigma_{n}
acts freely on each set

\mathcal{MOp}((g,p);(g_{1},k_{1}),\ldots,(g_{n},k_{n})).

This action is by permutation of the vertex labelling. Since the operations of
\mathcal{MOp}
are represented by strict isomorphism classes of connected, genus-graded graphs with labelled vertices and non-empty boundary, a non-trivial permutation of the vertex labels cannot fix such a class. Thus the
\Sigma_{n}
-action is free. Hence
\mathcal{MOp}
is
\Sigma
-cofibrant. For a similar argument, see
[
33
, Proposition 4.13]
.
∎

Remark A.8
.

The restriction to operations with non-empty boundary is important for the
\Sigma
-cofibrancy argument. The boundary labelling, together with the strict isomorphism convention for labelled graphs, prevents a non-trivial permutation of the vertex labels from fixing an operation of
\mathcal{MOp}
. If one allows arity-zero operations, equivalently graphs with empty boundary, this rigidity can fail. Such graphs may have non-trivial automorphisms which compensate for a permutation of the vertex labels, and the corresponding symmetric group action need not be free. Thus the coloured operad governing modular operads with arity-zero operations is generally not
\Sigma
-cofibrant. See
[
33
, Example 6.3]
or
[
18
, Figure 2]
for concrete examples.

Corollary A.9
.

The adjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Mod}(\mathscr{S})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Mod}(\mathscr{G})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{B}}

is a Quillen adjunction, and the right adjoint
\mathrm{B}
is homotopically fully faithful. In particular, for all
\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q}\in\mathrm{Mod}(\mathscr{G})
, the induced map

\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}_{\mathrm{Mod}(\mathscr{G})}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}_{\mathrm{Mod}(\mathscr{S})}(\mathrm{B}\mathcal{P},\mathrm{B}\mathcal{Q})

is a weak equivalence of spaces.

Proof.

By Lemma
A.7
, the coloured operad
\mathcal{MOp}
is
\Sigma
-cofibrant. The adjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{B}}

is a Cartesian monoidal Quillen adjunction. Hence, by
[
33
, Theorem 5.8]
, it lifts to a Quillen adjunction between categories of
\mathcal{MOp}
algebras, equivalently between
\mathrm{Mod}(\mathscr{S})
and
\mathrm{Mod}(\mathscr{G})
.

It remains to check homotopical full faithfulness of the right adjoint. Since weak equivalences are defined entrywise and every groupoid is fibrant, every object of
\mathrm{Mod}(\mathscr{G})
is fibrant. For each groupoid
G
, the counit
\Pi_{1}\mathrm{B}G\longrightarrow G
is an equivalence of groupoids. Therefore, for every
\mathcal{P}\in\mathrm{Mod}(\mathscr{G})
, the counit
\Pi_{1}\mathrm{B}\mathcal{P}\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}
is an entrywise weak equivalence. Since the derived counit is a weak equivalence, it follows that
\mathrm{B}
is homotopically fully faithful. The statement on derived mapping spaces follows.
∎

A.1.
Truncation of Modular Operads

We now define genus truncations of modular operads by restricting the coloured operad
\mathcal{MOp}
governing modular operads. Recall that our colours are
pairs
(h,p)
, where
h
is the genus and
p+1
is the number of boundary components.

For
g\geq 0
, let
\mathcal{MOp}_{\leq g}
be the coloured suboperad of
\mathcal{MOp}
whose colours are the pairs

(h,p)\qquad 0\leq h\leq g,\quad p\geq 0.

Its operations are those operations of
\mathcal{MOp}
whose output colour has genus at most
g
. Thus

\mathcal{MOp}_{\leq g}\bigl((h,p);(h_{1},p_{1}),\ldots,(h_{n},p_{n})\bigr)

is the set of strict isomorphism classes of connected genus-graded, labelled graphs
G=(G,\lambda,\ell,\epsilon)
with
n
vertices and
p+1
legs, satisfying:

•

the vertex labelled by
i
has valence
|nb(v_{\lambda(i)})|=p_{i}+1
and genus
\epsilon(v_{\lambda(i)})=h_{i}
;

•

the total genus of
G
is
h\leq g
, equivalently

h=\beta_{1}(G)+\sum_{i=1}^{n}h_{i}.

Graph substitution preserves the total output genus, and hence
\mathcal{MOp}_{\leq g}
is indeed a coloured suboperad of
\mathcal{MOp}
.

A
genus-
g
truncated modular operad
in
\mathscr{E}
is an algebra over
\mathcal{MOp}_{\leq g}
. We write

\mathrm{Mod}_{\leq g}(\mathscr{E}):=\operatorname{Alg}_{\mathcal{MOp}_{\leq g}}(\mathscr{E})

for the category of genus-
g
truncated modular operads. Concretely, an object of
\mathrm{Mod}_{\leq g}(\mathscr{E})
consists of the entries
\mathcal{P}(h,p+1)
for
0\leq h\leq g
and
p\geq 0
, together with all composition and contraction operations whose output has genus at most
g
. Thus compositions with
h_{1}+h_{2}\leq g
are retained, and contractions
\xi_{i}^{j}:\mathcal{P}(h,p+1)\to\mathcal{P}(h+1,p-1)
are retained only when
h+1\leq g
.

The inclusion of coloured operads
\iota_{g}:\mathcal{MOp}_{\leq g}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{MOp}

induces a restriction functor

\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{g}}^{*}:=\iota_{g}^{*}:\mathrm{Mod}(\mathscr{E})\longrightarrow\mathrm{Mod}_{\leq g}(\mathscr{E}),

which we call the
genus-
g
truncation functor
. This functor admits both a left adjoint and a right adjoint:

(A.3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Mod}(\mathscr{E})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Mod}_{\leq g}(\mathscr{E}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{g}}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{g}})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{g}})_{!}}

The left adjoint
(\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{g}})_{!}
generalizes the notion of the
modular envelope
; see
[
65
]
,
[
21
, Appendix A]
, and
[
32
, §4]
for related constructions.

Proposition A.10
.

Let
\mathscr{E}
be a cofibrantly generated Cartesian monoidal model category with cofibrant unit, a symmetric monoidal fibrant replacement functor, and a cocommutative coalgebra interval. Then both
\mathrm{Mod}(\mathscr{E})
and
\mathrm{Mod}_{\leq g}(\mathscr{E})
