admit cofibrantly generated model category structures in which weak equivalences and fibrations are defined entrywise. More precisely, a map
f:\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{Q}
in
\mathrm{Mod}(\mathscr{E})
is a weak equivalence, respectively a fibration, if and only if each map

f(h,m+1):\mathcal{P}(h,m+1)\longrightarrow\mathcal{Q}(h,m+1)

is a weak equivalence, respectively a fibration, in
\mathscr{E}
, for all
h\geq 0
and
m\geq 0
. Similarly, in
\mathrm{Mod}_{\leq g}(\mathscr{E})
this condition is imposed only for
0\leq h\leq g
.

Moreover:

•

the truncation functor

\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{g}}^{*}:\mathrm{Mod}(\mathscr{E})\to\mathrm{Mod}_{\leq g}(\mathscr{E})
preserves weak equivalences and
fibrations;

•

both adjoints
(\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{g}})_{!}
and
(\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{g}})_{*}
are fully faithful;

•

there are canonical isomorphisms

\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{g}}^{*}\circ(\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{g}})_{!}\cong\operatorname{id}_{\mathrm{Mod}_{\leq g}(\mathscr{E})}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{g}}^{*}\circ(\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{g}})_{*}\cong\operatorname{id}_{\mathrm{Mod}_{\leq g}(\mathscr{E})}.

Proof.

The functor
\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{g}}^{*}
is restriction along the inclusion of coloured operads

\iota_{g}:\mathcal{MOp}_{\leq g}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{MOp}.

Since weak equivalences and fibrations are entrywise, restriction clearly preserves both weak equivalences and fibrations.

The left and right adjoints are the left and right algebraic extensions along
\iota_{g}
. Because
\mathcal{MOp}_{\leq g}
is a full coloured suboperad of
\mathcal{MOp}
on the colours of genus at most
g
, extending a truncated modular operad and then restricting back recovers exactly the original truncated
object. Equivalently, the counits

\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{g}}^{*}(\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{g}})_{!}\longrightarrow\operatorname{id}_{\mathrm{Mod}_{\leq g}(\mathscr{E})}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{g}}^{*}(\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{g}})_{*}\longrightarrow\operatorname{id}_{\mathrm{Mod}_{\leq g}(\mathscr{E})}

are isomorphisms. Hence both adjoints are fully faithful.
∎

As in the modular case, cyclic operads are algebras over a coloured operad. In fact, the coloured operad governing cyclic operads may be identified with the
genus-zero truncation

\mathcal{C}\cong\mathcal{MOp}_{\leq 0}.

Its operations are represented by labelled unrooted trees, equivalently by the genus-zero graphs

T\in\mathcal{MOp}_{\leq 0}\bigl((0,p);(0,k_{1}),\ldots,(0,k_{n})\bigr).

Here, as throughout, the colour
(0,p)
corresponds to
p+1
boundary components. The following identification can be found in
[
21
, Example A3]
or
[
48
, Section 1.6.4]
.

Proposition A.11
.

There is a natural isomorphism of categories

\operatorname{Alg}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathscr{E})\cong\mathrm{Cyc}(\mathscr{E}).

As a consequence of Proposition
A.11
, Theorem 2.1 of
[
9
]
applies to cyclic operads as well. Thus
\mathrm{Cyc}(\mathscr{E})
admits a cofibrantly generated model category structure in which a map of cyclic operads
f:\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{Q}
is a weak equivalence,
respectively a fibration, if and only if each component
f(n):\mathcal{P}(n)\longrightarrow\mathcal{Q}(n)
is a weak equivalence, respectively a fibration, in
\mathscr{E}
, for all
n\geq 1
. Moreover, under the identification
\mathrm{Mod}_{\leq 0}(\mathscr{E})\cong\mathrm{Cyc}(\mathscr{E})
, the genus-zero truncation functor becomes

\mathrm{tr}_{\leq{0}}^{*}:\mathrm{Mod}(\mathscr{E})\longrightarrow\mathrm{Cyc}(\mathscr{E}).

It preserves weak equivalences and fibrations, and hence is the right adjoint in a Quillen adjunction.

Appendix B
Homotopical properties of the nerve

Recall that
\mathrm{dMod}(\mathscr{E})
denotes the category of modular dendroidal objects, that is, functors

X:\mathscr{M}^{\mathrm{op}}\longrightarrow\mathscr{E}.

This category carries several useful model structures. We write
\mathrm{dMod}(\mathscr{E})_{p}
for the projective model structure, in which weak equivalences and fibrations are defined entrywise
[
41
, Theorem 11.6.1]
.

The graphical category
\mathscr{M}
is also a generalized Reedy category
[
34
, Theorem 2.22]
. Hence, for any suitable model category
\mathscr{E}
,
\mathrm{dMod}(\mathscr{E})
admits a Reedy model structure; see
[
10
, Theorem 1.6]
. We write
\mathrm{dMod}(\mathscr{E})_{R}
for this Reedy model structure.

The identity functor on the underlying category
\mathrm{dMod}(\mathscr{E})
induces a Quillen equivalence

(B.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{dMod}(\mathscr{E})_{p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{dMod}(\mathscr{E})_{R}.}

This follows from
[
34
, Lemma 3.9]
.

Modular
\infty
-operads are obtained as the fibrant objects in a left Bousfield localisation of the Reedy model structure. We use left Bousfield localisation in the standard sense: if
\mathcal{S}
is a set of maps in a model category
\mathcal{M}
, then the localised model structure
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{M}
has the same cofibrations as
\mathcal{M}
, and its fibrant objects are precisely the fibrant objects
Z
of
\mathcal{M}
such that, for every map
A\to B
in
\mathcal{S}
, the induced map of derived mapping spaces

\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}(B,Z)\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}(A,Z)

is a weak equivalence. Its weak equivalences are the corresponding
\mathcal{S}
-local equivalences. We refer to
[
41
, Chapters 3–4]
for the general theory.

In Definition
5.3
, a modular dendroidal object
X\in\mathrm{dMod}(\mathscr{E})
is called a modular
\infty
-operad if it is reduced and
satisfies the Segal condition. Here reduced means that
X_{\updownarrow}=*
.

Any modular dendroidal object can be reduced via the pushout

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{\updownarrow}\times\mathscr{M}[\updownarrow]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{M}[\updownarrow]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{*}.}

This defines a functor
(-)_{*}:\mathrm{dMod}(\mathscr{E})\longrightarrow\mathrm{dMod}(\mathscr{E})_{*}
which is left adjoint to the inclusion of reduced modular dendroidal objects into all
modular dendroidal objects.

Both the projective and Reedy model structures restrict to
\mathrm{dMod}(\mathscr{E})_{*}
. We write
\mathrm{dMod}(\mathscr{E})_{*,p}
and
\mathrm{dMod}(\mathscr{E})_{*,R}
for the reduced projective and reduced Reedy model structures, respectively.

For each graph
G\in\mathscr{M}
, let
\mathrm{Sc}[G]_{*}\longrightarrow\mathscr{M}[G]_{*}
denote the reduced Segal core inclusion. Let
S
be the set of these maps:

S=\{\mathrm{Sc}[G]_{*}\longrightarrow\mathscr{M}[G]_{*}\mid G\in\mathscr{M}\}.

We denote the left Bousfield localisation of
\mathrm{dMod}(\mathscr{E})_{*,R}
with respect to
S
by
\mathcal{L}_{S}\mathrm{dMod}(\mathscr{E})_{*,R}
. Its fibrant objects are the Reedy
fibrant reduced modular dendroidal objects
X
for which, for every graph
G
, the induced map

\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}(\mathscr{M}[G]_{*},X)\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}(\mathrm{Sc}[G]_{*},X)

is a weak equivalence. This is exactly the Segal condition.

We can now make precise what we mean by the
\infty
-category of modular
\infty
-operads.

Definition B.1
.

The
\infty
-category of modular
\infty
-operads, denoted
\mathrm{Mod}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})
, is the
\infty
-category obtained from the full relative subcategory of
\mathrm{dMod}(\mathscr{E})_{*,p}
spanned by the modular
\infty
-operads, with weak equivalences given by the entrywise weak equivalences.

The main result of this appendix is that the modular dendroidal nerve is homotopically fully faithful. In other words, strict modular operads embed into modular
\infty
-operads without changing their derived mapping spaces.

Theorem B.2
.

Let
\mathscr{E}=\mathscr{S}
or
\mathscr{G}
. For any two modular operads
\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q}\in\mathrm{Mod}(\mathscr{E})
, the nerve induces a weak equivalence of derived mapping spaces

\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}_{\mathrm{Mod}(\mathscr{E})}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}_{\mathrm{Mod}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})}(\mathrm{N}\mathcal{P},\mathrm{N}\mathcal{Q}).

Equivalently, the modular dendroidal nerve

\mathrm{N}:\mathrm{Mod}(\mathscr{E})\longrightarrow\mathrm{Mod}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})

is homotopically fully faithful.

Before proving Theorem
B.2
, we record a comparison between decorated representables and nerves of free modular
operads. Let
G=(G,\lambda,\ell,\epsilon)
be a labelled graph with
k
vertices, written as
v_{\lambda(1)},\ldots,v_{\lambda(k)}
according to the vertex labelling. For each
1\leq i\leq k
, write
\epsilon(v_{\lambda(i)})=g_{i}
and
|nb(v_{\lambda(i)})|=n_{i}+1
.

Define a genus-graded sequence in
\mathrm{Set}
, denoted
X_{G}
, by:

X_{G}(h,n+1)=\coprod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq k\\
g_{i}=h,\;n_{i}=n\end{subarray}}*,

and denote the generator corresponding to the
i
th labelled vertex by
x_{i}^{(g_{i},n_{i}+1)}
. Thus
X_{G}
has one generator for each labelled vertex of
G
, placed in the entry determined by the genus and valence of that vertex.

We also regard
X_{G}
as a discrete decoration of the representable modular dendroidal set
\mathscr{M}[G]
: a map into
G
carries the vertex data of
G
, and each vertex
v_{\lambda(i)}
is decorated by the corresponding generator
x_{i}^{(g_{i},n_{i}+1)}
. We write

Z_{G}:=\mathscr{M}[G]\times X_{G}

for this decorated representable modular dendroidal object. Concretely,
Z_{G}
is the modular dendroidal object whose value on a graph
H
consists of a map
H\to G
in
\mathscr{M}
, together with the induced decoration of the vertices of
H
by the generators of
X_{G}
.

Let
\mathcal{F}(X_{G})
be the free modular operad generated by
X_{G}
. The graph
G
determines a distinguished operation in
\mathcal{F}(X_{G})
, obtained by composing the generators
x_{i}^{(g_{i},n_{i}+1)}
according to the internal edges of
G
.

Lemma B.3
.

Let
\mathscr{E}=\mathscr{G}
or
\mathscr{S}
, and let
G\in\mathscr{M}
. With
X_{G}
and
Z_{G}
as above, the decorated representable
Z_{G}
and the nerve of the free modular operad
\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{F}(X_{G}))
are weakly equivalent in the localised model structure
\mathcal{L}_{S}\mathrm{dMod}(\mathscr{E})_{*,R}
.

Proof.

The argument is the modular analogue of
[
11
, Proposition 4.4]
.

Let
\mathrm{Sc}_{G}(X_{G})
denote the Segal core of
G
decorated by the vertex generators
x_{i}^{(g_{i},n_{i}+1)}
. Thus
\mathrm{Sc}_{G}(X_{G})
is obtained from
\mathrm{Sc}[G]_{*}
by labelling the corolla corresponding to the vertex
v_{\lambda(i)}
by the generator
x_{i}^{(g_{i},n_{i}+1)}
. There is a canonical map
\mathrm{Sc}_{G}(X_{G})\longrightarrow Z_{G}.
Since
\mathrm{Sc}[G]_{*}\to\mathscr{M}[G]_{*}
is one of the Segal core inclusions, this map is a weak equivalence in the localised model structure
\mathcal{L}_{S}\mathrm{dMod}(\mathscr{E})_{*,R}
.

We now compare
\mathrm{Sc}_{G}(X_{G})
with
\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{F}(X_{G}))
. The generators
x_{i}^{(g_{i},n_{i}+1)}
define maps from the vertex corollas of
G
into

\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{F}(X_{G}))
, and these assemble to a map

\mathrm{Sc}_{G}(X_{G})\longrightarrow\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{F}(X_{G})).

Since
\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{F}(X_{G}))
is the nerve of a strict modular operad, it satisfies the Segal condition strictly. Therefore maps from a Segal object into
\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{F}(X_{G}))
are determined by their restrictions to the vertex corollas. By the universal property of the free modular operad

\mathcal{F}(X_{G})
, this is exactly the same data as a choice of images of the generators
x_{i}^{(g_{i},n_{i}+1)}
. It follows that the map
\mathrm{Sc}_{G}(X_{G})\longrightarrow\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{F}(X_{G}))
exhibits
\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{F}(X_{G}))
as a local replacement of
\mathrm{Sc}_{G}(X_{G})
.

Combining this with the local weak equivalence
\mathrm{Sc}_{G}(X_{G})\to Z_{G}
, we obtain a weak equivalence

Z_{G}\longrightarrow\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{F}(X_{G}))

in
\mathcal{L}_{S}\mathrm{dMod}(\mathscr{E})_{*,R}
.
∎

The modular dendroidal nerve
\mathrm{N}:\mathrm{Mod}(\mathscr{E})\longrightarrow\mathrm{dMod}(\mathscr{E})_{*}
admits a left adjoint, denoted

\tau:\mathrm{dMod}(\mathscr{E})_{*}\longrightarrow\mathrm{Mod}(\mathscr{E}).

The left adjoint is determined by its values on reduced representables:
\tau(\mathscr{M}[G]_{*})
is the free modular operad generated by the graph
G
. More explicitly, if a vertex
v\in V_{G}
has genus
\epsilon(v)
and valence
|nb(v)|
, then the corresponding generator lies in the entry
(\epsilon(v),|nb(v)|)
.

Proposition B.4
.

Let
\mathscr{E}=\mathscr{G}
or
\mathscr{S}
. The adjoint pair

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{dMod}(\mathscr{E})_{*,p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Mod}(\mathscr{E})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{N}}

is a Quillen pair.

Proof.

It is enough to show that the right adjoint
\mathrm{N}
preserves fibrations and
trivial fibrations. For a modular operad
\mathcal{P}
, the nerve is given on corollas
by

(\mathrm{N}\mathcal{P})_{C_{(g,n+1)}}=\mathcal{P}(g,n+1).

Thus, if
f:\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{Q}
is a fibration, respectively a trivial fibration,
in
\mathrm{Mod}(\mathscr{E})
, then each map

f(g,n+1):\mathcal{P}(g,n+1)\longrightarrow\mathcal{Q}(g,n+1)

is a fibration, respectively a trivial fibration, in
\mathscr{E}
.

Now let
G\in\mathscr{M}
. Since the nerve of a modular operad satisfies the Segal condition strictly, we have

(\mathrm{N}\mathcal{P})_{G}=\prod_{v\in V_{G}}(\mathrm{N}\mathcal{P})_{C_{(\epsilon(v),|nb(v)|)}}.

Therefore
(\mathrm{N}f)_{G}
is the finite product of the maps

f\bigl(\epsilon(v),|nb(v)|\bigr):\mathcal{P}\bigl(\epsilon(v),|nb(v)|\bigr)\longrightarrow\mathcal{Q}\bigl(\epsilon(v),|nb(v)|\bigr),

where
v
ranges over the vertices of
G
. Finite products preserve fibrations and trivial fibrations in both
\mathscr{S}
and
\mathscr{G}
. Hence
(\mathrm{N}f)_{G}
is a fibration, respectively a trivial fibration, for every graph
G
.

Since fibrations and weak equivalences in the projective model structure on
\mathrm{dMod}(\mathscr{E})_{*,p}
are defined entrywise,
\mathrm{N}f
is a fibration, respectively a trivial fibration. It follows that
\mathrm{N}
is a right Quillen functor, and the adjunction is a Quillen pair.
∎

We now prove the key input for homotopical full faithfulness of the nerve. The idea of the next lemma is that cofibrant modular dendroidal objects are built, up to homotopy colimit, from reduced representables. On reduced representables, the comparison with the nerve is controlled by Lemma
B.3
: the localisation identifies a decorated representable with the nerve of the free modular operad generated by its vertex decorations. The general case then follows because the relevant constructions preserve the homotopy colimits used to build cofibrant diagrams.

Lemma B.5
.

Let
\mathscr{E}=\mathscr{S}
or
\mathscr{G}
. If
X\in\mathrm{dMod}(\mathscr{E})_{p}
is cofibrant, then the derived unit

X_{*}\longrightarrow\mathrm{N}\bigl(\tau(X_{*})_{f}\bigr)

is a local equivalence in
\mathcal{L}_{S}\mathrm{dMod}(\mathscr{E})_{*,p}
.

Proof.

We give the argument for
\mathscr{E}=\mathscr{S}
. The proof for
\mathscr{G}
is analogous.

Since
X
is cofibrant in the projective model structure, we can use standard simplicial resolution of a cofibrant diagrams to write
X_{*}
, up to weak equivalence, as a homotopy colimit of coproducts of decorated reduced representables:

X_{*}\simeq\operatorname*{hocolim}_{\Delta^{op}}\coprod_{i}Z_{G_{i}}.

Here each
Z_{G_{i}}
is of the form considered in Lemma
B.3
, with vertex-decoration sequence
X_{G_{i}}
.

Since
\tau
is a left Quillen functor by Proposition
B.4
, its left derived functor preserves homotopy colimits. Thus,

\tau(X_{*})\simeq\tau\left(\operatorname*{hocolim}_{\Delta^{op}}\coprod_{i}Z_{G_{i}}\right)\simeq\operatorname*{hocolim}_{\Delta^{op}}\coprod_{i}\tau(Z_{G_{i}}).

By construction of the left adjoint
\tau
, each
\tau(Z_{G_{i}})
is the free modular operad generated by the corresponding vertex decorations:
\tau(Z_{G_{i}})\cong\mathcal{F}(X_{G_{i}}).
Putting this together, we have

\tau(X_{*})\simeq\operatorname*{hocolim}_{\Delta^{op}}\coprod_{i}\mathcal{F}(X_{G_{i}}).

We now compare the nerve of this homotopy colimit with the homotopy colimit of the nerves. After taking a fibrant replacement in
\mathrm{Mod}(\mathscr{S})
and applying
the nerve, we get, up to local equivalence,

\mathrm{N}(\tau(X_{*})_{f})\simeq\mathrm{N}\left(\operatorname*{hocolim}_{\Delta^{op}}\coprod_{i}\mathcal{F}(X_{G_{i}})_{f}\right).

The nerve of a strict modular operad satisfies the Segal condition strictly and, thus its value on a graph is a finite product of its values on the vertex corollas. Since homotopy colimits over
\Delta^{op}
commute with finite homotopy products in
\mathscr{S}
, it follows that

\mathrm{N}\left(\operatorname*{hocolim}_{\Delta^{op}}\coprod_{i}\mathcal{F}(X_{G_{i}})_{f}\right)\simeq\operatorname*{hocolim}_{\Delta^{op}}\coprod_{i}\mathrm{N}\bigl(\mathcal{F}(X_{G_{i}})_{f}\bigr).

On the other hand, by Lemma
B.3
, each decorated representable
Z_{G_{i}}
is weakly equivalent, in the localised model structure, to
\mathrm{N}\bigl(\mathcal{F}(X_{G_{i}})\bigr).
Fibrant replacement in
\mathrm{Mod}(\mathscr{S})
does not change the local equivalence class of its nerve, so we may equally use

\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{F}(X_{G_{i}})_{f})
. Therefore

\operatorname*{hocolim}_{\Delta^{op}}\coprod_{i}Z_{G_{i}}\longrightarrow\operatorname*{hocolim}_{\Delta^{op}}\coprod_{i}\mathrm{N}\bigl(\mathcal{F}(X_{G_{i}})_{f}\bigr)

is a local equivalence. Combining the displayed equivalences above gives a local equivalence

X_{*}\longrightarrow\mathrm{N}(\tau(X_{*})_{f})

in
\mathcal{L}_{S}\mathrm{dMod}(\mathscr{S})_{*,p}
, as required.
∎

Proof of Theorem
B.2
.

By Proposition
B.4
, the adjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{dMod}(\mathscr{E})_{*,p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Mod}(\mathscr{E})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{N}}

is a Quillen adjunction. Since the nerve of a strict modular operad satisfies the Segal condition strictly, the right adjoint
\mathrm{N}
sends fibrant modular operads to fibrant objects in the localised model structure
\mathcal{L}_{S}\mathrm{dMod}(\mathscr{E})_{*,p}
. Hence the same adjunction descends to a Quillen adjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}_{S}\mathrm{dMod}(\mathscr{E})_{*,p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Mod}(\mathscr{E}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{N}}

It remains to check that the right adjoint is homotopically fully faithful. For a strict modular operad
\mathcal{P}
, the counit
\tau\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{P})\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}
is an isomorphism. Indeed, the nerve records the values of
\mathcal{P}
on corollas, and the left adjoint
\tau
reconstructs the strict modular operad generated by these operations modulo the compositions already encoded by the nerve.

Now let
\mathcal{P}
be fibrant in
\mathrm{Mod}(\mathscr{E})
. Then the derived counit
\mathbb{L}\tau\,\mathbb{R}\mathrm{N}(\mathcal{P})\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}
is represented by the ordinary counit above, and is therefore a weak equivalence. It follows that the right derived functor of
\mathrm{N}
is fully faithful. Equivalently, for all
\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q}\in\mathrm{Mod}(\mathscr{E})
, the induced map on derived mapping spaces

\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}_{\mathrm{Mod}(\mathscr{E})}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q})\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}\operatorname{Map}_{\mathrm{Mod}_{\infty}(\mathscr{E})}(\mathrm{N}\mathcal{P},\mathrm{N}\mathcal{Q})

is a weak equivalence.
∎

Appendix C
The complex of markings over pants decompositions

In this appendix, we prove that the complex of markings
\mathcal{M}(S)
introduced in Section
2.7
is connected and simply connected. This is the complex used in the proof of Theorem
3.17
.

The
2
-dimensional CW complex
\mathcal{M}(S)
is closely related to the complex
\mathcal{M}^{max}(S)
of
[
3
]
. The main difference is that
\mathcal{M}(S)
only retains markings over pants decompositions: disc and cylinder components are not allowed. Following the strategy of
[
3
,
8
]
, our proof proceeds by comparing
\mathcal{M}(S)
with a simply connected complex via a forgetful map and applying two general comparison results.

The first lemma allows us to deduce simple connectivity of the target from that of the source.

Lemma C.1
.

Let
A
be a simply connected CW complex, and let
f:A\to B
be a continuous map such that every loop in
B
lifts to a loop in
A
. Then
B
is simply connected. ∎

The following proposition gives a criterion for deducing simple connectivity of the source from that of the target.

Proposition C.2
.

Let
A
and
B
be
2
-dimensional CW complexes, and let
\pi:A^{[1]}\to B^{[1]}
be a surjective map of their
1
-skeleta. Suppose that

(1)

B
is connected and simply connected.

(2)

For every vertex
b\in B
, the fiber
\pi^{-1}(b)
is connected, and every loop contained in
\pi^{-1}(b)
is contractible in
A
.

(3)

Let
b_{1}\overset{e}{\rightsquigarrow}b_{2}
be an edge in
B
, and let
a_{1}^{\prime}\overset{e^{\prime}}{\rightsquigarrow}a_{2}^{\prime}
and
a_{1}^{\prime\prime}\overset{e^{\prime\prime}}{\rightsquigarrow}a_{2}^{\prime\prime}
be two lifts to
A
. Then there exist paths
a_{1}^{\prime}\overset{e_{1}}{\rightsquigarrow}a_{1}^{\prime\prime}
in
\pi^{-1}(b_{1})
and
a_{2}^{\prime}\overset{e_{2}}{\rightsquigarrow}a_{2}^{\prime\prime}
in
\pi^{-1}(b_{2})
such that the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{1}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{2}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{1}^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{2}^{\prime\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e^{\prime\prime}}

is contractible in
A
.

(4)

For every
2
-cell
X
in
B
, its boundary
\partial X
can be lifted to a contractible loop in
A
.

Then
A
is connected and simply connected. ∎

The proofs of the preceding two results are standard. Proposition
C.2
is established by a cellular lifting argument: one projects a loop in
A
to
B
and lifts a nullhomotopy back to
A
cell by cell. Conditions
(2)
and
(3)
are used to compare different choices of lifts, and condition
(4)
is applied to lift the
2
-cells of
B
. We omit the details.

We now recall the pants complex of Hatcher–Lochak–Schneps
[
38
]
in a form suited to our setting. Let
S=(S,\delta)
be a labelled surface of type
(g,n+1)
, with
n\geq 0
. Recall that a
pants decomposition
of
S
is a cut system
C=\{c_{1},\ldots,c_{k}\}
of pairwise disjoint simple loops on
S
such that

\overline{S\setminus\bigcup_{r=1}^{k}c_{r}}\;=\;\coprod_{j}S_{j}

is a disjoint union of subsurfaces
S_{j}
, each of which is of type
(0,3)
. The
pants complex
of
S
, denoted
\mathcal{C}(S)
, is the
2
-dimensional CW complex whose vertices are isotopy classes of pants decompositions on
S
, whose edges are generated by
A
-moves and
S
-moves between pants decompositions, and whose
2
-cells are attached along loops of type
(3A)
,
(5A)
,
(3S)
,
(6AS)
, and
(C)
, in the sense of
[
38
, Section 2]
.

By
[
38
, Theorem 2]
, the complex
\mathcal{C}(S)
is connected and simply connected.

Recall from Definition
2.38
that the complex of marked pants decompositions on
S
, denoted
\mathcal{M}(S)
, has vertices given by marked pants decompositions
(C,[m])
on
S
. The
1
-cells are generated by
T
-,
A
-,
B
-, and
S
-moves. The
2
-cells are slight modifications of those appearing in
[
3
]
. Concretely, we include the following families of
2
-cells:

(1)

Commutativity of disjoint moves:
if
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
are moves supported in disjoint subsurfaces of
S
, then

E_{1}E_{2}=E_{2}E_{1}

[
3
, Section 4.7]
.

(2)

Boundary twist relations:
if
(C,[m])\overset{E}{\rightsquigarrow}(C^{\prime},[m^{\prime}])
is a move supported on a subsurface
S^{\prime}
and
a\in C
is a curve on the boundary of
S^{\prime}
, then if
E=B_{a,b}

E\;T_{a}=T_{b}\;E

otherwise, we have

E\;T_{a}=T_{a}\;E.

(3)

Pentagon relation:
whenever there exists a subsurface
S^{\prime}\subset S
of type
(0,5)
together with a diffeomorphism from
S^{\prime}
to the standard surface of Figure
16
carrying the relevant curves and markings to those represented in the figure, we attach a
2
-cell along the boundary loop of the pentagon diagram.

Figure 16
.
Pentagon relation

(4)

Hexagon relations:
whenever there exists a subsurface
S^{\prime}\subset S
of type
(0,4)
together with a diffeomorphism from
S^{\prime}
to the standard surface
S_{0,4}
of Figure
17
carrying the relevant curves and markings to those represented in the figure, we attach
2
-cells along the two hexagonal loops of Figure
17
.

Figure 17
.
Hexagon relations: Hexagon I (top left) and Hexagon II (bottom right)

(5)

Self-duality of associativity:

A_{c,c^{\prime}}A_{c^{\prime},c}=\operatorname{id}.

(6)

Twist–braiding relation:

B_{1,2}B_{2,1}=T_{0}T_{1}^{-1}T_{2}^{-1}.

(7)

Gluing relations for Dehn twists:

•

if two pairs of pants
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
are glued along a curve
a
, then

\operatorname{id}_{S_{1}}\cup_{a}T_{a}=T_{a}\cup_{a}\operatorname{id}_{S_{2}}

where the
T
-move is supported on
S_{2}
on the left-hand side and on
S_{1}
on the right-hand side.

•

if a pair of pants
S^{\prime}
has two boundary components
a
and
b
that get identified in
S
, then

T_{a}=T_{b}.

(8)

Genus-one relations for
(g,n)=(1,1)
:
as in Figure
7
in Definition
2.38
.

(9)

Genus-one relation for
(g,n)=(1,2)
:
as in Figure
8
in Definition
2.38
.

The complex of markings over the pair of pants
P
, will be of particular relevance for us.

Lemma C.3
.

Let
P
be a pair of pants. Then
\mathcal{M}(P)
is connected and simply connected.

Proof.

For a pair of pants
P
, a vertex of
\mathcal{M}(P)
is simply a marked pants decomposition of the form
(\emptyset,[m]),
since the only pants decomposition of
P
is the empty cut system.

Let
\mathcal{M}^{\max}(P)
denote the complex of maximal markings on
P
in the sense of
[
3
, Section 5]
. A vertex of
\mathcal{M}^{\max}(P)
is given by an isotopy class of a cut system

C=\{c_{1},\ldots,c_{k}\}

together with a marking, such that cutting
P
along
C
decomposes it into pairs of pants, possibly together with cylinder and disc components. In addition, one records a distinguished half-edge at each vertex of the marking graph.

We compare
\mathcal{M}^{\max}(P)
with
\mathcal{M}(P)
by means of a map

q:\mathcal{M}^{\max}(P)\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}(P).

On vertices, the map
q
forgets the cut system
C
and the distinguished half-edges, and contracts those edges of the marking graph that are not incident to to a boundary component, thereby producing a vertex
(\emptyset,[m])\in\mathcal{M}(P)
.

On edges, the map
q
behaves as follows. Moves of type
T
and
B
are either contracted to points or taken to the corresponding edges of
\mathcal{M}(P)
. By contrast, moves of type
F
forget cuts bounding cylindrical components and therefore become trivial after passing to the empty cut system. Likewise,
A
-moves are supported on a subsurface of type
(0,4)
, and also are contracted to points via
q
. Finally, a
Z
-move changes only the choice of distinguished half-edge, which is not retained in
\mathcal{M}(P)
. Thus
A
-,
F
-, and
Z
-edges are all sent to vertices.

This defines a cellular map on the
1
-skeleton of
\mathcal{M}^{\max}(P)
. Moreover, each defining
2
-cell of
\mathcal{M}^{\max}(P)
maps either to the corresponding
2
-cell of
\mathcal{M}(P)
or to a degenerate loop. Hence
q
extends to a cellular map

q:\mathcal{M}^{\max}(P)\to\mathcal{M}(P).

We next show that every loop in
\mathcal{M}(P)
lifts to a loop in
\mathcal{M}^{\max}(P)
. Given a cellular loop in
\mathcal{M}(P)
, each vertex in the loop is a marking of the form
(\emptyset,[m])
. Any such vertex can be lifted to one in
\mathcal{M}^{\max}(P)
by choosing a distinguished half-edge for the marking
[m]
. For instance, we may choose the half-edge incident to the boundary component labelled
0
. In this way, all vertices in the loop in
\mathcal{M}(P)
lift to vertices in
\mathcal{M}^{\max}(P)
.

Since every edge of
\mathcal{M}(P)
is a
T
- or
B
-move, each
e_{i}
lifts to an edge path in
\mathcal{M}^{\max}(P)
consisting of the corresponding
T
- or
B
-move, together with
Z
-moves, if necessary, to adjust the distinguished half-edge before or after performing that move. Concatenating these lifted edge paths yields a loop in
\mathcal{M}^{\max}(P)
projecting to the original loop in
\mathcal{M}(P)
.

By
[
3
, Theorem 4.9]
, the complex
\mathcal{M}^{\max}(P)
is connected and simply connected. Lemma
C.1
therefore implies that
\mathcal{M}(P)
is hence connected and simply connected as well.
∎

To study the complex of markings
\mathcal{M}(S)
for more general surfaces, we compare
\mathcal{M}(S)
with
\mathcal{C}(S)
via the natural forgetful map
F:\mathcal{M}(S)\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}(S)
defined on vertices by

F(C,[m])=C,

that is, by sending a marked pants decomposition to its underlying pants decomposition.

We use this to prove the main result of this section:

Proposition

(Proposition
2.39
)
.

Let
S=(S,\delta,\{x_{i}\}_{i=0}^{n})
be a parametrised, labelled surface of type
(g,n+1)
,
n\geq 0
. Then
\mathcal{M}(S)
, the complex of markings over pants decompositions on
S
, is connected and simply connected.

Proof.

Let

F:\mathcal{M}(S)\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}(S)

be the forgetful map sending a marked pants decomposition
(C,[m])
to its underlying pants decomposition
C
. By
[
38
, Theorem 2]
, the pants complex
\mathcal{C}(S)
is connected and simply connected. We now verify the hypotheses of Proposition
C.2
for the map
F
. By construction,
F
is surjective on vertices and edges.

Checking condition
(2)
:
Let
C
be a vertex of
\mathcal{C}(S)
, that is, a pants decomposition of
S
. The fibre
F^{-1}(C)
is the subcomplex of
\mathcal{M}(S)
whose vertices are markings with underlying pants decomposition
C
, and whose edges and cells are precisely those built from moves that leave
C
unchanged. In particular, its
1
-cells are the
T
- and
B
-moves, and its
2
-cells are the relations among these moves that do not alter the underlying pants decomposition, namely commutativity of disjoint moves, the boundary twist relations, the twist–braiding relation, and the Gluing relations for Dehn twists.

We next show that
F^{-1}(C)
is connected and simply connected. Let
S_{1},\dots,S_{k}
be the pair-of-pants components obtained as the closures of the connected components of
S\setminus C
. For each curve
c\in C
, choose a basepoint on
c
. These basepoints induce marked points on the corresponding boundary components of the
S_{i}
, so that each
S_{i}
becomes a parametrised, labelled surface of type
(0,3)
(Definition
2.27
). In particular, a marking on each
S_{i}
may be viewed as an embedded corolla meeting each boundary component at the chosen marked point.

Using these basepoints, we may glue markings on the
S_{i}
along the cut curves: if two boundary components of
S_{i}
and
S_{j}
arise from the same curve
c\in C
, then we identify them so that the marked points determined by the chosen basepoint of
c
agree. In this way we obtain a map

\mathcal{M}(S_{1})\times\cdots\times\mathcal{M}(S_{k})\longrightarrow F^{-1}(C),

sending a tuple of markings on the pair-of-pants components to the induced marking on
S
with underlying pants decomposition
C
. Since each
\mathcal{M}(S_{i})
is connected and simply connected by Lemma
C.3
, the product

(C.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}(S_{1})\times\cdots\times\mathcal{M}(S_{k})

is also connected and simply connected.

The gluing map is surjective on vertices: any marking on
S
with underlying pants decomposition
C
can be isotoped so that it intersects each curve
c\in C
at its chosen marked point. Such a marking then restricts, after cutting along
C
, to markings on the pair-of-pants components
S_{1},\dots,S_{k}
, and gluing these restrictions recovers the original marking. It is also surjective on
1
-cells, since every edge of
F^{-1}(C)
is a
T
- or
B
-move, hence is supported on a single pair-of-pants component, and therefore is obtained by applying the corresponding edge in one factor
\mathcal{M}(S_{i})
while leaving the other factors fixed.

The gluing map is not injective on vertices, because isotopies of the glued surface
S
are not required to preserve the chosen basepoints on the cut curves. In fact, the map (
C.1
) is the quotient of

\mathcal{M}(S_{1})\times\dots\times\mathcal{M}(S_{k})

by an action of
\mathbb{Z}[C]
, the free abelian group generated by the curves of the cut system
C
. For each
c\in C
, the corresponding generator acts on a tuple

(m_{1},\dots,m_{k})\in\mathcal{M}(S_{1})\times\cdots\times\mathcal{M}(S_{k})

as follows. If
c
bounds two distinct components
S_{i}
and
S_{j}
, then

c\cdot(m_{1},\dots,m_{k})=(m_{1},\dots,D_{c}m_{i},\dots,D_{c}^{-1}m_{j},\dots,m_{k}).

If
c
corresponds to two boundary components
c_{1},c_{2}
of a single component
S_{i}
, then

c\cdot(m_{1},\dots,m_{k})=(m_{1},\dots,D_{c_{1}}D_{c_{2}}^{-1}m_{i},\dots,m_{k}).

It is simple to check that this is a well-defined action on the vertices of
\mathcal{M}(S_{1})\times\cdots\times\mathcal{M}(S_{k})
which extends to a free and properly discontinuous action on the entire space. Then the quotient has fundamental group
\mathbb{Z}^{C}
generated by loops of the type

(\operatorname{id}_{S_{i}}\cup_{c}T_{c})\,(T_{c}^{-1}\cup_{c}\operatorname{id}_{S_{j}}),

and similarly in the nonseparating case. Since these loops are precisely the boundaries of the 2-cells (Gluing relations for Dehn twists) in
F^{-1}(C)
, we conclude that
F^{-1}(C)
is connected and simply connected.

It remains to verify conditions
(3)
and
(4)
of Proposition
C.2
.

Checking condition
(3)
:
this concerns comparing different lifts of edges in
\mathcal{C}(S)
. Recall that the edges of
\mathcal{C}(S)
are given by
A
- and
S
-moves. We begin with the former.

Let
C\overset{e}{\rightsquigarrow}C^{\prime}
be an
A
-move in
\mathcal{C}(S)
exchanging the curve
a\in C
with
a^{\prime}\in C^{\prime}
, and let
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
be two lifts of
e
. Then
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
must be edge-paths of the form
\gamma^{\prime}\circ A_{a,a^{\prime}}^{\pm 1}\circ\gamma
, where
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
are edge-paths in
F^{-1}(C)
and
F^{-1}(C^{\prime})
, respectively. Since Condition
(2)
has already been verified, we may assume that

e_{1}:(C,m_{1})\overset{}{\rightsquigarrow}(C^{\prime},m_{1}^{\prime})\quad\text{and}\quad e_{2}:(C,m_{2})\overset{}{\rightsquigarrow}(C^{\prime},m_{2}^{\prime})

are single edges corresponding to
A
-moves. By the
2
-cell (Self-duality of associativity), we may assume without loss of generality that these are
A
-moves, rather than their inverses.

For a fixed marking over the cut system
C
, there is a unique
A
-move exchanging
a\in C
. We now analyse how the markings
m_{1}
and
m_{2}
may differ.

By the definition of the
A
-move (Definition
2.38
), there exist diffeomorphisms
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}:S^{\prime}\to S_{\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/fork1.pdf}}}
such that
\phi_{1}(a)=\phi_{2}(a)=c
, and both
\phi_{1}\circ m_{1}
and
\phi_{2}\circ m_{2}
define the standard marking on
S_{\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/fork1.pdf}}}
. Moreover, we have
\alpha\phi_{1}(a^{\prime})=\alpha\phi_{2}(a^{\prime})=c^{\prime}
, and both
\alpha\circ\phi_{1}\circ m_{1}^{\prime}
and
\alpha\circ\phi_{2}\circ m_{2}^{\prime}
define the standard marking on
S_{\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/fork2.pdf}}}
. By Lemma
C.4
, we may further assume that
\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{2}
send the same boundary components of
S^{\prime}
to the components
\{1,2\}
of
S_{\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/fork1.pdf}}}
.

It follows that
\phi_{1}\circ\phi_{2}^{-1}
is a diffeomorphism of
S_{\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/fork1.pdf}}}
preserving each component of the standard pants decomposition. In particular, it is isotopic to a diffeomorphism fixing the curve
c
pointwise. Thus, we may regard
\phi_{1}\circ\phi_{2}^{-1}
as a pair of diffeomorphisms of the two pairs of pants in
S^{\prime}
, that fix the boundary components of the pants corresponding to
c
. From the description of the mapping class group of a pair of pants, it follows that
\phi_{1}\circ\phi_{2}^{-1}
is a composition of Dehn twists along
c
and the other boundary components, together with possible braiding morphisms that fix
c
.

On the other hand,

\alpha\phi_{1}(\alpha\phi_{2})^{-1}=\alpha(\phi_{1}\phi_{2}^{-1})\alpha^{-1}

is a diffeomorphism of
S_{\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/fork2.pdf}}}
fixing
c^{\prime}
. This implies that
\phi_{1}\circ\phi_{2}^{-1}
also fixes
\alpha^{-1}(c^{\prime})
. In particular, by considering intersection numbers, we see that
\phi_{1}\circ\phi_{2}^{-1}
cannot involve Dehn twists about
c
, since such twists would not preserve
\alpha^{-1}(c^{\prime})
.

It follows that
\phi_{1}\circ\phi_{2}^{-1}
is either a composition of Dehn twists along boundary components, or such a composition followed by the pair of braidings (one on each pair-of-pants component) represented on the right-hand side of Figure
18
. Hence we may assume that
m_{1}
and
m_{2}
differ from one of the two markings in Figure
18
only by Dehn twists along the boundary components.

Figure 18
.
Two standard markings on
S_{0,4}

If
m_{1}
and
m_{2}
are obtained from the same marking in Figure
18
by Dehn twists along the boundary components, then the required commutativity of the square follows from the
2
-cell (Boundary twist relations).

It remains to consider the case where
m_{1}
and
m_{2}
arise from different markings in Figure
18
. Without loss of generality, assume that
m_{1}
and
m_{2}
correspond to the left and right markings, respectively. Then Figure
19
gives the required commutative square.

Figure 19
.
Commutative diagram checking condition (3)

A very similar argument works for the lifts of the S-move, and can be found in
[
3
, Section 7.7]
. Here, the contractible square is obtained using the (Commutativity of disjoint moves) and the genus-one relation for
(g,n)=(1,1)
given by (
G1.1
).

Checking condition
(4)
:
now we must show that the boundary of each standard
2
-cell of
\mathcal{C}(S)
lifts to a contractible loop in
\mathcal{M}(S)
. In fact, even more, we have that each such boundary can be lifted to the boundary of a standard
2
-cell in
\mathcal{M}(S)
.

The correspondence is as follows:

•

The boundary of the
2
-cell
(3A)
can be lifted to the boundary of the
2
-cell corresponding to the hexagon relations; see Figure
17
;

•

The boundary of the
2
-cell
(5A)
can be lifted to the boundary of the
2
-cell corresponding to the pentagon relation; see Figure
16
;

•

The boundary of the
2
-cell
(3S)
can be lifted to the boundary of the
2
-cell corresponding to the Genus-one relation (
G1.2
); see Figure
7(b)
;

•

The boundary of the
2
-cell
(6AS)
can be lifted to the boundary of the
2
-cell corresponding to the Genus-one relation for
(g,n)=(1,2)
; see Figure
8
;

•

The boundary of the
2
-cell
(C)
can be lifted to the boundary of the
2
-cell corresponding to the relation (Commutativity of disjoint moves).

Thus the boundary of each
2
-cell of
\mathcal{C}(S)
lifts to a loop bounding a
2
-cell of
\mathcal{M}(S)
. Hence condition
(4)
holds. Proposition
C.2
now implies that
\mathcal{M}(S)
is connected and simply connected.
∎

Lemma C.4
.

Let
\phi:S^{\prime}\to S_{\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/fork1.pdf}}}
be a diffeomorphism witnessing an
A
-move, as in Definition
2.38
, and let
\{c,c^{\prime}\}
denote the boundary components of
S^{\prime}
that are mapped to the boundary components
\{1,2\}
of
S_{\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/fork1.pdf}}}
. Then there exists a diffeomorphism
\phi^{\prime}:S^{\prime}\to S_{\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/fork1.pdf}}}
witnessing the same
A
-move such that the boundary components
\{c,c^{\prime}\}
are mapped to
\{0,3\}
.

Proof.

Let
(C,m)\overset{A_{a,b}}{\rightsquigarrow}(C^{\prime},m^{\prime})
be an
A
-move on
\mathcal{M}(S)
and let
m|_{S^{\prime}}
and
m^{\prime}_{S^{\prime}}
denote representatives of the markings
m
and
m^{\prime}
restricted to
S^{\prime}
that, together with
\phi
, witnesses the
A
-move.

Let
z:S_{\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/fork1.pdf}}}\to S_{\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/fork1.pdf}}}
denote a difffeomorphism that fixes the standard marking and on the boundary components gives the permutation
(13)(20)
(this can be constructed using the standard marking and the diagrammatic representation of mapping classes).

Define the diffeomorphism

\phi^{\prime}\coloneqq z_{S}\circ\phi.

By construction,
\phi^{\prime}\circ m|_{S^{\prime}}
is still the standard marking on
S_{\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/fork1.pdf}}}
, and
\alpha\circ\phi^{\prime}\circ m^{\prime}_{S^{\prime}}
is still the standard marking on
S_{\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/fork2.pdf}}}
. Hence
\phi^{\prime}
also witnesses this
A
-move and, by construction, takes the boundary components
\{c,c^{\prime}\}
to the boundaries
\{0,3\}
on
S_{\raisebox{-4.0pt}{\includegraphics[height]{Figures/fork1.pdf}}}
.
∎
